president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking mr stockmann for the excellent report and the good cooperation. i think that at the end of the legislative process we will have taken a major new step towards ensuring that the main european airports finally comply with the same rules of the game when it comes to charging all kinds of fees for services relating to landings, take-offs, airport lighting, aircraft parking and also the handling of passengers and goods. the scope of the directive was one of the major obstacles. i think the compromise is excellent: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the free movement of goods are respected. it should also be borne in mind that our compromise provides for the possibility for member states to oblige other airports within the same member state to comply with these regulations, if necessary after an investigation. i think there are four elements that are important. firstly, transparency is the common thread throughout the legislation, the imposition of relevant objective and transparent criteria for determining the level of airport charges - that is essential. secondly, there is still a possibility of variation in the level of airport charges, and i am thinking, for example, of variation based on environmental performance, based on noise pollution. this is the way to achieve a policy of control. thirdly, a clear and mandatory procedure for consultation between the airport managing body on the one hand and the users on the other hand has been established, and a proper complaints procedure has also been set up. finally, there are clear agreements on the prefinancing of major investments in airports and how these can be charged. in short, i think we can get a good result if the council is of good will. i realize there are still some bottlenecks here and there. i am also thinking of the amendments adopted on the financing of security measures, which are intended primarily as a means of pressure to resolve another matter, as you know. that is now out of the way, and i therefore think that we as a parliament should be more flexible in this respect in the course of the procedure.

in writing. − the transport sector accounts for one fifth of greenhouse gas emissions. sustainable, efficient and clean cars can make a serious contribution to the fight against climate change. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. unfortunately, this message is not to be found in the commission and parliament's response to the cars 21 document. our amendments seek to reflect a number of key points: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules. we stress that the increasing global demand for transport fuels may lead to the exploitation of oil resources with significant negative environmental impacts such as tar sands or synthetic coal gas. further measures are needed to significantly improve fuel efficiency in the sector. the choice of agrofuels can only be made if the commission provides for a binding, comprehensive certification system with strong social and environmental criteria. finally, we also want to remove the paragraphs that (1) propose motorsport and formula 1 racing as possible contributions to more environmentally friendly technologies and (2) allow car manufacturers to produce cars that emit more co2 if these emissions are the result of legally binding measures taken at eu level. the bulk of our amendments were rejected. i therefore vote against this report.
in writing. − health and safety at work is at the heart of the european agenda and the lisbon strategy. the more investment is made in this, the higher the productivity and the lower the social security expenditure. for example, the previous policy plan already resulted in a significant reduction in the number of occupational accidents in the workplace. for the new policy period (until 2012), the 25% reduction target is again set and the commission commits to support smes in implementing the existing regulatory framework. the willmott report improves the commission proposal considerably. it seeks the right mix of stricter and better labour inspection, effective prevention, adequate incentives and sanctions, together with the exchange of best practices and greater employee participation. the report is supported by a number of green amendments, such as the additional attention to the occupational group of temporary workers (women, migrants), temporary and low-skilled workers and the attention to the underlying causes of mental illness and mental health, to addiction and psychological dangers at work, such as stress, harassment and bullying, and also violence.

in writing. after five years of discussions, european consumers will soon have identical information on consumer credit, allowing them to compare foreign offers for cars, dishwashers and sofa beds. although europeans already have the opportunity to take out bank loans abroad to purchase consumer goods, very few take advantage of this: less than 1% of the total volume of this type of credit is currently taken out abroad. however, rates can now vary from one to two (portugal 12%, finland 6%)! the main obstacles identified were the language problem, distance and lack of consumer confidence. the directive, which i support, should help to strengthen this confidence, to better inform consumers and to facilitate their choices, while ensuring that they benefit from uniform rules of protection (early reimbursement, right of withdrawal, etc.). two questions to conclude: is there not a risk of encouraging over-indebtedness if the conditions for credit institutions to check the creditworthiness of customers are not further strengthened? is it not urgent to open up the field of crossborder competition for mortgage loans?
in writing. it is a highly controversial directive, "on ice" for almost 6 years, that the european parliament voted today. however, this is a text which is very much in line with the concerns of europeans, since it provides for the harmonization of the consumer credit market. a market of €800 billion (two out of three europeans use credit to buy furniture, a television or a car), rates which currently range from 6% (in finland) to over 12% (in portugal) and yet transactions hitherto mainly national, less than 1% of credit now being crossborder. the directive opens up the european frontiers to consumers looking for the best credit: they can choose the best offer, while being guaranteed the same rights and standards in terms of information, comparison and, above all, protection against over-indebtedness. the assessment of the borrower's creditworthiness, prompt and free information in the event of a refusal and a right of withdrawal of 14 days in principle are some of the main objectives of the directive. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented.

president. - the assassination of benazir bhutto is a tragedy for pakistan and shows the enormity of the problems facing pakistan. it is a country of 165 million people, a country with nuclear weapons and a country engaged in a struggle against terrorist organizations such as al-qaeda and the taliban. it is of course of the utmost importance that political stability should be established in pakistan as soon as possible and that president musharraf should play an important role in this at this time. yet we must not be blind to the many important shortcomings in musharraf's policies. for example, the problem of terrorism has been treated far too laxly. for example, in large parts of the border area with afghanistan, the taliban and al-qaeda have free rein, and far too little is done about it. musharraf has also shown that he is not so concerned with democracy by declaring a state of emergency on 3 november. moreover, there were already alarming signs pointing in his direction after the first attack on benazir bhutto on 18 october. the same thing happened after the actual assassination of benazir bhutto. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, commissioner, i understand very well the concern of many people, including yourself, including bernard kouchner, to send an electoral mission to pakistan. it is indeed one of the only means of exerting pressure on mr musharraf that we still have, but it must be said that this observation mission would take place under very special conditions. we are used to studying and making the observations before, during and after. for what happened before, we already know how much stuff was faked. mrs benazir bhutto had written, with mr latif khosa, a 160-page report on the possible rigging of these elections, in particular by computer, which she was to hand over to two us parliamentarians on the very night of her death. do we have the ability today to control computer tampering in an election observation mission? if not, we could then rule that even if the "before" was more than doubtful, the "during" happened normally while there was fraud. i therefore call for a very special observation mission and i ask that we do not declare these elections democratic when we cannot control them.

president of the commission. − madam president, first of all i apologise for the slight delay. as you know, we have just concluded our commission meeting, and the first thing we did immediately after it was to come here to present what we believe is an historic package for the european union. that is the reason why i, together with the commissioner for the environment, mr dimas, and the commissioner for energy, mr piebalgs, have only just arrived. the final drafting of the document is being completed. we will have it immediately, but this is a very important occasion to present to you first-hand the conclusions of our meeting today. i should like to start by saying that we are very happy with the result. the result was obtained by consensus in the college, with strong backing from all the members of the commission for a very ambitious proposal, because we have agreed what is now the most comprehensive package in terms of climate action and renewable energy that exists in any part of the world. we believe we have reason to be proud of it. this package on climate action and renewable energy meets the challenges of the future. we believe it is good for our planet; it is good for the european economy; it is good for our citizens. the work of the european union is sometimes seen as rather technical: interesting to specialists, but not relevant to people’s daily lives. the action we are discussing today proves this theory wrong: the struggle against climate change and the quest for secure, sustainable and competitive energy touches every european every day. we are all affected by this, and this is one of the most – if not the most – important challenge of the 21st century. europeans ask for a vision and a plan of action. that is what we are doing now. this vision was set out last year with leadership from the european political community. it was agreed in the european council, following a commission proposal, to bring about the ‘20/20/20 by 2020’ goals: a 20% reduction in greenhouse gases, or 30% if other developed economies agree, 20% of energy use through renewables and a 20% increase in energy efficiency by 2020. this was agreed by the european council. we can be proud that europe is leading that effort. we then brought it to the heiligendamm summit, to the high-level event at the united nations in new york and finally to the bali conference. without strong european leadership, we could not have achieved the promising results of the bali conference, setting a road map for the global agreement we are seeking and that we expect to reach in copenhagen in 2009. the clear commitment of this parliament to this cause has been essential, and i want to thank you, once again, for all your support. your work over the past year has been critical in building the political momentum in favour of action. i believe that today’s proposals match the goals that you have set out, as well as meeting the mandate given by the european council last march. today’s package adds up to a detailed road map to bring about the political vision agreed last year. we have agreed on the vision and now we have to deliver on the concrete plans, the concrete instruments to achieve that vision. we believe that the main priority is to bring about a 20% cut in our greenhouse gas emissions by 2020 and be ready, if necessary, to step up to 30% with an international agreement. let us never forget that. this is about global warming; it is about global climate change and not just about climate change in europe. we have to put our proposals in such a way that we bring others along with us. it is also important to lead by example and that is why, for instance, we are now proposing how to achieve the 20% of energy use through renewables by 2020. the package we have just approved in the commission includes the following: an updated emissions trading system (ets) to create a borderless ets to drive cuts in greenhouse gas emissions from big industrial emitters; specific, binding national targets, so that member states know exactly what they have to do outside the ets, in sectors like transport, buildings, agriculture and waste; a new approach to actively promote renewable targets, again including binding national targets; new rules to stimulate carbon capture and storage, tomorrow’s technology to cut emissions; and new state aid rules that take into consideration the specificity of the action needed in this environmental field. part of our mandate was the 10% target for biofuels so that transport plays a part in emissions cuts. as you know, this 10% target was unanimously agreed by the european council. i want to be clear that, in putting forward proposals on biofuels, we have also fully respected the other side of the mandate: the need for environmental sustainability. the proposal creates the most comprehensive and sustainable system anywhere in the world for the certification of biofuels and for domestic and imported biofuels alike. we will also continue to promote the rapid development of second-generation biofuels. it is important to understand that what we are promoting is sustainable biofuels and we are making a contribution to a global regime, because what we have today is a situation where, in many cases, biofuels are not sustainable and do not respond to our criteria – criteria we want to be implemented not only in europe but throughout the world. the package of measures proposed today contains the most far-reaching legislative proposals to be made by the european commission for many years. how did we set about shaping this complex package? we spent a lot of time exploring options in great detail, but we always held firm to five key principles. first, respecting the targets. without this, we will not look serious to investors, to our negotiating partners and, most importantly, to our own citizens. second, fairness: recognising member states’ different capacities to invest and their different starting points. third, competitiveness: designing a system able to minimise the costs to the european economy and, in some areas, improving the competitive possibilities of europe by giving european industry and the european economy a first-mover advantage. fourth, the proposals had to be designed to promote a comprehensive international agreement to cut greenhouse emissions, including stepping up our own effort to 30% emission cuts should other developed countries do the same. last but not least, we must also start work now to halve global emissions by 2050. this means working today to bring tomorrow’s technology rapidly on stream. this package should also be seen in articulation with some of our earlier proposals, namely the internal market for energy and the energy technology plan. this is part of a very comprehensive set of proposals that is creating, for the first time, a real european energy policy, but we want this energy to be secure and also sustainable. of course, there will be those who say that change comes at too high a cost and that we have no choice but to bury our heads in the sand and hope for the best. i think they have got this wrong. there is a cost, but the cost is manageable. we have worked very hard on coming up with the right design to meet europe’s ambitions in the right way. so the additional effort needed to realise the proposals would be less than 0.5% of gdp by 2020. this amounts, on average, to about eur 3 a week for each european citizen. this is much less than the up to eur 60 per week that is the cost of inaction. going on even the most optimistic assumptions of the stern report, the cost of inaction is more than 10 times what we are now proposing. it is true there are some costs, but we have to compare the cost of this package with the cost of inaction and, since the cost of inaction is much higher, we can say that there is a relative gain in having this package. in fact, every day the price of oil and gas goes up, the real cost of the package falls. instead of costs, we really should be talking about gains for the european union. (applause) the package is hard-wired to reach our goals in an efficient way, using the market to drive change where it is most cost-effective and ensuring a level playing field while leaving as much as possible to member states we have paid particular attention to fairness. we have, therefore, designed the proposals to ensure that the demands on the poorer member states are realistic: all will contribute, but in line with their capacity to invest. at the level of business, we all know that there are sectors where the cost of cutting emissions could have a real impact on competitiveness with companies in countries that do nothing – or do very little – in the fight against climate change. there is no point in europe being tough if it just means production shifting to countries allowing a free-for-all on emissions. an international agreement is the best way to tackle this, but we also need to give legal certainty to companies that we will take the action needed. energy-intensive industries will have ets allowances free of charge if there is no global or sectoral agreement. if our expectations for an international agreement are not met, we will look at other options, such as requiring importers to obtain allowances alongside european competitors, as long as such a system is compatible with wto requirements. i am making this point specifically, because it is very important that this package and the measures that the european union will adopt are not in favour of the environment and against the economy. no, they are not only for the environment and our planet but also for our economy and for the competitiveness of the european economy. we want industry to remain in europe. we do not want to export our jobs to other parts of the world. (applause) fairness is also important at the level of the citizen. we are encouraging member states to act in a sensitive way, such as using a share of the billions of euros to be gained in auctioning revenues to help the less well off to invest in energy-efficient homes. but we must not forget the huge economic opportunity represented by europe’s transition to a low-emissions economy. europe’s leadership also means showing that the technology is there, that we will need an effective and competitive industrial sector up to the challenge. there are real opportunities there. the renewables sector alone will bring one million jobs by 2020 at least, according to our estimates. i am sure that, once again, european industry will show its ability to innovate and adapt. europe can be the first economy for the low-carbon age. we must seize this chance. this package represents an opportunity for europe to show itself at its best: tackling an issue of fundamental long-term importance; using the european union’s continental scale to best effect; turning political consensus into practical action. if some people in europe have doubts about the need for a european union, then these are exactly the kinds of policy that show why we need a strong european union more than ever. (applause)this is precisely where we can demonstrate to the most sceptical that, alone, our member states – even the biggest member states – do not have the scale or the leverage to push the agenda forward. but europe, if it is determined to lead, can do it. so, apart from the environmental aspects, apart from the economic aspects and apart from the very important geopolitical and security aspects – because let us not forget that we are speaking about security of supply, and we do not want to be dependent on regimes that are not our friends – it is also a great argument for european unity. it is also a great argument to show not only that we need a strong european union, but also that the world needs a strong european union to lead the global efforts to face the global challenges of the 21st century. (applause)
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe-de group. president, commission president, president of the council, ladies and gentlemen, today is a historic day. it is a day that will affect the way we live, think, act and behave. without prejudice to its other merits, this commission will certainly be remembered in the future as the commission which gave a new dimension to european integration, as the commission which drew up the climate and energy master plan. the basis for this plan was laid during the german presidency of the council, at the instigation of chancellor merkel, and we in the ppe-de group have supported it from the outset. europe is showing today that it is not afraid to take its responsibility in the field of energy and climate and that it can play a leading role in the world. now it is a matter of reconciling ambition with feasibility. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. just carrying out all these agreements is a big step forward. we are assuming, mr president of the commission, that the action plan you have just outlined is well thought out. the ppe group supports your five key principles. but as the group responsible, we naturally demand the opportunity to examine for ourselves whether each of the proposals put forward by the commission is fair and feasible for each of the member states and for each of the sectors most concerned. success does require an integrated approach, reconciling our ambitious energy and climate goals with our economic growth and jobs goals. at the same time, we as europe must secure our energy supply and self-sufficiency. as the ppe-de group we agree with the differentiated approach. we already know that we will be bombarded with questions to weaken the proposals. we also have a duty to listen carefully to the comments of national and regional authorities and representative civil society. but our basic principle is that the one who shouts the loudest should not get the most. we must work towards a differentiated approach based on transparent, objective and fair criteria, and this in the knowledge that not acting costs more than acting. in addition to mutual solidarity, the geographical and economic capacity of the member states must be a priority in burden-sharing. the efforts already made and the future innovation capacity of sectors should also be taken into account. we will not under any circumstances compromise these principles. the final decision we as the ppe-de group will take must be a win-win situation. on the one hand, we need to meet the climate targets, on the other hand, we need to have a performing economy that remains capable of creating jobs. if the balance goes sideways, we risk losing both, for example because economic sectors move to places where there are not even carbon caps. finally, the ppe group is aware that it is crucial that europe, and the rest of the world, should succeed in breaking free from its energy addiction. the time has come to learn to consume less carbon on a large scale. we all - businessmen and citizens - will have to turn the switch.
it is a paradox that a country with such a rich history, such a diverse culture and such a dynamic population as iran, as javier solana has said, is nevertheless committing the most atrocious acts in the field of human rights. the number of death sentences handed down last year - and the commissioner has also mentioned this - is a tragic illustration of this. i therefore believe - as does my group - that iran is in fact failing its own history when the current regime treats its people as it has unfortunately done for many years. nevertheless, i think that the solution is indeed to continue the dialogue, certainly at the level of the population and civil society, but also at the level of the political authorities, however difficult that may be. in this context, the debate with mr jalili last week was not exactly a happy exercise, but it was very important. i am sure he will have understood that none of the groups in this parliament, and therefore none, could agree or even consider that iran should again embark on the path of militarizing its nuclear research without any reaction. on behalf of my group, i would like to welcome very particularly the suggestion by javier solana to set up a kind of multilateral and multinational bank for nuclear fuel, because that is probably a way of linking our concern for non-proliferation with the possibility for countries to continue to develop nuclear activities in the civil sphere.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, security and defence policy, on the situation in iran. iran supports islamist and terrorist groups in lebanon, the palestinian territories, afghanistan, iraq, and so on. the european union's strategy should be to isolate iran as much as possible in the region. it is also important that russia be encouraged to reduce cooperation with iran in the nuclear field. iran's nuclear plans are a threat to world peace. the us national intelligence estimate surprised the world a few months ago by claiming that iran would have stopped its attempts to produce nuclear weapons by 2003. in the united states, however, votes are now being taken to further examine the findings of the nie. in any event, it would be wrong to assume that the nuclear threat posed by the iranian regime is suddenly no longer a matter of concern. the question remains, for example, why iran is making it so difficult for the international atomic energy agency to monitor it. we all know what the regime in tehran is capable of. there is a human rights situation. iran's involvement in international islamic terrorism has been clearly demonstrated in the past. when president ahmadinejad openly says that his ambition is to wipe israel off the map, then it is our duty to take such a thing seriously. so in the present circumstances it would be wrong to reduce the pressure on ahmadinejad. dialogue is necessary, but the european union must be resolute in its efforts, its ambition to further promote freedom and stability.
on behalf of the pse group. mr president, all walls eventually come down: the wall of jericho, the wall of the warsaw ghetto, the berlin wall, the atlantic wall, or the wall of indifference. rafah wall, by its symbolic weight, is part of this blind push of men towards freedom. but what did the gazans do with this newfound freedom? did they flee to egypt? did they go to the army with kalashnikovs? no, because the weapons, unfortunately, with or without a wall, always arrive at their destination. the gazans went to stock up on first-aid supplies. they went to do their shopping, to get medicine and baby milk, which was not available in gaza. motorcycles, goats and cows, lifted by cranes, flew into rafah to the cheers of the crowd. this is surreal! then each went to his own home. everything is in these images: the impossible of yesterday, suddenly within reach, and the prosaic life, which takes its rightful place. our responsibility today is historic. the question is no longer who will open the doors of an open-air prison, but who will dare to close them, who will dare to send the gazans back to their slow asphyxiation. since annapolis began, the european union has been losing control. it was the basis of the road map, and it had given control of the peace process to the united states. getting the co-chairmanship of the paris donors' conference was a real feat. that's good. but, for access to gaza, since 2005, the europeans have a mandate. will we, together with the egyptians, the palestinian authority, hamas and israel, succeed in re-establishing dialogue and managing palestinian access to the outside world, or will we, on the contrary, stand by as observers to the inevitable repression that is bound to ensue? president, high representative, commissioner, i would very much like to join with the colleagues who have said that the time for merely showing concern about the dramatic events and the humanitarian situation in gaza is over, and that merely being a donor is not enough. on the contrary, i think that the situation in gaza and the blockade cannot be separated from the overall conflict between israel and palestine. it is clear that the palestinians have placed their hopes in the eu to finally achieve results. we have made many promises in the past, but we find that the european union, in the follow-up to the implementation of the negotiations at the annapolis conference, as well as at the united nations, has actually failed to take on an active mediating role. i think this is unacceptable and that the eu can no longer accept being sidelined by the united states, but must adopt an independent stance.
i support the compromise reached by mr markus ferber and accepted by the council, because i am in favour of an open postal market. i think that this directive gives member states enough room to ensure that their market is also opened up in an orderly way, that there are different service providers competing with a guarantee of quality for their customers. it is certainly not the intention that the member states should use this space to ensure that their market does not open up. because there's a risk of that, too. it is easy to apply this directive in such a way that the bar for new companies is set so high that no one from a new company will be involved in the mail. if that is the way member states are applying the directive, then we have adopted a fine text, but we have not changed anything in practice for postal customers. i think we should certainly approve the text, but i would ask the commission to ensure that the objective of creating an open postal market is not circumvented by measures taken by member states. i note that, for example, in the rapporteur's country, measures have recently been taken which have effectively closed the postal market in germany again.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in the past, and i am sure that the commission will make a number of proposals in the future. i understand that many people want to leave it at that, but behind the positive elements that have been added to the directive there are also a number of dangers. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. firstly, there are still many unanswered questions about the financing of universal service. member states have a number of options, but it is not always clear whether they work. we shall see that in many cases this will lead to all kinds of legal disputes. i think it is therefore useful to clarify a number of points. firstly, the member states are obliged to guarantee the universal service and its financing in all circumstances. secondly, that the member states should be obliged to do their homework in good time and thoroughly and to prepare for this new fact. the second major theme is, of course, the social theme. in the social field, it is important to note that this directive allows member states to impose on all postal operators, through a licensing system, the obligation to comply with, for example, the same collective agreements or other minimum standards. that is a positive thing, but it remains optional and its application will vary from country to country. in short, i think that the directive can be tightened up, and that has nothing to do with fear of the cold, but with the fact that a free market must be regulated and that its liberalisation must be prepared in any case properly.
the committee on regional policy and regional planning has been asked to make a statement on the proposal for a regulation on the european regional development fund. i am very pleased, commissioner, that you have referred to the eurodistrict project on the border of france and belgium. mr. beaupuy has already referred to it. the cities of kortrijk, doornik and lille - have just this week signed a cooperation agreement with the intention of actually working together on projects that people are working on. and then i'm talking about border work, public transportation, hospital cooperation, crime prevention. these are the things that people expect us to do in practice. in another border region - on the border between belgium and the netherlands - several concrete initiatives have been taken to establish very concrete cooperation in the field of university cooperation through an egtc. the regions of belgium, limburg and the netherlands, as well as the aachen region, are concerned. the aim is to create a single, crossborder university with the help of the egtc. the aim is to overcome, as it were, a great many of the administrative obstacles which still exist today by means of this instrument. commissioner, president, the egtc does not solve all the problems, of course, and i am well aware - as we have just heard - that many member states have yet to start. however, on the basis of the first experiences with this instrument, we should already be looking further ahead and, as it were, looking for some improvements on the basis of the first preliminary evaluation. and we should be addressing certain existing gaps. i am referring, for example, to the different social statuses faced by workers who work together in the framework of such an egtc. i can only say, commissioner, that it is of the utmost importance that you, as commissioner, go after the governments which have so far failed to make this an operational instrument.

the rapporteur. - mr president, the report which i have the honour of presenting to you today, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, has its origins in a proposal tabled at the end of 2005 by our colleague antoine duquesne, whom i would like to welcome here. i should also like to point out that most of the work on this report was done by our colleague mayor oreja, who decided, for reasons which are not for me to comment on, to withdraw his name after the vote in our committee. as regards the content of the report submitted to this house, i think it can be summed up by articulating the many elements it contains around four main lines. the first axis is the severity and permanence of the threat. it is an undeniable fact that terrorism in general, and jihadist terrorism in particular, is one of the most serious threats to the security of citizens in the european union today. the bloody attacks in madrid in 2004 and london in 2005, as well as the many failed attempts since then, are clear evidence of the continuing and serious nature of the threat. it follows, and the report strongly emphasises this, that the fight against terrorism must remain a priority for the european union and all its member states. but, and this is the second point of the report, this determined struggle cannot be waged in any way. there are fundamental principles to be respected, otherwise we will be distorting the fundamental values which govern our societies. the fight against terrorism must be conducted with full and strict respect for fundamental freedoms and rights, including freedom of religion and the right to a fair trial. at the same time, and specifically in the case of jihadist terrorism, we must refrain from equating any culture or religion as such with terrorism. the overwhelming majority of muslims, who are, let us not forget, the first victims of terrorism in iraq, afghanistan and pakistan, have nothing to do with the extremist groups that must be fought and which distort the religion they claim to be. finally, on the basis of the principle that hatred and frustration are the factors that drive radicalisation, the report reminds the member states that the fight against all forms of discrimination, and in particular measures to promote the integration of all minority groups, are priority policies to be implemented to promote appeasement, tolerance and coexistence in our societies. the third pillar is specific prevention measures. in the long list of measures recommended to the member states, the report stresses in particular the need to strengthen surveillance of propaganda sites, particularly via the internet, preaching places and audiovisual media in general. the fourth pillar concerns repressive measures. one of these is clearly worth highlighting and will be the subject of considerable debate. it is contained in paragraph 10 of the report and provides for the extension of the definition of terrorist acts to the justification of terrorism. although i am not sure that the term "justification" is adequate personally i think that in french it would have been better to use the term "apology" or "apology" of terrorism and if it would be desirable to change it, i am waiting with interest for how our society will decide tomorrow on this delicate point, which is at the heart of attention, between security and respect for fundamental rights and freedom of expression.
the rapporteur. - mr president, at the end of this debate i would like to make three points. the first is that if we listen to the commissioner and to most of the speakers, we are struck by the fact that there is - as regards the analysis of the phenomenon, the seriousness of the threat, the balance to be struck between preventive and repressive measures - a huge agreement between us all from the moment we discuss operational matters to be put in place: what to analyse, what to prevent, what to repress. secondly, unfortunately, i feel that on a number of rather symbolic items we may not reach the necessary consensus tomorrow and i would regret it because, after analysing the list of amendments, it seems to me that there are more symbolic conflicts than substantive conflicts. the trouble is that these symbolic conflicts could be alleviated if everyone decided to make a gesture, which is not always the major feature of the political groups in this house. thirdly, there are still some things i would like to say to a few people, in particular - and alexander alvaro wanted to say this too - to our irish friend, who believes that respect for human rights is not necessary in the fight against terrorism. i think that's a danger, i would say a very serious danger to the values of our societies. there is a head of state, i heard him speak on television once, a european head of state who said that with a knife in his hand, he would go and kill the terrorists right down to the toilet. well, this regime, now, is a regime in which they poison the opposition and let them kill journalists and they don't know if the elections are free or if they are not rigged. to play with human rights in the fight against terrorism is to risk an unacceptable drift of democratic societies. on the other hand, we were directly challenged by sarah ludford on the problem of the apology of terrorism. i cannot answer her on the substance, i simply say that i am the rapporteur on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, in the circumstances she is aware of and that when i made my comments earlier i was basing myself on paragraph 10 of the report which was voted by the committee on civil liberties, and which very expressly says, sarah, that the committee, and therefore parliament, 'calls for an open debate on an amendment to the framework decision to extend its scope to the justification of terrorism', etc. so i did not make a plea, i simply stated what came out of the committee on civil liberties, but i will still give him a personal answer. when i speak of apologising for terrorism, apologising is incitement and i think incitement is punishable by criminal law.
the president, commissioner, minister vizjak, colleagues, i would like to thank everyone for the good cooperation, but especially alexander stubb, who as rapporteur, as shadow rapporteurs for his report, has very openly involved us in all the discussions. thank you for that. the vote on the goods package is a serious step forward in the achievement of the single european goods market and the three proposals as a whole are necessary instruments for the proper functioning of a single goods market. the principle of mutual recognition exists, but it exists mainly on paper. too often the principle is ignored. the single market is not yet working as jacques delors envisioned it a quarter of a century ago. the european commission has calculated that this means that companies have to pay between 2 and 10% more to market their goods in another member state, due to the incorrect application of the principle of mutual recognition. the european union is not a mere institution, but a political entity. this is bad for both companies and their employees, but also for consumers who have to pay higher prices. it was therefore high time to take the principle of mutual recognition out of anonymity and apply it correctly. that is why we have a rigorous procedure with a reversal of the burden of proof, whereby the commission must also be informed of the member state's decision at the end of the procedure. i hope that the commission will also take action at the end of the procedure if there are still rules which are not based on objective grounds. a few things for the future: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. our committee on the internal market visited the port of antwerp and we saw there how the customs services on the one hand are asking for more resources - and that is a matter for the national member states - but also are asking for more cross-border work and we must work on that for the future. secondly, the clarity of the ce marking is a step forward, but i support the request by mr mccarthy and mr gebhardt to work towards a real safety label for the future. i welcome the positive answer you have given to that, commissioner verheugen. mutual recognition must now be able to function better and for this everyone on the ground will have to take responsibility, the member states and also the commission. but it's still the second best solution. we therefore expect further legislative initiatives to harmonize the internal market in goods in the future.
madam president, i would like to join mr bloom in the praise that has gone before me to our three co-rapporteurs for their investment of time, perseverance and the achievement of this fair compromise, which is a boon both for european industry and consumer safety. i have only two minutes and i would like to focus on a few points, one of which is the issue of ce marking. seven months after the mattel case and the withdrawal since then of over 20 million ce-labelled toys made in china, the eu was under more pressure than ever to improve the marking system. in recent years it has advocated a flexible, voluntary approach based on the new approach, a method which has advantages, but which, when used alone, sometimes shows certain limitations when certain manufacturers affix the ce marking without complying with the standards, which is a clear risk in terms of consumer safety. this is why this legislative package is essential to give the authorities concerned with this problem - the accreditation and certification bodies and the customs services - the tools to fight against these ce marking infringers. it is no coincidence that the european parliament has already expressed itself on several occasions in favour of increased market surveillance, most recently in this resolution on toy safety of september 2007. our rapporteur, mr brie, has made this point abundantly. in january 2004, when i was reporting on the ecodesign directive for energy-consuming products, peter liese, karin scheele and claude turmes and i had already denounced the apparent lack of safety in many products and the unreliability of mandatory or voluntary labels in some cases. i see that, four years later, we have not made much progress and i regret this, i must say, on behalf of the many professionals who have approached us. of course, all measures that are aimed at greater legal certainty and additional support for industry and smes are welcome. i am thinking of the product contact points being created at local level, announced in this report, in addition to the very personal info point announced this morning by alexander stubb. i do not know whether it will be stubb/smes.com, but i wish it good luck, in any case, for its sme access point and i must say that i share the caution of my colleagues expressed in the oral question about a complementary marking and its compatibility with the existing label. i agree with mrs mccarthy in her allegory of the kettle this morning, and i thank the commissioner for making a great deal of progress on this issue. i would like to conclude, madam president, by recalling that the rules of the game we are setting up here are only valid if they are respected by european producers and distributors, of course, but also, and this is essential, by the actors - as mrs de vits has just said - coming from outside, first and foremost the importers.
mr president, i voted for the musotto report because it mentions many things in the name of fraud and the fight against fraud. i also have a feeling of déjà vu when the report mentions, for example, the millions of dollars wrongly paid and ineligible expenditure. for example, the hundreds of millions of euros of european money that have already flowed into the bottomless wells of the corrupt walloon ps state. a state that has had numerous directors in trouble, including money laundering. when the report mentions the lack of supervision by the commission, i am reminded of the words of my colleague from wallonia, mr deprez, who spoke earlier this month about - and i quote - 'bad projects for which not only wallonia but also the commission is responsible because it approves these projects'.

in writing. artificial electromagnetic waves have significant health consequences for european citizens, first and foremost for workers, who must be protected from electromagnetic fields as they already are by two european directives setting exposure limit values for noise and vibration. no, electromagnetic fields, emitted by both radios and electronic devices and by various telephone devices - antennas and mobile phones, fixed-base mobile phones and wi-fi - are not without danger! this is what the recent bioinitiative report reveals with its undeniable conclusions: the risk of cancer, alzheimer's disease, nervous problems caused by chronic and/or excessive exposure to electromagnetic waves. a health threat recently relayed by the european environment agency. that is why i voted for the report by mr andersson, which aims to protect the health of workers while taking into account the concerns expressed by the medical community for compatibility of community law with magnetic resonance imaging (mri) techniques. a vital medical practice for some patients, which i think should have been exempted rather than postponing the whole directive by four years!
mr president, mr prime minister, it is a little strange that a debate is being held here on the future of europe without the question of the frontiers of the european union being raised. it is, however, one of the most fundamental problems we face. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the question is whether the european union will remain a european project. this will no longer be the case if turkey joins. if the european union continues to be stubborn and ignore the will of the people, its democratic support will slowly but surely disappear. mr. prime minister, you warned of a wall that might be built against turkey. but it has nothing to do with that. nobody in europe is waiting for nice talks. the copenhagen criteria must be met, including by turkey, and that is clearly not the case. it is therefore time to put an end to these negotiations. that is nothing more or less than the respect of the agreements made.
the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but you must not conclude from this that we have become less concerned about the spring summit and the lisbon strategy. on the contrary, we have simply had the gallantry to give our morning for once to mr corbett and mr méndez de vigo for the discussion of their extraordinary report on the reform treaty, which together with the lisbon strategy is decisive for the future of europe. last week in brussels we discussed the state of play of the strategy with the national parliamentarians and we quickly agreed on one thing: the new approach, which focuses on growth and jobs without forgetting the sustainability dimension, is bearing fruit. the strategy promotes the growth potential of the european economy and creates a climate for job creation: 6.5 million already, 5 million more to come. the commission is proposing to continue on the path we have taken, because we are not there yet. in all member states and at all levels, much improvement is possible and necessary. we have much to learn from each other and we must also make better use of regional opportunities. we will therefore wholeheartedly support the joint resolution on the lisbon strategy. the priorities of our group are clear. investing more in research and innovation and ensuring that intellectual property is well protected are crucial to developing the economy and creating quality jobs in the future. a better business climate is equally essential. the completion of the internal market, better regulation and less red tape are important for all businesses, but even more so for those 23 million smes. that's why the small business act needs to come through and become more than a symbol. we therefore welcome the fact that we have been able to complete the legislative package on the internal market for products this week, but we are not so happy that new labelling rules for food have been proposed which will make the administrative burden unbearable. thirdly, we need to reform the labour market, flexibility and job security must go hand in hand, and we need to invest more in people's skills. finally, we support the 20-20-20 objectives, but in such a way that they create jobs here, not that they export jobs to other continents. finally, the spring summit rightly draws attention to the looming crisis in the financial and insurance markets. we must be absolutely vigilant that this crisis does not undermine our efforts to create growth and jobs. if we keep that in mind, if we stay on course, we can restore people's trust. confidence is stability, and that will enable us to give europe a good future. and that's what we're here to do.
madam president, everyone knows that europe must become even more of a knowledge-based economy, better able to cope with global competition. we should not be entering too many open doors here, we all agree. what we do not agree on, for example, is that the commission sees new mass economic migration as one of the main means of achieving the lisbon objectives. this is absurd. we have enough talent in europe. we have a few tens of millions of unemployed in europe in particular. that is the great challenge for government and for business. it is also true that the european citizen does not want to know about a new wave of immigration. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the new member states are able to apply the provisions of the treaty in their national legislation. but yes, the commission misses the mark more than once. i remember that the european commission a few months ago considered that the political crisis in belgium would slow down the pace of the reforms necessary to achieve the lisbon objectives. the reality is the opposite. the reality is that the belgian political crisis has shown the bankruptcy of the belgian state and that the very existence of belgium prevents flanders from modernising its labour law and its labour market, reducing its social burden, simplifying and reducing its taxes, precisely in order to achieve the lisbon objectives. the european commission is of course not happy to admit that belgium is holding back both flanders and wallonia.
president, mr president-in-office, commissioner, i am frankly disappointed by the lack of social ambition for the spring summit you have shown today. my group continues to believe that there are good reasons to update the lisbon strategy and the integrated guidelines on a number of key points. i will mention three of them in relation to the social dimension. it is true that lisbon has brought growth and jobs, but not everyone has been able to enjoy them. six million young people leave school without a diploma. migrants, people with disabilities, hardly get a job, 16% of europeans live in poverty. it is proof that a strategy of growth and jobs does not automatically lead to social inclusion and decent work for all. we therefore want to see the social dimension of the lisbon strategy strengthened by a new guideline which ensures active social integration for all. second, not all jobs are of good quality. the share of precarious contracts (temporary work, involuntary parttime work, temporary agency work) has increased considerably. women and young people in particular are often stuck in low-quality jobs. in addition, expenditure by member states on active labour market policies, guidance and training has decreased rather than increased. the member states have thus clearly not yet understood that a balanced flex-security approach must combine flexibility and contract security and that active investment in people is a must to counter segmentation in the labour market. we therefore want all the basic principles of flex security to be included in the guidelines, including quality of work and investing in people. thirdly, the social dimension of the lisbon programme is a very thin one. we are promised a new social agenda, and it is good that it will be part of the lisbon package. we do, however, expect the european commission to present an ambitious social agenda, not only with the announcements on demography, training and migration, but also with legislative initiatives to improve the quality of work and to strengthen the fight against poverty and social exclusion. i would like to wish the council, mr president, an extra dose of social ambition for the spring.

mr president, european integration - and this is what the fathers of european integration have always said - is built up step by step, sometimes in small steps, sometimes in large steps. the rapporteur has rightly emphasized that the lisbon treaty is a major qualitative step forward for europe. you could even speak of a quantum leap. it is comparable to the treaty of rome, when we created the common market, the single act with the single market and the maastricht treaty with monetary union. the maastricht treaty also gave a modest start to political union. by giving the union legal personality and abolishing the pillars, the treaty of lisbon gives europe its definitive political dimension. it needs it to be able to act as a global actor in today's global world and thereby set the standards that that global world needs. the generalization of codecision has also made the process of decisionmaking in this political union more democratic. the next step is to ratify this treaty, and a coordinated text would certainly help to better understand the progress being made. but text is one, ratification is two, implementation is three. that may be the most important thing we have to do right now. i was pleased to hear from the presidency and the commission that they too are beginning to think and work on this implementation, because in this implementation we can still go in different directions, we can also go in the wrong directions if we are not careful. it is therefore also very important that parliament should also be involved in implementing this treaty, so as to set out clearly the way in which it is to be implemented. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we want the progress that is in the treaty.

mr president, like my good friend mr claeys, i voted against this report. oh yes, many of the elements mentioned in this report are undoubtedly true and pertinent. i am thinking, for example, of the modernisation of labour markets, investment in entrepreneurship and simplification of taxation which are being advocated here. but this parliament is completely wrong when it considers a european immigration policy to attract economic immigrants to be a spearhead of the lisbon strategy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat illegal immigration, including the introduction of a system of border controls at the external borders of the union, the rejection of any national policy of mass regularisation and a systematic policy of deportation of illegal immigrants.

vice-president of the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, you are looking at a proposal for a new framework regulation on aviation safety, which you are all very familiar with. i thank you for all the work and efforts you have put into bringing about a positive outcome in a conciliation process which has been long and difficult. we are now at the final stage of the road to a new framework regulation, further proof of better regulation. the council formally completed the third reading on 4 march of this balanced and beneficial text which is important for industry and passengers. it is now up to you, members of the european parliament, to complete this process. i thank mr costa for inviting parliament to follow this text and thus allowing the conciliation to be successful. in this conciliation, everyone has shown compromise. but i think i can say that parliament has won because the majority of the amendments and ideas proposed are in the text on which you are going to vote. i understand your expectations about the funding principle. i have made a very firm personal commitment during the conciliation, mr president costa, ladies and gentlemen. if i may quote myself: i intend, as i said, to submit to the commission a draft legislative proposal on the sharing of the costs between taxpayers and users of the stricter aviation safety measures that member states may adopt beyond the basic standards provided for in this new regulation. this is without prejudice to the commission's right of initiative and to the respect of internal procedures, including the carrying out of an appropriate impact assessment before any legislative proposal. in addition, the adaptation to the new comitology decision will give the european parliament, through the introduction of the regulatory procedure with scrutiny, greater power over the general rules which the commission is called upon to draw up following the adoption of the framework regulation. while allowing more flexibility in the means of adapting the legislation of the regulatory procedure with scrutiny, all this offers a counterweight in the general implementing legislation. so in all these situations we are making progress institutionally. i would remind you of the statement made on article 4. the commission declares its intention to propose general measures within the meaning of the current article 4 (1a) and detailed implementing measures within the meaning of the current article 4 (2). the commission intends to make use of article 251 of the treaty on european union to propose any addition of essential elements to the common basic standards provided for in this regulation. the commission notes that it is important to allow for the development of detailed enforcement measures through methods that not only ensure effective detection and deterrence, but also allow for the necessary flexibility and speed, while respecting the confidentiality of sensitive documents in order to protect travellers. the commission also notes that the continuity of the implementing rules is ensured until they are replaced by new measures adopted under this regulation. the new regulation allows the development of better adapted legislation, better able to address safety issues in a holistic and flexible way, rather than through a series of individual and repetitive approaches. initial estimates by the european airline association suggest that costs could be reduced by up to 500 million as a result of more flexible methods. i will summarize, mr president. this new regulation, when adopted, will make it easier to adopt provisions with third countries, which have equally high levels of aviation safety. this concept, called one stop security, is in the interest of passengers, but also in the interest of the aviation sector in the european union. and then, the latest technological developments will allow us, i hope, to amend the total ban on liquids on aircraft by the beginning of next year. as soon as the appropriate technologies for detecting liquid explosives are available, i will endeavour to take account of this new situation at the regulatory level. and the european parliament will have the opportunity to consider any road map for changing the rules on this. ladies and gentlemen of the house, it has been a long journey. it took a lot of effort on the part of everyone to find that compromise which allows the adoption of this new regulation. this new regulation will not only improve aviation safety, but will also reduce costs, simplify rules and protect civil aviation more effectively. so i thank parliament for the work it has done in drawing up this legislative proposal and i naturally hope with all my heart that you will support it and that it will be adopted.
the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. and also how huge the impact could be for the economy and therefore for each of us. it is therefore important that we do everything we can to ensure maximum safety, and to do that it is of course essential that we achieve maximum harmonisation at european level. but at the same time we must also strike a balance between security on the one hand and comfort, privacy and freedom of movement on the other, as the rapporteur has control. the future will tell whether the system works to everyone's satisfaction. my group will also support this.

mr president, the commission communication has said a great deal about foreign affairs, and yet it leaves me a little bit hungry. it is true that heading 4 is underfunded. yes, we will have to pay more, unfortunately, for palestine and for kosovo. but that's a fact, not a strategy. i would like to know, for example, what are the political conditions for aid to be effective, not just visible, but effective. how can we use financial instruments to make europe a necessary soft power, a democratic model, and not just a cash drawer? how are we going to use conditionality? all these questions, though vital, are not addressed. one example, and not the only one: we have never paid so much money for palestine, the people of gaza have never been so poor since 1967, and the escalation of violence continues in the middle east, where we are constantly rebuilding what has been destroyed. what is the lesson for us? as long as there is no perfect coherence between european foreign policy and its financial instruments, the latter serving the former and not the other way around, europe will never be the global player it wants to be and in this respect i think the commission's communication is singularly lacking in ambition.
mr president, mr president of the commission, madam commissioner, as regards the european union in the world, the european socialists share the commission's concern to continue the work begun in 2006 to improve the efficiency of the way in which it cooperates. i would remind you that there is still a great deal of work to be done, whether it be defining development itself, ensuring coherence between the various policies of the european union, coordinating donors or ensuring that actions are complementary. as regards the economic partnership agreements, i would like to point out that it is essential for us european socialists to consider them first and foremost from the point of view of development, that is to say, from the point of view of their capacity to significantly improve the lives of the people of the acp countries. it is essential that the commission's 2009 strategy is perfectly clear in this respect. that does not seem to be quite the case at the moment. we all know that additional funds will be needed to finance accompanying measures to offset the negative impact of the economic partnership agreements on the world's poorest countries. we would like the commission to clarify where these funds will come from. we also regret that the commission document does not contain any specific proposals for the implementation of the millennium development goals before 2015. is it because there is no ambition in this area or because 2009 will mark a radical change in the paradigm of european development policy? in the annex, the commission note also refers to the implementation of the first action plan of the eu-africa strategy. we welcome this and, above all, we urge the commission to make every effort to ensure that the first actions to be undertaken under this new strategy are in line with the legitimate expectations of millions of africans who are directly concerned by the success of this strategy.
in writing. − i wanted to support the proposal for a european institute of technology initially, because it fills the existing gap between higher education, research and innovation, an important fact for the economy and the internal market. the eit therefore seems to be a brilliant project, except that the funding is not adequately provided. i therefore do not support the present proposal and the council's common position, mainly because the budget is inadequate. the commission proposed a reasonable amount to cover the needs of the kics (knowledge and innovation communities) for 6 years. the council is cutting the budget to the extent that this is no longer possible. however, an eit impact assessment study showed that sound basic funding is an essential success factor. in its present form, kics are forced to seek external funding in other community programmes, which are not intended for this purpose. the member states will therefore have to provide the financing themselves, as if private sponsorship could be counted on. with this proposal, the project is doomed to fail. finally, the fact that climate change as a focus for the first kics was explicitly removed from the proposal no longer makes my support desirable.
the chairman of the council. − the presidency of the council welcomes the report by rapporteur lutz goepel as a very important and also a significant contribution to the debate. the council also had a lot of work to do in preparing its response to the commission's communication. as you know, it was discussed by the ministers at three council meetings in november, january and february. next week, we will also try to reach certain conclusions with the ministers regarding the joint reform health check-up communication. we will do so on the basis of some general principles. it follows that, when adopting the reform of the common policy in 2003, the council clearly stated its intention to evaluate in future the effectiveness of the reforms adopted, in particular their impact in relation to the objectives pursued and the effects on agricultural markets. it is also important to note that decisions on health reform will take into account the european council conclusions on the financial perspective 2007-13 and the european council's call to the commission to carry out a comprehensive review of the eu budget and its expenditure in 2008-09. the presidency agrees with the commission that the review of the cap constitutes an important part of the preparatory work for the budgetary review in 2008/09, but it does not prejudge the conclusions in this respect. we note that the reforms of 2003-04 have largely achieved the objectives set, both in terms of responding to market conditions and in bringing agricultural policy closer to the citizen. the ministers are also convinced that the commission's communication correctly assesses the main developments following the reforms adopted in those years. the council will pay particular attention to three key issues: the single payment scheme, of course, the role of market-support instruments in the market, and of course, responding to existing and emerging challenges in the area of climate change risk management, among others. we will strive to strike the right balance between adapting policy to new challenges and changing circumstances and maintaining a clear and stable framework. as regards simplification, i would like to say that the european council has attached a key importance to this issue and we also expect the commission to identify concrete measures to achieve this objective. as i have already said, the council will try to reach conclusions next week on these and many other issues, such as risk management and so on, the milk quota, the transition to abolition in this respect, the issues related to the second pillar, and your report will undoubtedly make a valuable contribution to the further discussion on this.
member of the commission. − mr president, once again i want to thank the committee on agriculture and rural development and mr goepel in particular for their hard work, resulting in an excellent report. we are at the stage in the first round where we have consulted many stakeholders and different organisations and individuals to have their opinions and to collect different ideas. the opinion of the european parliament is crucial, together with the expected conclusions of the council next week, as we work to finalise the legislative proposals scheduled to be adopted by the commission on 20 may. immediately thereafter, on the same day here in strasbourg, i will be presenting to the european parliament the legal texts. but, basically, i am quite happy to see that all the three institutions – the european parliament, the council and the commission – although we have different ‘shopping lists’, are all in the same shop. i will not go into detail on the different issues, but will give my view on the three most important ones. firstly, on direct payments: these are inevitably linked to the long-term security that we want to give to our farmers. i therefore agree with the statement in your report that direct payments remain necessary as a basic income guarantee. the single payment scheme gives a real lift to the competitiveness of our farmers, but we must make the system more effective, more efficient and simpler. therefore, i advocate further decoupling. our experiences with the 2003 reform show that the decoupling has been working quite well and, therefore, i think we should move further and extend the benefits of decoupling, except where coupled payments have a very solid justification. i have also noticed that you support the idea of a more flexible article 69 as a valuable tool. however, we should not expect article 69 to solve all the different problems, and i will fight any attempt to re-introduce coupled support through the backdoor using article 69. i will not go into detail about the dairy sector again, as we have already had this discussion. however, i will just mention some of the market instruments: intervention and private storage. like you, i recognise that some of our market instruments still have a place, but we must keep them relevant to the european union as it is now. i am glad that we agree that they should play their role in the future as genuine safety nets. risks related to bad weather and outbreaks of animal diseases, as you highlight, are certainly a focus point in our discussion. however, we are not starting from zero: the council discussed this issue in 2005 and concluded that new measures at eu level should not interfere with what is already available in the different member states. we have to be sure that new measures do not interfere with farmers’ ability to react to market signals, and they have to be compatible with the wto system, where there has to be a loss of more than 30%. concerning modulation, i have made it clear on many occasions that we need to have for the future a very ambitious rural development policy. i was very disappointed with the fact that, during the discussions on the financial perspective at the end of 2005, the heads of state and government agreed to take a significant part off the rural development funding. however, i must say that your report is rather cool about my ideas in this area. but i think that we can justify the fact that our rural development policy is financially overstretched. if we have high expectations for our rural development policy, including the need, as the president-in-office rightly said, to use it to meet new challenges, like water management and bio-diversity, then it does not work to load more tasks on to it without putting in more money. i think modulation is the best way to provide more funding. i have read with interest what you are proposing and i am sure we will come back to this issue. if your report is cool on modulation, i would describe it as extremely chilly on reductions of payments to large farms – you call that ‘degressivity’. this is not an issue that can be simply ignored. we know that with our transparency initiative, there will be a strong focus from all sides on the large sums of money going to single farmers and single landowners. on the other hand, i take everyone’s concerns on this issue very seriously. and i have looked with interest at your report’s concept of ‘progressive modulation’. i think there are some good ideas there that we would consider carefully on the legal proposals. if i have been a little too long, it is only to show clearly my dedication and my huge interest in having the opportunity to discuss here in parliament the future of the european agricultural policy.
