member of the commission. − things have moved on since the commission’s update to the committee on development in january of this year. the legal texts have been jointly checked for the caribbean economic partnership agreement, and we are beginning the process leading to signature and ratification. in other regions, we have been preparing the interim agreements for signature and have been reflecting on how to replace these with full epas. i have just returned from a visit to southern and eastern africa taking in lesotho, south africa, botswana and zambia. i held ministerial meetings with the sadc and esa regions, and i had extensive discussions with president mbeki on both epas and the dda, incidentally. i was struck in all meetings with esa and sadc by the commitment of the regions to move forward with the negotiations, not to look back and not to unravel what has been achieved, with all the disastrous implications for trade security that that unravelling would have. this was reflected in joint declarations in which we made clear our shared commitment to agree full epas by the end of this year. we have also held senior official meetings with central africa and west africa in recent days. central africa wants a full epa by july, and west africa intends to reach a full epa in mid-2009. finally, the pacific region is consulting internally, but the agreed objective remains to finalise a full epa in 2008. my clear sense is that most of the regions are now coming out of a period of stock-taking and reflection after the developments of last december and are clearly restating their commitment to move towards full epas. this is an important sign that their commitment to integrating trade and development strategies in a progressive, forward-looking way. it is a commitment i welcome. our objectives for full epas for whole regions and covering the full range of trade, trade-related and development issues remain unchanged. in the mean time non-ldcs like kenya, côte d’ivoire and others say they have protected thousands of agricultural jobs through the interim agreements, and ldcs like tanzania and lesotho praise improvements in rules of origin. sensitive agricultural sectors are protected, and the acp now have time and space to work out how to reach full regional agreements. epas need to be closely monitored in both aid and trade aspects. this is why the caribbean epa establishes compressive monitoring arrangements, including parliamentary and other consultative subcommittees. the interim agreements will be replaced with full epas that include similar provisions before any substantive liberalisation commitments apply on the acp side.
commissioner, i'll take note of your answer. we shall examine this in more detail. but you cannot deny that most of the acp countries have a very poor negotiating capacity and yet sometimes they are faced with a block, with a fait accompli. i met last week - and this has nothing to do with the acp countries - a trade union leader from guatemala, mr pinzon, who told me about his difficult situation as a trade union leader in the negotiations between the european union and central america. this is a common situation in countries where certain groups of the population and certain positions are extremely weak. my question is: can you make sure that in trade agreements of this kind, both in the epas and in those with other blocs in the world, such as central america, that certain rights, for example trade union rights and international labour rights, are imposed in a binding way so that they are respected? i am not an expert on the subject, but i have the impression that there are important gaps in the agreements.
member of the commission. - madam president, i would of course like to thank mr staes for his excellent work as rapporteur and the environment and agriculture committees for their contribution to the debate on this proposal for a regulation. the proposal is part of the thematic strategy on the sustainable use of pesticides. the objective of this thematic strategy is to reduce the impact of pesticides on human health and the environment. the proposal for a regulation under discussion today contributes to this objective by ensuring that reliable and comparable data are collected in all member states, so that harmonised risk indicators can be calculated. the commission proposal is the result of years of work with the oecd and research bodies on the development of these risk indicators. our initial intention in the commission was to cover all types of pesticides, including biocides. however, the preparatory phase of the legislation made it clear that this sector, that of biocides, is somewhat harmonised and neither the commission nor the member states have sufficient experience or knowledge to propose concrete measures on biocides, so the decision was to exclude this category of products from the initial proposal, which is subject to its consideration. i know that mr staes' report defends this inclusion, but we had reasons to consider finally non-inclusion. i can assure the rapporteur and all of you, however, that the commission will take the opportunity afforded by the revision of the biocidal products directive, due to be completed by the end of this year, to make a proposal which covers this sector in a coherent way, including the collection of data. the report by mr staes also calls for the extension of the scope of the regulation to non-agricultural uses of plant protection products, including production and marketing statistics. in our proposal we decided to leave these data outside the scope of the regulation to avoid overlaps with other statistical regulations on production and marketing, overlaps which would have the effect of increasing the administrative cost of producing these statistics. we are, however, open to examining how to make the most of existing production and marketing statistics for the benefit of the objectives of the thematic strategy. i would like to conclude, ladies and gentlemen, madam president, by recalling the high economic cost of implementing this regulation, which ranges from eur 10 to eur 25 million per year in an initial estimate for the union of 25, which leads us to recommend that member states be given sufficient flexibility in applying this regulation. i am confident that, with good cooperation between parliament, the council and the commission, we can reach an agreement as soon as possible, since, as you know, the process of collecting this data is long, complex and takes about five years. it would be desirable for the regulation to be applicable as soon as possible and, of course, once the framework directive on the thematic strategy has been adopted. finally, i would like to thank mr staes again for his excellent work on a very complex proposal, but at the same time one of enormous importance for the environment and human health.
- i'm the reporter. - i think the commissioner has summed up his point very well on a number of points. the commission's proposal for the regulation on statistics and pesticides is indeed directly linked to the thematic strategy on the sustainable use of pesticides. the commissioner has also made it very clear. the aim is to achieve european harmonisation of national statistics on the use and placing on the market of pesticides. and that has to be done at the level where that is functionally best possible; the aim is therefore - and this is an important objective - to reduce the risks and burden on the environment and human health resulting from the use of pesticides in the medium term. in drawing up my report i had four objectives: 1. ensure sufficient comparability of data; 2. ensure that no duplication of data is made; 3. extend the scope of application to non-agricultural uses; 4. other make the most of the data thus obtained through good reporting practices. i think i have achieved those four objectives after the vote in the committee on the environment, but i know there are still difficulties. the european people's party, in particular, was urged by the shadow rapporteur, mr nassauer, to ensure that the collection of data was regulated in a good, correct and adequate way, both in terms of confidentiality and in terms of openness and transparency. i think we member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to congratulate dr adamou on his excellent report on the subject of organ donation and transplantation. i am particularly pleased that the report supports the commission's view that the three main lines of action for organ donation and transplantation should be: firstly, improving the quality and safety of organs, secondly increasing the availability of organs and thirdly making transplants safe. ensuring high quality and safety standards in human organs is a benefit to everyone. we know that a number of organs are exchanged between eu member states. at present, there is a lack of common quality and safety standards in the procurement of human organs and, therefore, meeting this need is very important. it not only provides a comparable safety standard to apply across the eu, but also leads to a feeling of security and solidarity in the transplantation system. the commission plans to propose a legislative framework on the basic safety and quality principles in relation to the donation and procurement of human organs. these requirements will be broad and flexible, to maximize the number of transplantations taking place. after all, this is all about saving lives. we must keep uppermost in our minds the fact that there are 50 000 people on organ waiting lists across europe at this point. increasing organ availability requires raising public awareness of the issue. citizens need to know that transplantation systems are safe and reliable. otherwise they will not consent to donation. we already have some good examples in member states where the addition of transplant coordinators, for example, has had a positive impact on donation rates. the commission intends to propose an action plan with 10 priority actions to meet the challenges of organ donation and transplantation in the eu. the commission recognises the importance of fighting organ trafficking as reflected in the communication on organ donation and transplantation. i also noted dr adamou's comments and will seriously consider them. his report makes it clear that we should look very carefully at how we intend to respond to the menace of organ trafficking in the eu. the commission will monitor the situation in collaboration with our international partners, the council of europe and the world health organisation. we shall also support interpol in order to continue with the monitoring of this problem. moreover, i believe that by increasing organ availability in the european union, we shall indirectly fight transplant tourism and trafficking of organs. in addition, organ trafficking will be included in this year's commission report on an eu action plan on trafficking in human beings.

organizations. the hearing we organized and the study visit to spain also gave us some interesting insights, and i would like to thank the rapporteur today for including our input in his report. firstly, we want to ensure that the directive on quality and safety, as proposed by the commission, provides sufficient flexibility and does not lead to additional administrative burdens or even result in a reduction in the number of bodies available. for us, it is still the doctor who ultimately decides on quality and safety. in order to guarantee that quality and safety, we also ask that member states monitor and evaluate post-transplant and post-donation results. since the comparability of data between member states is of great importance, a common methodology for data analysis may be sought. secondly, the organization of the donation system is also the most essential factor for us in increasing the number of donors, even more than the legislation on the subject. we therefore ask that hospitals should employ sufficient staff to identify potential donors and to provide support to the families of those who have died. we see that countries that already have such transplant coordinators are seeing an increase in the number of donors. this also shows that the exchange of good practice between member states is crucial. the european union can only facilitate this. it is also important that hospitals raise awareness of the positive outcomes of transplants among citizens and that they also proactively engage with the media. fourthly, we think it is very important that living donation can be considered as complementary to post-mortem donation. we also ask that living donors not be discriminated against, for example by insurance companies. finally, organ donation must also remain strictly non-commercial, altruistic and voluntary. we believe that any compensation should only cover the costs and inconveniences associated with the donation. the commission, together with the member states, needs to be much more proactive in combating organ trafficking, organ trafficking and transplant tourism. traceability is very important here and europol must also do vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us. the european commission has been working hard for years to reduce particulate matter and ultra-fine particulate matter. the commission has proposed a whole series of legislative measures since 2005 to improve air quality, which you are aware of. but important aspects of a more environmentally friendly transport policy are also being examined in the seventh framework programme. in developing standards for particulate emissions from motor vehicles, the commission initially focused on diesel cars and commercial vehicles, as the stock of these vehicles is significantly larger than the stock of motorised two-wheelers. so we've been focusing on tackling the problem where it's most prevalent. we have reached important milestones. in 2007, the parliament and the council adopted a regulation on emissions from light passenger and commercial vehicles. these are the euro 5 and euro 6 standards. it has already been adopted and is now in force. this euro 5 and euro 6 standard will reduce the additional particulate emissions from diesel vehicles from the current 25 milligrams per kilometre to 5 milligrams per kilometre. the commission also presented a proposal for a regulation on emissions from heavy-duty vehicles, the euro vi standard, on 21 december 2007. it will reduce the limit values for nitrogen oxide emissions by 80% and particulate emissions by 66% compared with the current euro v standard. the problem of particulate emissions from two-wheel vehicles is essentially a problem of two-stroke engines. the particulate emissions of four-stroke engines are very low compared to these engines, even if they comply with the euro 5 standard. in 2004, the commission commissioned a study to determine whether legislation to limit particulate emissions from two-stroke car engines is appropriate. the study found that the particles emitted by such engines are predominantly hydrocarbons. as the limits for hydrocarbon emissions from motorcycles and mopeds i.e. two-wheelers were already significantly tightened in 2004 and 2007, the situation for new vehicles has already improved considerably. in addition, manufacturers are moving from two-stroke engines to four-stroke engines in order to comply with the hydrocarbon limit values. for this reason, the commission has not yet proposed specific measures to limit particulate emissions from two-wheelers. however, i must say that the situation is much more critical in the case of older vehicles with two-stroke engines, which emit hydrocarbons and particles in larger quantities. however, these emissions can be significantly reduced by using synthetic oil instead of mineral oil for engine lubrication. the commission is therefore supporting a campaign by the european motorcycle manufacturers' association and the motorcyclists' association. the commission is nevertheless examining the need for a revision of the legislation on two- and three-wheel motor vehicles. a study was commissioned earlier this year to review the findings of the 2004 study in the light of the latest technical developments. the results of this study will be available by the end of this year. if it turns out that a legislative proposal by the commission is necessary - and i am prepared to make one if necessary - then it would be in 2009. the framework directive on the assessment and control of air quality and the relevant separate directives setting emission limit values for so-called pm 10 particles and for benzene require member states to take appropriate measures to ensure that the limit values are complied with nationally by the reference date. member states must also provide for emergency measures in the event of temporary exceedance of the limit values. in areas where the likelihood of exceeding the limit value by two-stroke engines is significantly increased, the commission considers that member states will take this situation into account in their respective air quality plans and emergency measures.

on behalf of the pse group mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his recommendation for better administrative cooperation and better possibilities for labour inspectorates. but, commissioner, you are aware that our joy in this matter is being seriously undermined by the raval and rupert decisions. they have caused a great deal of commotion, not only in the pse group in parliament but also in the trade unions outside parliament and in the countries which are currently ratifying the treaty of lisbon. we have long thought that the secondment directive was a good directive with a clear starting point. since we cannot ensure that wages and working conditions are the same everywhere in europe, it is only fair that workers can count on equal treatment wherever they work, regardless of their nationality. it is true that the posting of workers directive does not apply the full employment country principle, but only imposes a number of binding conditions. but the directive also gives member states the freedom to impose more generous provisions on the protection of workers, in accordance with their own social traditions, in accordance with collective agreements or generally binding collective agreements. the judgments now saw the legs off our equal treatment chair. the minimum protection provided for in the directive is gradually becoming a maximum. the problem of poor transposition in some member states is long gone. but we are becoming increasingly aware that the basic philosophy of the directive is wrong, and that is: the commission has already taken a number of measures to protect workers, but in a limited way. social rights to negotiate, to strike, exist only as long as they do not jeopardize the free movement of services. this is not a matter of xenophobic rhetoric, on the contrary. foreign workers are more than welcome. the free movement of workers guarantees equal treatment from day one, and that is what we want under the free movement of services. my group therefore calls for a revision of the secondment directive for three reasons. firstly, to ensure that equal treatment is guaranteed for national and foreign workers. secondly, to ensure that national systems of social consultation are fully respected, and thirdly, to ensure that fundamental social rights are safeguarded for all. we are counting on your the chairman of the council. − the council, like the european parliament, is deeply concerned by the recent events and human rights violations in tibet. on 19 march, just a few days after the events, the presidency issued a statement on behalf of the european union calling for restraint. it called on the chinese government to respond to the concerns of tibetans about respect for human rights. it called on the chinese authorities and the dalai lama to establish a concrete and constructive dialogue. one that would lead to a lasting solution that would be acceptable to all and would fully respect tibetan culture, religion and identity. the slovenian presidency also made a similar statement on behalf of the european union at the human rights council in geneva on 25 march. we are aware of calls for a united nations-led investigation into china's response to events in tibet. the council called on the chinese government to lift restrictions on access to the region, which would allow an independent assessment of the situation. the eu-china human rights dialogue, which has been mentioned several times today and will take place on 15 may in ljubljana, slovenia, will be a further opportunity to discuss the situation in tibet and other pressing issues, such as the situation of human rights defenders. the eu troika will insist that the chinese authorities allow foreign journalists to enter tibet in accordance with new regulations adopted in the run-up to the olympic games. the european union will also discuss internet censorship with china in ljubljana, continuing discussions that began in the last round of dialogue last october. it is important to assess the human rights dialogue in the context of the wider eu-china dialogue on human rights, reforms and political and social progress. the human rights dialogues with china regularly address issues that have been the cause of the unrest we have seen in tibet. these are freedom of religion and belief, minority rights, cultural rights. in the last round of dialogue last october, the eu troika discussed very openly with the chinese authorities the restriction of religious freedom. examples of positive changes in china's human rights record include the new labor law that came into force this year and the reinstatement of the supreme people's court's power to review death sentences. as a result, the number of executions has decreased. finally, i would like to stress that the european union is following the situation in burma as closely as it is following the situation in tibet. in the latter respect, the european union is very active in supporting the efforts of the united nations to accelerate the transition to democracy, reconciliation and development and is involved in consultations with its asian partners.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in 2001, the wto's own sutherland report launched a very lively debate on its institutional reform, a debate which has unfortunately been extinguished far too quickly. some people think that the debate on reform is not appropriate today. they want to wait for the outcome of the doha development agenda before they can debate the institution as such. but we don't think it's mutually exclusive. from the development perspective, we advocate a new differentiation of the developing countries in the wto, based on the development needs of individual countries rather than categories of countries. mrs muscardini herself has already explained why this is necessary. not only is there a need for more transparency within the wto, but also for better cooperation with other international organisations such as the international labour organisation and unctad. the proposal in the sutherland report to regard financing arrangements for technical assistance to the least developed countries as a contractual right, so that they can participate fully in the multilateral trading system, is essential for our committee. finally, the dispute settlement system, which for the reasons we all know often leaves developing countries on the short end of the stick, must also be revised in the sense advocated by sutherland. in conclusion, a reform of the wto is crucial, not only to strengthen its legitimacy with all its members, including the poorest, but also, i think, to safeguard multilateralism.

we must be very aware of the price of our ambition. to divide co2 emissions by three, for example, each european would have to emit eight times less carbon dioxide. these figures are brutal to show that beyond the use of these market instruments in environmental protection, our individual responsibility cannot be avoided here and that the union will need a lot of energy to keep the course so that sustainable development is not just a fad but our model for all of us for tomorrow.

the rapporteur. - mr president, fifteen years after the first election observation mission to russia, and eight years after the commission's first communication on this subject, what is the outcome of the election observation? well, first, a positive report. the aim of this report, which was written jointly and in full agreement with ignacio salafranca, was to highlight the successes achieved: the increasing professionalisation of election observation missions, the creation in the union of a corps of experienced observers - successes such as the fact that the budget now amounts to about 25% of the instrument for democracy and human rights - and, above all, the successful combination of two missions, also led by parliamentarians, the union's, which is more technical and long-term, and parliament's, which is short-term and more political. but we must continue to dig the path. the main recommendations of this report are, firstly, to open the missions to acp, eurolat and apm member countries, but with great caution. care must be taken not to break the professionalisation and to maintain the european seal. secondly, to keep the budget allocated to the democracy and human rights initiative at around 25%, to draw up an annual report assessing the year's observations - this is extremely important -, to look at the security of computerised elections - a new challenge for election observers - and, above all, to work much more on follow-up. because it is in the monitoring that the work hurts and that elections really struggle to be the real lever towards democracy that they should be. the report outlines several ways forward in the follow-up, but i shall simply focus - i am addressing the council here mainly - on the political follow-up. it is unacceptable that when elected representatives or presidents are elected on fraudulent grounds, the european union's policy towards them should continue as if nothing had happened. business as usual. it is unthinkable - and it is disastrous - that when elected representatives are elected on a democratic basis, they are not entitled to the respect and legitimacy that this election confers on them. we've had unfortunate examples in the past that have led to dramatic situations. i don't want to make a school case out of it. it is a consensus report, not a polemic one, and europe has a good instrument. you can't deny yourself the pleasure.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the negotiations, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations, and i am not saying that it should not be involved in the negotiations. we should only have the courage to make a number of points clearer and to denounce without any hesitation, first and foremost, the fanatical totalitarianism of the muslim states. the fact is that in europe islam is recognized and treated as an equal religion, and muslims are naturally treated as equal citizens, and islam in my country is recognized and subsidized by the government, but christians and non-believers in the muslim world are openly treated as second-class citizens and openly discriminated against as second-class citizens. for example, in so-called modern algeria, a penalty of five years in prison and hefty fines are imposed on anyone who attempts to convert a muslim. in other muslim countries too, discrimination and oppression of non-muslim minorities is just official policy; it is therefore time for concerted european action. i note that the organisation of the islamic conference constantly talks about the so-called discrimination against muslims here and there, but that the european union, on the other hand, is silent in all its languages about the very unreal, systematic, officially organised persecution of people of other faiths in muslim countries. it is a little bit of an impression that the official human rights dialogues and clauses have become just a kind of moral wrapping paper. but how credible can the european union be if it opens its doors wide to a country like turkey, where, as we know, there is widespread torture by the police? what lessons can the european union teach us about freedom of expression and freedom of the press, when it has itself been conducting secret and open accession negotiations for years with a country like turkey, which reporters without borders describes as one of the worst violators of freedom of expression? and what lessons does the union have to teach about religious freedom if it embraces a country like turkey, which has murdered or expelled all its former religious minorities, and which openly discriminates against the few remaining ones. but if we look beyond the official rhetoric and the official declarations and clauses, we often see a european human rights policy of two measures and two weights and a huge gap between word and deed. one of the perfect symbols of this ambiguous european policy is the current european commissioner for development cooperation, louis michel. when he was still belgian foreign minister, he presented himself with his european cordon sanitaire against austria, as a sort of conscience of the world, but at the same time he baked sweet sandwiches with the criminal dictator fidel castro. the european commissioner himself, a few months ago, again called for a massive improvement in relations with cuba, whereas, according to all the human rights organizations, the cuban state apparatus is constantly bent on curtailing the rights and freedoms of the cuban people. such men, such european commissioners, are ill-placed to pursue a human rights policy. and one more thing: what is absolutely lacking in this report is an urgently needed plea for the restoration of the right to free expression in a number of our own european countries, including belgium. in belgium, the opposition party is besieged with complaints and trials and numerous laws have been tightened to make it impossible for people to express their opinions freely on the immigration problem. it is time we too dared to see the beam in our own eye.

member of the commission. − mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission's strategy report of december last year contained a very positive message, which was supported by the european council at its spring meeting in march 2008. the results of the 2005 renewed lisbon strategy are already visible at the end of the first three-year cycle. economic growth and employment growth are impressive. a number of things indicate that structural reforms are beginning to bear fruit. all member states have been reforming since 2005, but some are doing better. in the last year, we have seen signs of a certain fatigue with reforms. but europe must not stop and slow down. on the contrary, reforms need to be pursued, if not pursued more vigorously. this is in the context of the commission's proposal to maintain the integrated guidelines including the employment guidelines in their current form until 2010. the commission is firmly convinced that these guidelines provide the right framework for the current challenges of the european labour market and that they are fulfilling their role. this was confirmed by the spring european council, which adopted a general approach based on stability. member states must be given the opportunity to complete the reforms they have started, while at the same time time time allowing time for the results of these reforms to be reflected. the commission also found that amendments should be proposed, with an emphasis on some important tasks to be addressed in the near future. these include climate change, energy, the social dimension and flexicurity. the commission also wanted to stress the need for greater involvement and stronger implementation. as a result, the agreed objectives and reference levels were incorporated into the text of the guidelines.
- i'm the reporter. - first of all, let me thank the colleagues with whom i have had the opportunity to work on this report for the excellent cooperation. i think that the report has been slightly over-simplified, but the message, commissioner, must be clear: the european parliament does not want business as usual for the employment strategy. it is true that there is still a lot of work to be done in the member states to put the guidelines into practice, but it is also true that the guidelines need to be adapted to address a number of essential shortcomings. i will mention three essential ones: the commission's proposals are not without merit. too many groups in society are not benefiting from growth and employment. people with disabilities, migrants and the unskilled are too often left to their own devices, and we need everyone in society and everyone in the labour market. the employment strategy should therefore also focus on active social inclusion to combat poverty and social exclusion, by offering decent wages and high-quality services in combination with active policies for guidance and training towards work. secondly: the quality of the work. clearly more jobs have been created, but not necessarily better jobs. too many people are unwillingly stuck in precarious contracts, temporary jobs, involuntary parttime work and jobs that often guarantee them only an insufficient income. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. the training effort must be drastically increased and, moreover, all workers must be granted social rights, regardless of their status. not only is there a need for flexibility in the labour market, but there is also a need for greater security for workers. thirdly: the gender dimension. women have made a strong advance in the labour market, but there is still a long way to go to achieve equal opportunities. the pay gap remains unacceptably wide. women do not have the same access to education or the same opportunities to start a business. those who want to return to work after a career break have a particularly difficult time. overcoming difficulties in reconciling work and family life is still too often a problem of women alone, and the impact on their income is often felt even after retirement. the gender dimension must therefore be given special attention in the employment strategy in order to eliminate all inequalities between men and women. finally, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the employment strategy as a method is a necessary element in the commitment of the member states and the european union to sound social legislation. i therefore hope that all the member states of the european union will consistently transpose and apply european legislation, and i also hope, commissioner, that the commission will present us with an ambitious social agenda soon, within a few months, perhaps within a few weeks. i hope that the june council meeting and the commissioner and the commission will listen to our message. i regret, mr president, that no representative of the council presidency is here, member of the commission. - ladies and gentlemen, strict parliamentary rules do not allow me to comment in detail on your contributions, but nevertheless let me thank you for a discussion which was in-depth and which touched on the labour market and the european labour market strategy from many angles. i'd like to narrow it down to just a few basic points. first, the european employment strategy has delivered tangible results. the unemployment rate and the employment rate are now incomparably better than they have been since the 1980s, which is a clear success. in your debate you have often expressed concern about the quality of jobs. i would like to point out that the lisbon employment strategy is designed to formulate more and better jobs, more and better jobs, i.e. quality jobs are part of the strategy. what's the score? of those millions of new jobs, more than half are full-time jobs and there can be little question of quality of work. in the second half, many jobs are part-time or temporary. i think it is not a defensible hypothesis that every fixed-term job and every part-time job is a low-quality job. this hypothesis is not justified and a number of these jobs are of high quality. but there is no doubt that some jobs do not have that quality, and that is one of the issues we have to address. i personally find it disturbing and i think we need to give more attention to the problem of the so-called working poor, people who remain poor even though they work. according to the data available, this is approximately 8% of the working population, and that is short, the ultimate aim is to regulate the so-called services directive in the transport sector. how can i provide services in another country if i come from a third country? can i do this indefinitely? can i do this on any conditions? that is the fundamental question. it is not just the large hauliers that are concerned, but also the small, structured companies. on the one hand we take account of the environmental aspect by avoiding empty cars, on the other hand we are allowed - knowing that at the moment the social and also the fiscal conditions differ so much from country to country - not to promote direct dumping in these two areas through this regulation. a limitation of cabotage to three a week is, in my view, at least once a clear statement. this is much better than the term 'temporary', which would have almost led to 27 different laws in 27 different countries in the next few years, which would certainly not have been helpful to the sector. because i think the rules are clear. the fact that this must now be completed within a reasonable time is, in our view, - and i am speaking for myself - a clear thing, provided that the differences in pay and taxation are reduced. we have also looked at cabotage in a more realistic way, i.e. it can be started after the first unloading. i am opposed to the commission's view that cabotage must be possible on international journeys, even on the return journey. we therefore want a certain degree of coherence in market access as regards serious crime and information on it, just as we want to simplify administrative procedures. i would like to conclude by making another appeal to the countries themselves. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted by the introduction of new fishing vessels. it's not a ton of work. we believe that the aim is to harmonize the administrative paperwork over time and to ensure that the total cost of cabotage at european level, which is 2% for certain countries, is properly controlled, but that this represents 30% to 40% of the work in this area. i think that's the only way that what we're doing here makes sense. and then certain details that we overlook make sense.

the fight to eliminate the use of depleted uranium in weapons and ammunition worldwide is part of our overall struggle for arms control and disarmament. our very special attention is directed to weapons and munitions which are extremely dangerous and harmful to civilians and which remain dangerous long after the conflict has ended, such as landmines, cluster munitions, and weapons and munitions containing depleted uranium. i know it's a little ironic to talk about weapons that are more dangerous and more harmful than others, but that's the way it is. that is why we are concentrating on those which could have the most serious consequences for innocent civilians. in november 2006, our parliament called for a moratorium on this type of weapon, namely weapons containing depleted uranium. my country, belgium, last year enacted not just such a moratorium but a real ban on all use of uranium weapons by law and i think i can be a little proud of that. i would now like to hear from the commission and the council what they will do to make this moratorium general and to support a general ban. in addition, as the previous speaker has pointed out at length, uranium weapons also appear to have a negative effect on the soldiers who use them or are exposed to their use. and even if there may not yet be total security, it is appropriate that the council should ensure that soldiers and other personnel involved in missions under the european security and defence policy are not exposed to such risks. i would therefore like to hear from the council what concrete measures are being taken to ensure that soldiers and personnel participating in such missions are not exposed to such risks and to ensure that the population of the areas where such missions are deployed are not exposed to them either. i thank you for the answers you will give me.

which is the authority on this subject, continues to refer to an extremely broad definition of development cooperation. the improvement of our efficiency will also pass through this. commissioner, two i would like to congratulate rapporteur johan van hecke on a good report. i would also like to thank you, commissioner, for a very good staff working paper on accra. three points for you. firstly, more effective aid does require that donor countries coordinate their aid more closely on the basis of a country strategy with the southern partner countries taking the lead. but that means that the priorities must also be set in those countries democratically, in cooperation with national parliaments and ngos, which is not the case today. i also think it is absolutely essential to involve women in the dialogue. secondly, donor countries must absolutely abandon the myriad of conditions they place on aid and fully commit to measurable results. the economic conditions imposed by the imf and the world bank must be removed, since they are often in conflict with the millennium development goals. so, johan, we shall abolish paragraph 28 in the report. third, better aid is no excuse for less aid. at the moment, not only is development aid falling, but the figures are also clouded by so-called aid which does not really help the partner countries, such as money spent in europe on students or refugees or debt relief. it is therefore crucial that the donor countries reaffirm their commitments for the 0.7% and set concrete timetables. i hope, commissioner, that the council will listen to this message on monday.

security, burundians urgently need to see a positive development in their country in their very difficult daily lives.
the reports by our colleagues saryusz-wolski and kuhne are important documents and it is impossible to deal with all the aspects in a short time. so i'm going to focus on three of them. firstly, the role of our parliament in foreign and defence affairs, secondly, the very great responsibility of all member states for a coherent and effective foreign and security policy and, finally, the need to continue the fight against the proliferation of nuclear weapons and to continue to work for general arms control. the fact that we are having this debate here this afternoon with these participants is a good example of the extent to which we as the european parliament have succeeded in giving ourselves a greater role in foreign and security policy, even though this was not at all the original intention of the existing treaties. it is our determination that has led to this and also the understanding that we have found both with the commission and with the high representative and that has led to an interinstitutional agreement that makes debates like the present possible. we will of course make use of all the possibilities of the lisbon treaty to continue to play our full part. it is also because we have never exaggerated, because we have taken care not to overplay our hand, as they would say in english, that we have been able to play this role. it is clear that an effective foreign and security policy, as mr swoboda has stressed, is only possible if all the member states and the governments of all 27 member states, including the large ones, are willing to act in unison. we can make fine speeches here and everyone can make fine speeches here, but when heads of government, prime ministers or foreign ministers are each doing something different on the world stage, it cannot, it will not succeed. so the responsibility of our member states is very great, not only in the area i have mentioned, but also in making their words follow their deeds. we have had to experience this painfully, for example, in the preparations for the intervention in chad. a positive decision was taken and then it took weeks to assemble the troops and all the material. this is a very serious loss of credibility and i hope that we will all work together to ensure that this does not happen again in the future.
the partnership between the european union and the united states is a cornerstone of the eu's external policy, as the joint resolution rightly states. such a partnership presupposes a number of self-evident agreements, such as the principle that we do not interfere in the internal affairs of the other partner. i regret to note that our american allies have not always been able to do so in recent years, even in recent times. for example, the president and the state department have repeatedly spoken out and pressed for turkey's accession to the european union. this is unacceptable. it is as if the european council or the european commission were pushing for the accession or integration of mexico into the united states. it is not because turkey is a nato member state that it must be able to join the european union. nato is important to the european union, but it is not the european union. the two are separate issues, and next week's summit is a good opportunity to recall them in a friendly but firm manner.
the rapporteur. - mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the report i drafted for the committee on development gives an overview of the work of the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly in 2007. those of you who are actively involved in this work or who are interested in it further know that this is not a purely formal or symbolic structure. on the contrary, this house is increasingly acting like a genuine parliamentary assembly. its members are increasingly invested in it, influential personalities are rushing to join it and it is therefore acquiring increasing political importance. that is the first point i wanted to stress. the growing political importance of this assembly is also due to the fact that, in addition to the substantive issues relating to cooperation between the acp countries and the union, topical issues of great importance to both europeans and acp countries are discussed together. this is the case, for example, of the economic partnership agreements negotiated between the union and the acp countries. on a matter like this, the parliamentary assembly confirmed in the kigali declaration that the main stakeholders very clearly wanted the european commission to give the acp countries the time they needed to negotiate regional agreements with development as their priority objective. kigali has not only shown that there is real resistance to these agreements as they are currently being negotiated, but also that it is based on quite legitimate concerns which will now have to be taken seriously into account. similarly, it was only by participating in the assembly's proceedings that many acp parliamentarians became aware of the existence of country-specific strategy papers which, however, define the lines of cooperation to be carried out between the european union and their own countries. this situation is a reminder that for many acp parliamentarians this house is sometimes the only place where democratic expression is possible. i would also like to emphasise that in 2007 we clearly saw an increasingly important investment by civil society in the acp countries, who organise meetings and work of very high quality on the sidelines of each of the sessions organised by the assembly. in the fight for development waged by civil society in the acp countries, the assembly also provides an extraordinary opportunity for exchange and meeting. in this connection, the union must be able to provide technical and financial support for the presence of these associations and ngos in the work of the joint assembly. finally, i would like to stress that in our report we encourage the joint parliamentary assembly to strengthen the role of its political affairs committee. the idea is to make it a genuine forum for conflict prevention and resolution and to encourage close coordination between the activities of acp and eu parliamentarians as electoral observers in the context of electoral observation missions. for the rest, you should know that in 2007 the assembly met in wiesbaden and then in kigali. nine resolutions were adopted. standing committees met four times, twice in the margins of the sessions and twice between them in brussels. these committees, which are responsible for monitoring resolutions, have been organising since 2007 hearings of officials responsible for the committee in the relevant areas. in addition to several reports, the wiesbaden session adopted a resolution on the situation in darfur. there was a very constructive emergency debate on the situation in zimbabwe. the workshops on immigration, climate change, medicines for neglected diseases, all attracted a large number of participants with keen interest. the kigali session adopted the three resolutions contained in the reports of the standing committees, as well as two emergency resolutions on national disasters in the acp countries and on the situation in the congo. that is, mr president, madam commissioner, a brief summary of a report which is certainly formal, but which i wanted to give a political content which should help to make the work of the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly better known and to support it more effectively.

the fact that the commission has not taken any action to combat the scourge of the presence of millions of illegal immigrants in europe is particularly regrettable, mr president, as is shown by the hysterical reactions of the politically correct left-wing opinion-makers to this return directive. sorry, this is not a return policy. they even oblige member states to provide free legal aid to illegal aliens to challenge their expulsion. in addition, at least seven member states are obliged to reduce their detention limits. moreover, the directive actually puts the two choices of either deportation or regularisation on more or less the same level, as if it were a neutral choice, whereas the massive regularisations in several member states in recent years have had a massive suction effect and also weighed heavily on the other european countries. in conclusion, i wish this was a real return directive that would stop the sucking up of illegals once and for all, but unfortunately that is not the case. i am far from certain that this will be even a first small step in the right direction.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that we are debating a very delicate problem today - it divides this house - and at the same time a very sensitive, if not dramatic, problem, since it is likely to have fundamental consequences for the living conditions and even the lives of people who are seeking, even illegally, to come to europe to find a future that their country cannot offer them. i shall not repeat what has already been said by other speakers but will emphasize four specific points. the first is to my colleague and friend, giusto catania. it is unfair, giusto, to speak of fortress europe as if it does not accept anyone on its territory and as if it is insensitive to the misery of millions of people. according to official figures, there are now between 1.5 and 2 million immigrants who enter the european union legally every year, coming through the path of regularisation, through family reunification, through recognition of political refugee status. this means that at this rate, if we continue for 30 years - and i have no objection to this happening - between 45 and 60 million people will enter the european union legally. secondly, this directive, even if it has shortcomings, and i admit that it does, is not a regulation. in fact, it is practically a framework directive which leaves each country a margin of appreciation - with the exception of the minimum standards it sets and the limits it establishes which cannot be exceeded - it leaves each of the states the possibility of having legislation which corresponds to what the democratic majority of these countries will decide. in addition, it is provided that every three years the commission will report to parliament and that it may propose amendments. read the directive! thirdly, we must stop - i say this to some of our socialist friends - from proclaiming untruths which are too strong. the directive does not establish detention as a rule. article 14 of the directive provides for the possibility of detention in cases which are defined and it also subjects them to extremely strict judicial control. you have to read article 14 (2). so to say that detention is the rule, that it is imposed on the member states, is a falsehood. we must not stir up public opinion. there are enough real problems to invent problems that aren't real. the fourth and final point, mr president, is a message to manfred weber and the epp. despite all the shortcomings i acknowledge, i will vote for the directive, but i ask the epp not to try to solve, by means of procedural manoeuvres, problems which are essentially political and which can divide our assembly. whatever vote this house takes this morning, i think it must be respected.

- i'm the reporter. - the report by mr moreland, on behalf of the committee on energy, research and technology (a4-0177/97) on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0171 - c4-0177/97) for a directive amending directive 76/769/eec on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the permissible sound power level of towed vehicles structural unbundling of the energy sector is needed today more than ever, but it should not be an end in itself. the question is and remains: is it better for the consumer? it's not obvious. if we look at the situation on the ground, i find that in my country, belgium, consumers will pay on average eur 300 more for their energy bill in 2008 than in 2007. i realize that this is due in large part to the rising price of oil, but certainly not entirely. for example, the figures provided by mrs kuneva show that 20 to 32% of the british who have changed operators since the market opened up are worse off today than they were before. it is therefore essential that consumers should be aware of their rights in the energy sector. this is definitely not the case today. there is a lack of comprehensible information on these rights. hence the need for a strong, clear and comprehensible document that sets out the existing rights, a charter. mrs morgan has already spoken about what should be in it. i would like to add a few more elements. that the consumer should be able to change operators, yes, free of charge and within a month, certainly, but that is why the consumer should also be able to compare offers and that is why it is important that there should also be standard contracts and standard invoices. in order to be able to compare, consumers must have a clear view of their actual energy consumption and be informed at least four times a year of their actual consumption. mrs morgan has also spoken about this - and i support those ideas; the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the introduction of the smart meter, which is also included in my report. as regards social charges, i would say to mr vidal-quadras that we believe that their application should be a possibility for the member states, an appropriate instrument. that is all we want to do away with, mr vidal-quadras, in the matter of social charges. so i think it is not impossible to support that. it also seems important that there are physical points of contact where information can be obtained. the digital divide is still an incredible reality today. and last but not least, perhaps most importantly. if the consumer is to be aware of his rights and thus be well informed, we believe it is essential that he be sent a copy of the existing rights, which are clearly and comprehensibly listed, when a contract is concluded. that is what our group is asking for, nothing more and nothing less, to bring together the existing rights and to give them to the consumer in a readable document. i do not understand the commission's attitude, commissioner piebalgs, nor do i understand the attitude of the other groups. the commission announced a charter a few months ago. we're now a few months into it, and we're just down to a simple, informative checklist. we feel it is necessary to go one step further. i therefore call on the other groups to support the amendment tabled by the socialist group. we are not really inventing hot water, we are simply asking that the consumer be informed of the existing rights by sending him or her a copy when concluding a contract. if the main problem today - and this is what the european commission is saying - is clear information and the lack of it, well, then i think our proposal will provide a solution.

the commission's vice-president - i think i can say that frontex's initial actions seem satisfactory, but it is true that this agency depends on the member states for the technical equipment needed for the operations it coordinates. it is true that these are often very expensive means which the agency is not in a position to purchase because of its limited budget. the purchase of a helicopter would be equivalent to the annual budget of frontex operations, i.e. eur 40-50 million. frontex has therefore concluded technical agreements with the majority of member states on the conditions of use of the equipment made available to it. in its evaluation report, the commission recommended that frontex could buy or lease its own equipment, at least for a number of small items of equipment. that's what we can say today. i thank you for reminding me that frontex is based in warsaw and that there are indeed very motivated people there. i want to thank you for making a connection with frontex. i believe that in view of what frontex is doing now, we will be able, as the evaluations progress, to see what improvements need to be made to its operation.
the commission's vice-president - madam president, any measures taken by the european union or its member states to combat terrorism must of course take account of fundamental rights. the instruments or policies for combating terrorism which do not respect fundamental rights very often play into the hands of the terrorists themselves. the data retention directive requires member states to require providers of communication services and public electronic networks to retain data relating to the exchange of communications for a minimum of six months and a maximum of two years. this directive provides important safeguards against the misuse of data. the directive states that the preservation of the content of communications is strictly outside the mandate of the directive. the directive also states that access to retained data must be defined by the domestic law of each member state, that these provisions must be in accordance with the european convention on human rights and, of course, respect the principle of proportionality and necessity. this means that data retained under the directive cannot be processed by the authorities of a member state to interfere with a person's privacy, except in very justified cases for the purpose of detecting and prosecuting a serious criminal offence. the directive requires that the data retained can only be accessed by the competent national administrations. this means, of course, that the data stored cannot be used by companies or private individuals. of course, the fact that this access to stored data is limited means that once again, we cannot use this data to monitor citizens. in addition, directive 95/46 on data protection and directive 2002/58 on data protection in the electronic communications sector apply, of course, to data retained in accordance with the directive. in conclusion, mr president, the data retention directive is essential to make the police more efficient and to study, detect and search for terrorists, while ensuring respect for the privacy of individuals and the protection of personal data. the commission will monitor and ensure that the implementation and application of the directive by the member states is done in accordance with fundamental rights.
the commission's vice-president - madam president, i have to say that for the data retention directive, 15 member states have notified their transposition instruments and the commission has initiated infringement proceedings for failure to notify against all member states which had not notified the appropriate measures by the end of november 2007. in answer to your question, the commission has set up a group of experts to assist the commission in analysing the effectiveness of this directive and the balance you want. the commission will report in september 2010 to take stock of the directive's contribution to the investigation, detection and prosecution of serious infringements. the commission will of course examine any comments which may be forwarded to it by the member states or by the working party set up by the article of the directive. i think i can confirm that we really have to make sure that this is in line with the data retention directives and the data protection directive. i therefore believe that if there are data breaches here and there, we will draw the conclusions immediately. i think we have really tried to respect the necessities of the fight against terrorism and to reconcile them with the protection of fundamental rights. that is what i have to do now, and i will be very careful, mr mitchell.
the commission's vice-president - madam president, you are right; we must ensure that data protection is respected. the data protection officer is also responsible for monitoring all this. you're right, we need cooperation; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the data protection rules are respected. so i think that cooperation is progressing at the moment. this group is called the 'article 29' group because article 29 of the directive provided that the independent data protection authorities of each member state were to meet. i think there is a good tool here and it should be used. that's all i could tell you.
the commission's vice-president - i thank mr moraes for raising this important issue. the european arrest warrant has really achieved significant results. the innovation is the mutual recognition of legal decisions and there is no longer a requirement for double incrimination for a list of 32 serious offences. this makes the procedures quicker. compared to the lengthy extradition procedure which usually takes over a year, a surrender procedure through the european arrest warrant takes on average only 43 days. all of this is happening judge to judge. the new procedure is widely used by the member states. in 2006, 6 752 european arrest warrants were issued. 2 042 persons were arrested on this basis and 1 892 persons were actually handed over. more than one fifth of the persons surrendered in accordance with the eaw procedure were nationals of the executing member state. when you consider that in 2006 there were almost as many arrest warrants as there had been proceedings in 10 years, it is clear that the european arrest warrant was a success. the initial constitutional problems of some member states have been overcome. nevertheless, mr moraes, it must be admitted that in the case of the surrender of national nationals, the use of old extradition practices, which are a sign of residual distrust in certain states and in certain criminal justice systems, has sometimes been observed, but we believe that this will disappear and that the system will be able to function optimally. on the basis of an analysis of the transposition of the framework decision into the legislation of the member states, the commission reported in 2005 and 2007 on the functioning of the european arrest warrant. this has revealed some adjustments in the member states. a series of peer reviews are currently being conducted, in which the commission is participating as an observer. this series of evaluations will be completed in early 2009 and it is then, on the basis of the outcome of these evaluations, that the commission will present a new report identifying the different practices, shortcomings and issues examined at european union level to further improve the quality of the functioning of the european arrest warrant, which nevertheless remains, in my view, one of the great successes of the principle of mutual recognition.
the commission's vice-president - mr moraes, first of all i would like to remind you that, on the basis of the current treaties, the commission does not have the power to initiate infringement proceedings against a member state, in this case, if during the monitoring and evaluation procedures, incorrect implementation of the framework decision is noted. however, i can tell you that i will be very attentive to the implementation of the european arrest warrant. the forum on justice, which we will be organising periodically, will be an opportunity for us to bring together judges from all the member states of the union, and we will use this opportunity to make even greater progress in the proper use of the european arrest warrant. i thank you for asking this question and i can assure you that we will do our utmost. i would add that the strengthening of eurojust will also enable us to make better use of the european arrest warrant.
the commission's vice-president - you are quite right, the european arrest warrant must be a success, because it is really entering into a system of mutual recognition, which means that at this point the different judicial authorities begin to really work together in a climate of trust. that is why the proper use of the european arrest warrant, which has greatly accelerated and simplified the procedures, must really be the very example of this new justice which you so ardently desire, much faster and obviously much more simplified in its exercise. that's all i can tell you. we are going to be monitoring very closely the use of this european arrest warrant, which i believe is one of the best tools we have at present in the context of judicial cooperation in criminal matters, and which obviously needs to be improved a great deal.
member of the commission. − mr president, on this issue i had exhaustive discussions with my colleagues on the authorisation of the use of amt substances as a decontamination measure to clean the surface of poultry carcases, during the college meeting of 28 may. as you know, the possibility of removing surface contamination with substances other than potable water was approved by council and parliament in 2004 in the hygiene regulations. in order to approve substances for use in decontamination of any meat, the applicable law requires a positive, evidence-based finding in comitology, indicating that the use of the substances does not pose risks to health or the environment. let me remind you that there was a long-standing request by the usa for approval of four amt substances for use in decontamination of poultry carcases. against this background, we have requested various scientific opinions which have assessed the risk of the use of the four amt substances from the point of view of health, environment and anti-microbial resistance. on the basis of the outcome of the various scientific opinions, we decided to present a proposal. the scientific opinions allow us to proceed with a proposal for authorisation, but not at any price. pursuant to the scientific opinions, a number of risk-management conditions had to be formulated. in proposing this text, my bottom line is very clear: no matter which commercial partner is at stake, no international commitment can be allowed to undermine the principles of food safety and the health of eu consumers. hence the need to ensure that any use of such substances would not replace the obligation to comply with the hygiene conditions along the food chain as set by community legislation, and that such use would not make up for prior poor hygiene conditions. in fact, in its opinion, efsa has indicated that amt could be considered as a useful tool to complement the hygiene practices already implemented in the european union. this is why i have imposed strict, yet proportional conditions. under these conditions, we can assure a proposal which is defensible from the point of view of public health and safety. the conditions that we propose are the following. first, one single substance is applied. time of exposure and concentration of the substances are defined. the poultry carcases are rinsed with potable water. no residue is left on the final product after the use of the substance. the efficacy of the rinsing is monitored to ensure the absence of residues. by setting these conditions, we ensure that the use of amt substances will not make up for poor hygiene conditions, and that no residues are left in the end product. i would also like to stress that the use of amt only reduces the number of bacteria and does not replace good hygiene practices as a core requirement, equally applicable in third countries as in europe and applicable from the flocks to the processing of poultry carcases. we will maintain a holistic approach to the reduction of salmonella at all stages of the production chain: feed, farms and slaughterhouses. moreover, the proposal provides for appropriate labelling. as with other approved substances such as additives, the consumer has the right to be informed. thus we suggest two alternative descriptions. appropriate environmental medication measures on waste water effluent quality standards, as proposed by dg environment. these consist of conditions for collecting and treatment of wastewater in slaughterhouses. last but not least, the proposal foresees a review within two years of the date of application of the authorisation, allowing further collection of data from food business operators regarding the use of the substances so that scientific questions on antimicrobial resistance can be addressed. this review will place the burden of proof on the food operators rather than on the commission’s scientific bodies. that said, when the proposal was presented to the standing committee of the food chain and animal health on 2 june, 26 member states expressed a negative opinion and one abstained. the commission will now transmit the proposal to the council of ministers to decide. you will ask why the commission went ahead with this proposal despite the eu domestic opposition to it. firstly, because the legislative framework foresees the possibility of approving such substances. secondly, because there has been a request for approval which could not be left unanswered, considering also our international commitments. finally, because there are scientific opinions which indicated that we could proceed with the authorisation provided it is flanked by a number of strict conditions, as we did. it was, therefore, for the commission to stand up to its institutional responsibilities and make the proposal. what happens next is part of the decision-making process. in this sense, i have taken good note of your strongly held position. the council will now be asked to pronounce on the text. as you know, if a qualified majority of member states is against this proposal, it will not be adopted, so we will see the end result under the french presidency.

i voted in favour of the weber report, but i must say that i was somewhat reluctant. the left and the far left have been shouting murder and arson against the directive. they have presented it as if the directive were a gross violation of the human rights of illegal immigrants, when in fact the opposite is true. for example, for the first time, the regularisation of illegal immigrants is being put forward by a european directive as an acceptable policy option. the member states are also obliged to provide free legal aid to illegal immigrants who request it. it is unacceptable to derive rights from a situation of illegality. many of the elements of the directive are, moreover, purely optional. that said, the directive allows a number of member states to extend the period of detention of illegals in the context of their expulsion. the ban on refusing entry to the rest of europe for five years after a deportation is also positive. the problem of immigration, however, requires much more far-reaching measures than those supported in this report, but this is a step in the right direction.
mr president, let us look at the figures in a calm manner. in about a century, the world's population has increased about sevenfold, from 1 billion to 7 billion people on earth. this population explosion cannot be allowed to remain without consequences for a prosperous european continent which is also facing a large demographic deficit. the immigration problems we face today are particularly serious, but they are manageable now compared to what we will face in the coming decades. in this respect, the measures provided for in this alleged return directive are in fact futile and certainly inadequate. it will take a lot more than a procession of echternach half-hearted measures to stem the flow of illegal electricity. but because a small signal for a return policy for illegal immigrants is actually better than nothing at all, i voted without enthusiasm for the weber report.
