in writing. - the far too long term of imprisonment is one of the main reasons. the possibility of incarcerating adults and even children for as long as 18 months is going too far. especially since they are also detained if their country of origin does not cooperate by providing the correct documents. except for their own fault, they often cannot return. the fact that illegal residents can be sent back to the country through which they travelled is also not a solution. an entry ban of up to five years is contrary to the idea that people in europe should be protected when they need it. in addition, this measure will give rise to illegal trafficking and human smuggling. the union urgently needs agreements on who can enter, but is now concentrating on agreements on expulsion. the proposal is therefore one-sided and highly unbalanced. such agreements are only meaningful if they provide legal protection to people without valid documents. unfortunately, this compromise does not do that enough. i am not doing the european immigration policy a favour by agreeing to this.
the chairman of the council. − in my opening speech i already mentioned the finding of the competent authorities that the reasons for higher oil prices are structural. that's an important finding. the impact of speculation was mentioned several times in this debate. i do not wish to deny or minimize it, but i would stress that speculation does not in itself lead to higher energy prices unless structural reasons are given for it. and they're yours. they require a longer-term approach. the era of cheap energy, at least fossil energy, is probably over for a while, probably for a long time. and this requires the european union to take the long-term action i have already mentioned, which is essentially two-pronged. first, to increase energy efficiency and thereby reduce our consumption, among other things. and thereby reduce europe's and the european economy's dependence on imported fossil energy. and secondly, diversification, especially the focus on renewable energy sources. and here i agree with mr papadimoulis that more investment is needed in renewable energy, but more investment is being driven by higher fossil fuel energy prices. if it's cheap again, if we make it cheap with these and other unilateral measures, we're going to reduce the incentive to finance, to invest in renewables. while there is a broad consensus on the need for long-term action, we must of course not lose sight of the short-term problems we face. they were mentioned, i would emphasize them again here. the high energy prices are a particular problem for the poorer sections of our union, they are a particular problem for fishermen, which will be discussed in the next item on the agenda, they are a particular problem for transport operators. of course, it makes sense to study and reflect and take appropriate action. of course, we do not exclude the audit of taxation in the european union. i can say that the council expects to receive proposals from the european commission on this issue, on the taxation of energy, shortly. let me end with this thought. i think that this debate is taking place at a very opportune time, the day before the european council, where one of the main points of discussion will be the question of high oil prices.
member of the commission. − madam president, it has been a fascinating debate and i am very sorry that the time has passed so quickly. as my job requires, i have been to the places where oil is being produced: the caspian sea, the barents sea, saudi arabia. i think one mistake we make is thinking that it is easy to produce. it costs billions, and in very difficult environments. costs overrun, there is local damage to the environment and people are unhappy about a lot of projects. so it is very clear that, if we speak about oil issues, we should not be looking for those responsible but should try to find the appropriate responses in the european union. if we believe that this same approach would help everybody, we should follow it. there are no magical measures. energy efficiency is the number one measure. without this, the world will have much higher prices. it is very clear. (interjections from the floor)well, we are doing quite a lot. renewable energy and also alternative energy sources like nuclear are important also to alleviate the problem. new technology investment is needed, and for the sectors it is important to address structural measures, not just subsidies: subsidy is taking from one pocket to put into another pocket. for example, regarding tonight’s fisheries debate, i would ask why fishermen cannot pass increased fuel prices on to the price of fish, because that is the basic question: what happens, what prevents it? this means that we should address sectoral measures. on a global level i believe that it is very clear what we are trying to do, to try to remove the opec policy of capping supplies to the market, of not allowing, in many cases, western companies to come with technology and knowledge to produce oil and thus create better supply. for developed nations we should lead the way in energy efficiency, and my proposal for international partnership has been adopted by the g8. but, as everybody expected oil prices to come down to usd 9 per barrel, the world was late, and now we know that we should follow this policy. concerning consumer protection, i believe that the commission has always been very consistent on this. i have a lot of infringement cases where countries have not even informed the commission, as is their duty, about public service obligations, meaning that legal documents adopted after the commission proposal have all the necessary elements in them, and they should be implemented. i believe that the commission’s response and the council’s response is measured and right. each place in the market has its role. i know that nobody likes to defend speculators, but what is the future market’s role? it indicates where the price could go and allows investments to be made. well, i could say, ‘let’s tax everybody higher’ – we can increase tax to 100% for everybody, but it will mean that we lose the incentive to invest. let us instead make the incentive to invest, let us allow positive ways to investment to be made. so that is what we need. (interjections from the floor) whether it is the companies of saudi arabia, saudi aramco, that we should tax, or gazprom, we cannot tax them because they have national legislation on taxation. concerning european companies, we do not have any company nowadays that has a huge windfall profit because of oil and gas. (interjections from the floor) they also needed to invest in other projects as well, and if you know these companies, you should indicate them. if you know these companies that have such a beautiful life... in writing. the high oil price hurts us all, but certainly the most vulnerable low-income groups are hit hard. the european summit tomorrow must send a strong signal that action for the weakest in society is indeed justified and necessary. a reduction in vat or excise duty is not immediately appropriate, which is understandable, because it may encourage oil producers to charge even higher prices. but maybe there's another way. perhaps there should be a shift in taxes, in particular air tickets becoming a little more expensive (from autumn, for example) through a levy, and the proceeds of this being used to reduce the fuel bill for the low and middle income groups. the surplus can be used to provide premiums for insulating houses. that's the cheapest and most efficient way to do it. investing in renewable energy and energy saving is the task, especially in the medium term. but in the meantime, we must find solutions for those who are in difficulty. europe must not remain deaf to that side.
mr president, commissioner, i would first like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr aylward, on the work he has done on this report. the aim of this report is to revive the sheep and goat sectors in europe. these sectors are in fact threatened because, for several years, their production volume has been steadily decreasing. producers prefer to switch to other types of production because prices have fallen sharply and no longer guarantee them a sufficient income. on the other hand, european consumers, especially young people, are losing interest in these products. we must revive these sectors because meat, milk and cheese come from our land and we must promote them. the maintenance of these sectors is essential for several reasons. firstly, the milk sector, like the meat sector, is a guarantee of tradition and know-how which we must continue to make known to consumers. secondly, these two sectors also provide jobs in the production, processing and sale of these products. it should also be borne in mind that sheep and goat farms are often located in remote, mountainous or island areas. i can speak about this because i was born in sardinia and it is a region i know very well and where the possibilities for conversion are very limited. sheep and goats also play a beneficial role in the preservation of the environment. that is why we must ensure that these farms are maintained and that the market is revived.
in writing. - i fully support this report. every year, a huge amount of dangerous goods, such as chlorine and petrol, are transported inland in the european union. the commission has not yet drawn up any rules on this subject, although there is legislation on the transport of dangerous goods by road and rail. legislation on the transport of dangerous goods by ship is badly needed. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of the community's ships is not compromised. many of these transport operations cross borders. moreover, european rules also benefit entrepreneurs who now have to comply with different national requirements in different member states. inland waterway transport also reduces greenhouse gas emissions. it is therefore very important to harmonize the requirements for the different modes of transport. this makes it more attractive to combine several modes of transport. for example, by taking part of the journey by rail and then transporting the cargo by ship. that's not only efficient, but above all, it's sustainable. i hope therefore that transport companies will actually choose to combine different modes of transport more often.

the rapporteur has in fact already said what this report is about: the european parliament must become, even more than it is today, a sort of representative of the eurocratic unity-mindedness. so let us get rid of the little bit of freedom that the smaller groups have. the european parliament is no longer a representative of the political diversity of our peoples, but a servant of the mandarin of europe. what an undemocratic farce it is that your europe is becoming! the famous amendment no 6 on parliamentary questions is another abridgement of our rights. we also read that these rules are being introduced because the european commission - i quote - has 'complained loudly about the number of these questions and the administrative burden that their answering causes for its departments'. i think, however, that it is rather members of parliament who should complain loudly about the unfortunate way in which many european commissioners have barely answered our written questions, that is, have not answered the question in full and have sometimes refused to give us correct information. we should rather have a procedure for sanctioning commissioners than making it difficult for members of parliament to do the work for which they are paid.

the corbett report introduces a number of measures that have nothing to do with this under the guise of reforms to make parliament more efficient. on the contrary, these are amendments to the rules of procedure which merely restrict the normal work of parliament. the most striking example is the amendment of rule 110 (4) which limits the number of ordinary written questions to three per month. in his explanatory statement, the rapporteur claims that this restriction is to prevent the alleged abuse of the right to ask parliamentary questions. the question of the future of the community is a question of the future of the community. to suggest that this could lead to abuse is in fact an insult to any member of parliament who takes his job seriously. this is a typical example of socialist manipulation, and it is unworthy of this parliament and any parliament!

in writing. with today's adoption of a directive to include air traffic in the european emissions trading scheme, the european union is making further progress in the fight against climate change. the agreement between the european parliament and the council will allow for a gradual and real reduction of greenhouse gases from 2012 onwards for all flights taking off and landing on european soil. i particularly welcome the adoption of two concrete measures which underline that europe is often, and fortunately still, a particularly balanced legislature which takes account of the interests of the 'small' ones: passenger planes, research planes and small airlines will be able to continue flying without worrying about the binding provisions of the directive; the revenue generated by the auctioning of the 15% of co2 emission allowances will go to research and development in the aeronautics sector. because it is clear that if the eu is to be innovative, engineers will also have to be very resourceful in reducing air pollution, while traffic is growing by 5% a year.
in writing. − if the eu is to meet its climate targets of reducing co2 emissions by 20% by 2020, the aviation sector must also be included in an emissions trading system for greenhouse gas emissions. aviation emissions have increased by almost 90 percent in the past year. at the same time, air transport escapes the payment of excise duty on kerosene and vat on air tickets. legislative action is therefore necessary. the report calls for the trading system to be introduced from 2011 for all flights entering european airspace. from 2012, the sector should be allowed to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions by more than 1,5% per year. the proposed level of free auction rights is far too high. i think the airline industry should at least start by auctioning off 25%. in the end, the goal is not trade itself but reducing emissions. parliament also expressed its views on the use to which the proceeds of the auction should be put: it wants to use the money to reduce the burden on environmentally friendly modes of transport and to invest in research into technology for less polluting aircraft. the compromise we are about to reach is far from ideal, but it is a step forward which enables us greens to support the agreement reached.

mr president, there is a famous painting by the belgian painter magritte showing a pipe with the caption: 'this is not a pipe'. even beautifully painted, in fact, this pipe will never allow smoking. it's a bit like the brok report. despite its title, it is not an enlargement strategy. because it is not about strategy or the essential questions that citizens ask themselves. why enlarge the european union? which way? what are the risks or the benefits? what brok is talking about is a method of consolidation, a defensive tactic. to put it simply, enlargement is a contract between europe and the candidate countries. the latter must meet the copenhagen criteria and europe must show its capacity to absorb. but here's where it gets tricky. europe is not ready for further enlargement, because it is trapped in a too narrow treaty of nice from which it cannot escape. the european institutional crisis should therefore result in a halt to enlargement. that is the opinion of many european citizens and, to a certain extent, it could also be my own. but beware, this slogan, all alone, without any ambitious strategy, is dangerous. it opens a royal road to all those who are prepared to reject a new treaty in the name of withdrawal, to reject turkey or even the balkan countries, to all the nationalists who are wary of foreigners who might one day disguise themselves as europeans. for these nationalists, this slogan is just a parade. in fact, they do not want either enlargement or deepening. we must prove to our citizens that the successive enlargements have been a chance for europe, and we must reiterate to them that multiculturalism is a wealth, that immigration is our democratic future. the institutional crisis must be overcome. it does not signal a victory for the eurosceptics or the left. but it reveals a lack of glory, a weakness that can afflict anyone. the brok report handles this impotence well, intelligently, and i commend it for that, but unfortunately it does not go any further.

in writing. the french presidency is focusing on the right priorities: but let me focus on one aspect that is less in the spotlight, but is extremely important for our youth and for the whole sporting sector in europe. the french presidency would like to adopt the so-called 6+5 principle in sport, i.e. a limitation on the number of foreign players. the objectives are fine: the commission has also proposed that the commission should be able to take account of the fact that the commission has already adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. we in parliament agree with that 100%. hence our support for the home grown measure, which pursues the same objectives, albeit more modestly. the question is whether the 6+5 principle is feasible in a european context. it is contrary to the free movement of workers and is applicable only if there is an exception to the european treaty. we're not even close. it is doubtful that the court of justice would ever accept such a thing, even with the new sport article in the lisbon treaty. we want to help parliament find a solution that will benefit european football. we're just asking for a solid solution that doesn't throw football into chaos. a bushman biscuit, no one needs that.
the regulation rightly states that for every person in possession of a visa, the vis should be systematically consulted for biometric checks. it is the best and safest way to verify the authenticity of a visa. it is therefore regrettable that parliament feels called upon to undermine the principle in effect by introducing here a list of situations in which it will be sufficient to check the identification sticker, and not to proceed to a check of the biometric data. it is therefore to be feared that the introduction of these lists will make biometric checks the exception rather than the rule. i also know that it is not possible to carry out biometric checks systematically in all circumstances, but it should be the rule. we cannot afford to be lax and unconcerned in the fight against illegal immigration and the fight against terrorism and crossborder crime.
mr president, this summer, on both sides, there was an attempt to force our hand, with two immediate consequences, apart from this tragic drama: firstly, the almost immediate signing of the missile shield by poland - even if it is directed at iran, that is a problem; secondly, the european union's commitment to finance the reconstruction of georgia, when we know very well that the external relations budget does not allow it, as you said, madam commissioner, and that it is dramatically underfunded and i support you: we will have to find other sources of funding, otherwise we will not be able to cope. so i think we must stop this escalation and my group and i are in favour of clear, even tough relations with russia, particularly on energy issues, on human rights, on international law, but not for a return to the cold war. i also warn against the hasty entry into nato and the european union of countries which do not yet have the necessary guarantees. and i invite the french presidency, which i congratulate for its swift action this summer, to look at the black sea union as it has done with the union for the mediterranean. finally, i say to my colleagues in the east, and especially in the baltic states, we are no longer in 1938, but in 2008 we will not allow history to stutter. (the president cut the speaker off)
mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as a member and chairman of the committee on civil liberties, i shall confine myself to the proposal for an anti-discrimination directive. i would like to begin by congratulating you, commissioner, on finally getting the commission to adopt a proposal for a directive aimed at combating discrimination on grounds of religion, belief, disability, age and sexual orientation. until recently, as you know, we were afraid - we were worried and we made it known - that you would limit yourself to a mini-directive, which would have only dealt with discrimination on the grounds of age and disability. i am not surprised to say that i regret that our parliament is only consulted on such a fundamental matter, but i felt that the presidency was open to proposals or suggestions from the european parliament. so we are going to get down to business, commissioner, and i personally see three aspects of the text that we are going to try to improve. first, an effort should be made to clarify certain concepts or the boundaries between certain concepts. i am referring in particular to the area of objectively justified differences in treatment. i am not disputing them, of course, but these objectively justified differences in treatment must not be allowed to turn into discrimination, either directly or indirectly. it happens fast. secondly, the directive must not be emptied of its substance by the untimely use of concepts such as public security or public order, as seems to me to be the case, for example, in a large country in the south of the european union which i will not mention. thirdly, we must make an effort to impose sanctions. when you write in article 4 'member states shall determine the system of penalties applicable to infringements of the national provisions adopted pursuant to this directive', commissioner, you are not going very far. in short, i hope that this directive, which is giving rise to so much hope, will not turn out to be a truly great mini-directive, generous in its principles but very vague in its provisions.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. what concerns me, ladies and gentlemen, is the systematic undermining of european social ambitions during the course of the parliamentary term. i think there are obvious political reasons for this. it is striking how the commission continues to emphasize that social policy must remain primarily a national matter. the social package, commissioner, contains very interesting working papers and communications, but only four legislative initiatives. welcome as you are, but it's a very meager harvest! as if guaranteeing social rights, creating a level playing field in social matters, were no longer a european task in a europe of 27. together with the etuc and the social organisations, we expect, in addition to this social package, at least strengthened legislation on posting, which safeguards social rights, better legislation to eliminate the gender pay gap, a framework law for the protection of social services and binding commitments to the fight against poverty. what we need is a real social pact with a coherent vision and a serious commitment to a social europe, to at least show the citizens that europe is more than just a market. i hope, mr bertrand, that we can count on the support of the french presidency in this struggle for the time being.
i am of course not opposed, mr president, as i suppose most people are, to improvements to the schengen visa information system, but what is presented in this report is really too absurd to be put into words. the visa system would be truly relaxed because there are waiting times at the borders! anyone who has travelled the world knows that waiting lists exist and that they are sometimes necessary. i wonder what kind of evaluation would be possible to make border guards decide when there is danger and when there is not. who knows where the terrorist and other dangers may come from? i also object to the fact that we are once again looking for facts. the europeanisation of our border controls has been carried out unplanned, unprepared and under pressure from ideological zealots who consider the safety of citizens to be secondary to the great ideal of the new european soviet union.
the rapporteur, mrs lambert, is certainly right when she says that the dublin objectives have not been achieved in any case in terms of asylum shopping. - that's right. - what? it is also right to say that the system inevitably places a disproportionate burden on the member states bordering the european union. that's right, too. so it is a good thing that we are calling for support for these countries. on the other hand, i do miss some important points in this report and i do not agree at all with most of the assumptions and objectives of the rapporteur. let me give you an example. the commission's evaluation already stated that the dublin system leads to the hiding of tens of thousands of asylum seekers, and yet the rapporteur is advocating against detention measures. it can't be more serious. the close cooperation between the european member states in the field of asylum can bear fruit, but this will require the overthrow of a whole series of politically correct dogmas which this report is full of.
mr president, it is impossible to sum up all the problems with the lambert report in one minute, so i will confine myself to a few elements. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide network of information centres for the protection of children. that sounds good, but in fact it is an open invitation to more identity fraud. the report also states that the definition of family member is too narrow, which again is an open invitation to more abuses. in africa, for example, almost everyone is related to everyone else, and if we have to take that into account, we better open all the gates immediately. the report also opposes access to the eurodac database for the police and judicial services of the member states because, i quote, 'this would increase the risk of stigmatising asylum seekers'. this is a ridiculous consideration, all the more so because eurodac could contain a wealth of information in the fight against illegal immigration, international crime and terrorism.
i have not voted against the kauffmann report, mr president, although i am not at all convinced that the europeanisation of our judiciary or the creation of a european public prosecutor's office is the only way to improve the functioning of the police and the judiciary and to improve the punishment of even crossborder crime. on the contrary. i do, however, support the need for very far-reaching and improved cooperation between all the sovereign european security services and in that sense i can largely approve a number of recommendations, improvements to the kauffmann report, improvements to the european judicial network. but this must not lead to a european judiciary that is alien to the world, overpaid and arrogant, as we have seen in recent months, with a spirit of interference that goes far beyond the necessary cooperation between sovereign member states. i therefore abstained on the final vote on the kaufmann report for that second reason.
in writing. for the first time in history, brussels wants to throw out the window what the french and dutch people rejected in a referendum in 2005 and the irish in june 2008: the creation of a single european prosecutor's office. the temptation is too great for our european sorcerer apprentices. at all costs, the objections, refusals and legitimate resistance of the peoples of europe must be overcome and ignored in order to forcefully bring all the questions of justice, security and immigration under the community umbrella. europe is wrong. the necessary cooperation between states in judicial, police or criminal matters must not be subject to a supranational legal order established in spite of all the existing disparities between the legal systems and traditions of the member states. we do not want this supranational legal order which would go against our own principles and values.
in writing. contrary to what it wants to show, europe has not made progress in the area of freedom, security and justice. on the contrary, the abolition of checks at national borders has, since the criminal schengen agreements came into force, led to an exponential increase in organized crime and trafficking of all kinds. the european union, a real witch's apprentice in security matters, with its methods which all too often prove dangerous for the security of states and their peoples, has imposed on us this space of insecurity and the absence of freedom and justice. the schengen borders code will be of no use because the very foundations of the schengen agreements are bad and should be rejected. common security will only exist if each state regains full sovereignty in the management of its borders and its migration policy. the paroxysm of aberration is definitely reached when it comes to transferring ever more responsibilities to this union which is already paralysed.
in writing. it is ironic that for the first time a european parliament report describes the mass arrivals of immigrants in the territory of a member state of the union as a 'burden'. would immigration no longer be an opportunity, an asset for all the peoples of europe? the absurdity of the obligation to take in asylum seekers and the absolute respect for the principle of non-refoulement are not in any way called into question. the only weaknesses of the dublin system for determining the member state responsible for examining an asylum application are highlighted. this is evident in view of the constant increase in migration flows to countries often located on the southern periphery of the union. this report once again proposes a wrong solution to the technical and human problems associated with the flood of migrants. it is not a common asylum system, which is necessarily ineffective in an ever-expanding territory and with permeable borders, that should be implemented. on the contrary, the member states must be left to decide for themselves on migration and the management of their borders.
in writing. − the dublin ii agreements are based on the political fiction that there is mutual trust between the 27 member states in dealing with asylum issues and that all member states assume their responsibilities in the same high-level way. i myself have investigated the reception of chechen refugees in poland because a number of chechen refugees were returned to poland from belgium on the basis of dublin. the protest was very large. hence the need for an independent investigation. the footage of it is on my website, by the way. as long as there is no adequate and consistent level of protection in all 27 member states, i believe that dublin ii is little more than a political fiction and creates a gross injustice. i have seen firsthand in poland that the principles of the dublin system are not a reality. the quality of reception, the reception of children and the failure to provide education, the hygienic conditions under which refugees must stay, the lack of health care: it is also a number of cases which vary considerably from one member state to another. the lambert report puts the finger on the sore spot, proceeds from a correct analysis and offers solutions in many areas. it deserves our full support.
in writing. the new telecom law we are discussing today has far-reaching implications for all of us as internet and telecom users. there will be better protection of our privacy on the internet. personal information stored on the computer or transmitted via the internet (including your surfing profile!) may not be used (abused) unless you have given your express prior consent. cooperation between the content industry (music and film in particular) and telecom operators is encouraged to tackle the problem of piracy (illegal downloading). it is important that consumers are well informed about what is and is not allowed on the net, but in no case should access to the internet be denied. phone numbers will be easier to take with you if you change operators. the transmission of a number may take up to 1 day, except in the case of measures to prevent malicious intent. i hope that we can reach an agreement on this quickly and that subscribers can benefit from these improvements as soon as possible.
i voted against the garcia pérez report, although i am convinced that men and women are naturally equal and that they should receive equal pay for equal work. we are still far too often forgetting that equality between men and women is one of the fundamental achievements of present-day europe, of the european world and of the western world, and that it is not shared at all in a number of other regions of the world. we must never forget that. but that is only one aspect of this report. the report also contains a whole host of other points with which i fundamentally disagree, such as support for the perpetual electoral quotas for women. as if women were an immature species that could not achieve positions on their own strength and with their own abilities. and the eternal support for abortion: i wonder what this will do in this report. for all these reasons and many others, i have voted against the garcia pérez report.
if i were to sum up, mr president, why i voted against the svensson report, i could simply say that i think it is utter nonsense, utter nonsense. it is yet another report in which this parliament, which is supposed to defend the freedom of european citizens, calls for restrictions on freedom and for censorship. several of the provisions in the svensson report, such as paragraph 14 on censorship, seem to come directly from fahrenheit 451, the book that outlines a world in which books are banned and critical thinking is suppressed. i am very critical of this european parliament anyway. but it must also be a little careful not to make a hopeless fool of itself and become a sort of clone of the supreme soviet.
i would like to congratulate mrs svensson. her report is one of the most inflammatory, interventionist and politically correct texts of the whole legislature. it seems as if she is convinced that advertising and marketing are one big conspiracy to give rise to discrimination on grounds of sex from the first years of the child's socialization, which reinforces the perpetuation of lifelong inequality between men and women. i 'm not making this up . the majority of this sentence is taken literally from recital n of the text. the report naturally calls for more regulation and for the creation of bodies specifically to monitor compliance with all these new rules. i'd almost say jobs for the boys if that expression wasn't so horribly gender insensitive. the highlight is paragraph 14 of the text which calls for the removal of so-called gender stereotyping messages from textbooks, toys, video games, the internet and advertising. censorship, that is. i don't know if the term textbooks also refers to literature, but if it does, then we can start right now burning the books of shakespeare on the street.
in writing. there are sometimes happy coincidences, even funny ones. we take the opportunity afforded by this annual report on equality between men and women and the fact that it coincides with the french presidency of the european union to highlight a marginal but distracting element which is, at worst, a lack of tact and, at best, a perfect application of the principle of equality between men and women consisting in no longer differentiating between them. a few days ago, on the occasion of the beginning of mr sarkozy's much-publicized presidency, gifts were given to members of the european parliament. among the gifts in the briefcase was a tie. of the 785 meps, nearly a third are women. did they not have the right, too, to a little personalized gift, or are we to conclude that women must also wear the tie? however, in the age of great speeches on the square and the role of women in political life, it seems that mufflery still often prevails over chivalry.

of certain provisions of the lisbon treaty. that convention, as we all know, was rejected in a democratic referendum in ireland, and it was rejected only in ireland because in no other country do on behalf of the pse group. i shall not mention the text which i was going to read to you here on behalf of my group because i believe that we are in the midst of a particularly important debate. the failure of this millennium development goal, this fifth objective, which is fundamental because it concerns women and their suffering in pregnancy, calls for abandoning the extremely hypocritical attitude and analysis we have here at european level, when we know, when we see and when we can testify to the situation in africa, on the ground, in the villages, in the countryside and in the bush. and what our colleague kaczmarek has just said has made me a little angry, which is why i have given up my paper. for here we are not talking about the claim that abortion is a miracle cure for all the problems of women who are about to give birth. no, i'm not going to. we are talking about devoting the necessary resources to ensuring that these women can have everything they need: education, proper family planning, contraception and, where necessary, voluntary termination of pregnancy under good conditions. but in this chamber it is extremely difficult to say clearly because some of us, in the name of a certain morality, sometimes even in the name of a certain obscurantism, continue to deny that we are taking real measures, effective measures, for the benefit of these women in the countries concerned.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is true that maternal mortality illustrates the most poignant inequality of women in the north and the south. it is clear that some colleagues in this hemisphere have still not understood this. mr casini, every year 50 million women become pregnant unwanted because they do not have access to contraceptives. 42 million of these women have unsafe abortions and 80,000 of them die. those are the hard facts. the vast majority of these women live in sub-saharan africa, so the west has absolutely no reason to lecture these women. that's a shame, because maternal deaths are perfectly preventable, if only women had access to health care and sexual and reproductive health. according to the world health organization, it takes 34 euros per person per year to provide basic health care. it is possible. if the european union, in addition to the commitment of the developing countries themselves, also spent 15% of development aid on health care, including sexual and reproductive health. but that's where the problem is. the member states' investments in health care have been declining steadily in recent years. family planning budgets have been cut almost in half since 1994. the european development fund also spends just 4% on health care, compared with 30% on infrastructure and budget support. it is therefore clearly time that we not only get words from the council and not only promises from the commission, but also clear projects to link, for example, budget support to clear results in the area of millennium development goal 5 and saving women's lives in africa.

the rapporteur. - madam president, madam secretary of state - thank you for your apparent haste to get to this debate on time - commissioner dimas, colleagues, health and the environment do not always go hand in hand, especially at the beginning of the 21st century. citizens are exposed to pollution in various forms, often interrelated, whether they live in the city or in the countryside, on the sea or in the mountains. it is therefore no coincidence that, according to the latest figures provided by eurostat, six out of ten european citizens think it is very likely or relatively likely that environmental pollution is harmful to their health and also, importantly, that the european union is not doing enough in this area and that is the whole point of our debate this morning. i would first of all like to thank my colleagues and in particular the fictitious rapporteurs on this report, mrs ferreira, professor trakatelllis, mrs breyer, mrs belohorská and mrs de brún, for the excellent collaboration we have had since the beginning of work on this issue, and that goes back to 2003. it was in 2003 that the european commission launched the scale initiative, which focused on children's health, and then, of course, the following year, the launch of this action plan, which runs until 2010. we considered this initiative to be insufficient, since in february 2005 the parliament's plenary adopted a rather critical resolution, which i must say, based on a simple observation, that an action plan, in essence, cannot be set as the sole objective of more data, more research, even though these are essential, of course. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. three years later, where are we in reducing pollution-related disease? i think that the community level is not very far off, and i shall now turn to the mid-term evaluation itself and its context. the european union, it has been said, has had a number of successes in combating various forms of pollution. among others - it is impossible to list them all here - the new legislation on ambient air quality - which owes much to your energy, commissioner - the pesticides package, which is about to be completed, and then of course reach, with the control of more than 10 000 chemicals and the envisaged substitution for the most problematic of them. i should also like to mention, and this is important, the funding by the commission over the last three years of more than thirty-eight projects on the environment and health in the context of the sixth research framework programme, for a total amount estimated at more than eur 200 million. as for the rest, and in view of the difficulty of assessing this plan, which has a rather bad name, our assessment remains, i would say, mixed. the core of our draft resolution today, therefore, is the reinstatement of the precautionary principle, which is also dear to the secretary of state, i know. i sincerely believe, as she does, that this principle must be given life, given new life, a principle of action rather than a principle of abstention - i say it again and again - and its application also in community policies, as article 174 (2) of our treaty allows and as the constant case law of the court of justice states. on this point again, i think it is important to encourage the reversal of the burden of proof - that is what we are proposing in paragraph 13 of our resolution - for all product legislation, because it is normal, it is obvious that the burden of proving the safety of a marketed product lies with the producer and the importer. i would add that this is what most consumers wrongly believe. the second and not least concern, which is covered extensively in our report in paragraphs 23 to 25, is the issue of climate change. we have been working on this crucial issue in close collaboration with who experts. the phenomenon most often described by these experts is the increase in the intensity and frequency of heat waves. it is hard to forget that after the heat wave of the summer of 2003, more than 70 000 additional deaths were recorded in some ten european countries. the systematic introduction of preventive measures - reduction of exposure to heat, warning system, assistance, of course, to the elderly - seems to us indispensable. i would also like to point out that the rise in temperatures is also the appearance of certain viruses, chikungunya for example, in italy in 2007 - anything but an epiphenomenon, according to experts, but the precursor, perhaps, of many pandemics in europe. it is clear that this also requires a response that is up to the challenge and, at a minimum, regular coordination between the commission, the stockholm centre for disease prevention and control and the european capitals. i would like to conclude my speech on what constituted, in the health field, the soap opera, the saga of the summer of 2008, in any case in a whole series of countries - france, belgium, and not only - i want to talk about this avalanche of information, articles, research, mostly contradictory, on the proven or unproven dangers of the mobile phone on health, especially on that of the most vulnerable, especially children. and the very, perhaps overly media-savvy david servan-schreiber was not the first to sound the alarm. what we find in paragraphs 21 and 22 of our resolution is simple: this increase in studies tends to show a health impact of electromagnetic fields on human health, exposure thresholds which have not been changed, it should be remembered, since 1999 and which are therefore still the official standard of the european union and the lack of consensus among researchers on the health hazard or not of gsm. scientific uncertainty has its bright days ahead. there is a time when politics must decide and that is what we are doing in our resolution before you today.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i would like to congratulate each and every one of us here on the quality of this debate. i think we are at the heart of the concerns of europeans, at the heart of the europe of the people. it's the most important thing. i thank each of my colleagues for the many comments that have been made and also for the proposals, most of which are very ambitious - i will not summarize them, they were made by the secretary of state and commissioner dimas. i should like to return very briefly to the question of electromagnetic fields. mrs kosciusko-morizet mentioned the interphone study, but we are at the heart of the problem: the study is not published in its full results precisely because, according to some, they are contradictory, whereas, for example, the experts working on this study in israel have just highlighted the link between exposure to gsm waves and the development of parotid cancer. so we're actually waiting for proven evidence to act. as i said earlier, when scientific uncertainty persists, politics must decide. i would like to conclude by recalling our amendment no 1 in plenary, which i ask you to support, an amendment which is proposed by most of the groups in this parliament. it is indeed a matter of confirming that when technologies evolve and change, and this is the case in this area, the exposure thresholds must also be changed, otherwise i would say that there is no consumer assistance at risk and i sincerely hope that the french presidency will support this proposal to amend the 1999 recommendation.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, security and defence policy, on the situation in the middle east. on the contrary, this parliament has always systematically taken sides with the palestinians and against the israelis. it is not enough for this parliament that tens of millions of euros of european taxpayers' money disappear every year into the bottomless, corrupt and anti-western pits of the palestinian territories. it is not obvious enough to this parliament that ngos which openly - i stress this - openly approve and condone acts of terrorism are being sponsored again with millions of european taxpayers' money. now this parliament is also literally calling in a resolution for the release of convicted terrorists. this attitude may well be politically correct. i think she's gonna break us all up again.
i also voted against the resolution on the palestinian prisoners in israel because it at least gives the impression - and i am speaking in a friendly manner - that we as a european parliament are not really serious about condemning terrorism. the resolution calls for the release of people who have engaged in terrorist activities. at least one of them is responsible for the deaths of several israeli civilians. the adoption of the resolution is therefore not good for the credibility of parliament, but much worse, it undermines the fight against terrorism in general.
mr president, i voted in the flautre report on amendments nos 4 and 5, which were not adopted and which concerned israel. i would like to explain this: these amendments did not concern sanctions against israel, they dealt - in particular amendment no 5 - with israel's violations of international law, which are well documented. i would like to say that i am against sanctions in general, whether they are against the palestinian people or against israel. what i regret, however, is that this amendment, which spoke of initiatives to be taken with regard to the state of israel, and not of sanctions, was not adopted. if we abandon the idea that we, the european union, must take initiatives to prevent human rights violations, we betray our democratic model. i would also like to say that in saying this we are not saying anything against the jewish people we love and we reject any anti-semitism. we are not saying anything against the state of israel, whose existence and security we want, but we are rebelling against those inside israel who undermine the democracy of that state, which is something very different. and we support all israeli ngos that work for human rights and international law.
i have already had the opportunity to point out in the debate yesterday that the flautre report on the european union's human rights policy is actually a fairly balanced and good document. but what i miss in the report is a clear reference to the problem and danger of islamization in europe and in the world. this is an undeniable islamization which is putting at risk a number of very fundamental european and western values and fundamental rights and human rights. i am thinking first of all of the important separation of church and state and, above all, of the equality of men and women. the islamic countries themselves are also being spared far too much in this report, although in some of these so-called developed and often very rich countries, oil states such as saudi arabia, conditions are unacceptable, ranging from actual slave trade and slave labour to particularly extreme and degrading discrimination against women. that should certainly be improved in a future report.
i voted against this resolution, not only because i am actually opposed to the pro-abortion propaganda contained in this resolution, but also because i think that the attitude of parliament in general on this issue is actually rather hypocritical. on the one hand, parliament rightly says that everything must be done to achieve a large reduction in maternal mortality in developing countries, but on the other hand parliament also continues to advocate for ever greater, ever more far-reaching legal immigration and for the european commission's proposals on the so-called blue card. it is precisely this immigration policy that is leading to a huge brain drain from the developing countries to the western countries and it is precisely this policy that is depriving the developing countries of the best people they need, including in health care, including doctors and nurses, who are needed there in africa much more than in the west. i actually refuse to participate in such a hypocritical attitude.
author. - mr president, commissioner, we can consider the coup d'état in mauritania to be a real tragedy. as it does in many countries, the european union has invested heavily in the democratization blunders or mistakes, it was up to the mauritanian people, the parliament, the elected representatives of the nation to react, to criticize, to sanction. it was not, of course, the duty of the army, the gendarmerie or any police force to interfere in any way with matters which were exclusively political. we therefore call on the new strongmen of mauritania to return to the mauritanian people the power they have stolen from them. we ask them to allow the elected president to resume his duties as soon as possible, even if he suffers all the criticism, as long as they express themselves in a democratic manner and in respect of the aspirations of the mauritanian people once again held hostage by the will of a minority. and i would like to say, mr president, that i had the honour of chairing the parliamentary observer mission of our parliament in mauritania and i am naturally particularly moved by what is happening, and i end here, with marie anne isler béguin who will speak and who has led the european union observer mission. we deeply regret it because what we had achieved there was really the will of an entire people that was expressed throughout one of those years and what has now happened is dramatic.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, security and defence policy, on the situation in the middle east. it's a never ending story, a saga that goes on forever. it is no exaggeration to say that this house has probably condemned dozens of coups d'état in africa in recent years. in a majority of african countries, arbitrariness continues, and the same rulers have often ruled for decades. billions of dollars in development aid have not changed that. the potentates often stay where they are and we too often roll out the red carpet. this is pessimistic. the elections, which were monitored by this parliament, were conducted in an orderly manner, as has already been stated here. but events this summer in mauritania have once again proved that elections alone will not be enough to ensure that democratic values are established in africa in a lasting way. the lessons we have to learn today are that europe must have the courage to make economic aid and development aid dependent on good governance and democracy, because it is the africans themselves who will ultimately benefit from this. but the union is not yet prepared to take that position. verbal condemnation of the coup in mauritania is not enough if the european union does not at the same time take concrete and tangible sanctions to isolate the junta.
mr president, we should not be under any illusions. the theocracy in tehran will no doubt have only contempt for democracy in europe. but it is good that this parliament is once again condemning the executions in iran in clear terms. the execution of minors is not only contrary to international law, it is downright barbaric and it speaks volumes about the ruthless nature of the regime that has been sweeping tehran for decades. it may also be a warning to the naïve who think that iran can be dealt with with velvet gloves. i am not sure that this resolution is right. for it is rightly noted that iran executes more people than any other country in the world, except china. what should have been added, however, is that since january 2005 only saudi arabia, sudan, yemen and pakistan have sentenced and executed minors. it is no coincidence that islamic countries are where the sharia is applied to the letter and the spirit. maybe it's politically incorrect to say it, but the facts speak for themselves. these practices are further proof that this islam, which has not yet been enlightened, is incompatible with our western values.
author. - mr president, i would simply like to confirm that we will not support the amendment tabled here by our colleague, but that we have tabled an oral amendment to article 9 which goes along with what our colleague has said and which we of course support.
i would ask you, mr president, to make a formal protest to the mayor of cologne for depriving last weekend members of the european parliament and other elected representatives of the right to express themselves peacefully and to attend a congress on an important social and political problem, the islamization of europe. left-wing extremists have physically attacked people, set fire to city furniture, and paralyzed the entire downtown area to prevent people from attending a peaceful convention. the mayor, mr schramme, not only refused to condemn this violence and intimidation, he even approved it and presented it as if it were the people of cologne who had spontaneously opposed the organization of a congress. several people have been injured and it is a shame that such conditions can still be found today in a country which calls itself democratic, that people are violently denied the right to speak.
i think that the report, as voted by the committee on economic and monetary affairs, is a good report and that we should be grateful to mr rasmussen, in particular, for having insisted for many months on the need for greater community action in the areas we are discussing this evening. nevertheless, my group has tabled a number of amendments which - in a peculiar way - are not written by my group but by mr rasmussen himself. in other words, a number of important points, including the creation of a supervisory authority at european union level, the european registration and approval of management companies and fund managers - amendments nos 6 and 7 - and the fixing of a debt limit for private equity funds - amendment no 9 - were proposals put forward by mr rasmussen, which were unfortunately withdrawn because, in his view, it was necessary - and this can be understood in some respects - to obtain a majority in this parliament, that is to say a majority with the epp and the liberals. i believe that the reason for the withdrawal of amendments, points which were in these working papers and which we consider interesting and which we believe deserve to be defended, is that in this house, in the council and certainly in the commission, there are two schools of thought: the first is that the current regulations are sufficient, at national and european level, and that, for the rest, self-regulation of the sector should be a solution to any problems, and the second is that, like mr rasmussen and others, has been insisting for many years that the system in place to monitor the whole financial markets is inadequate. i don't think, mr. mccreevy, that we haven't done anything up to now because we couldn't foresee it or because we didn't know. i think there were a lot of authoritative voices, particularly within the financial stability forum, who for several years had been drawing the attention of the public authorities to the kind of risks we were facing. you, in particular, and the barroso commission as a whole, have advocated a laissez-faire approach, considering that the current system is sufficient. the request made by parliament is clearly to change your shoulder gun. i don't know if you'll be able to do it, but i think it's necessary. i would like to stress three points which have already been mentioned but which are of particular concern to environmentalists. as regards the climate/energy package to be adopted, we know that its counterpart is a level of long-term investment, with a rate of return, a rate of return on capital, relatively moderate to meet the needs of investment. equity funds or hedge funds are not the right instruments and we are waiting for proposals from the european banking and monetary authorities to revive old proposals, i am thinking of jacques delors's in the early 1990s, and the renewed role of the european investment bank to provide long-term investment at moderate rates of return. it is difficult to stabilise the financial system - the rasmussen report alludes to this - if we do not take a firm stand against offshore havens and tax havens - on this point too, the barroso commission is very much behind in this proposal. finally, in terms of corporate governance - other colleagues have alluded to this - we should look a little more closely at how the remuneration policies of the managers of these funds are conducted. they are unacceptable and they are a threat to the economy as a whole.
president, commissioner, people are worried about what is happening to their savings, but you seem to care little about it. workers were encouraged to take out-of-court pensions, which were said to be safer and more efficient than government pensions. well, their money is in the pension funds today, but they're not sure of anything today. what is happening now is a mark on the accounts of those who believe in the unfettered free market mechanism. and you're such a free-market person. we must not make too many arrangements where there is a possibility of foreseeing them, you said in this parliament last year and you still say the same thing today. well, it's never too late to change your mind. because what else do you think needs to happen to act preemptively? the consequences of failure to act in time will be felt for years to come. so prevention is better than cure.
mr president, i have read the 2007 report of the committee on petitions with mixed feelings. positive feelings in the first place, because it goes without saying that our citizens have a right to a correct application of all laws. i am of course not opposed to citizens turning to any body, and certainly not to the european parliament, to enforce the correct application of these laws when the national or local authorities refuse to do so. the 2007 report lists a very large number of examples of cases in which the committee on petitions took very justified action. but i have mixed feelings, because on the other hand i am very apprehensive and anxious about the increasing interference of european regulation, about the increasingly european meddling and greed in matters which, in my opinion, are absolutely within the scope of subsidiarity and should actually remain within the competence of the member states. in my own country, flanders, for example, we have seen in recent years an increasing amount of european interference in matters of vital importance to us. i am thinking in particular of the example of the defence of our dutch language, our culture and our identity in our capital, brussels, and in the flemish region around brussels. we find that in such cases eurocrats with little knowledge of the facts and with very general principles come to lecture us. that is a particularly sensitive matter for us and we cannot accept it. i also note that the report makes repeated references to the procedures laid down in the treaty of lisbon. and yet i must point out again that this treaty of lisbon is politically and legally dead after the vote in ireland. we in the committee on petitions, perhaps more than any other committee, must respect the legal reality and the democratic voice of our citizens, in this case the irish people, who have put the treaty of lisbon into the dustbin.
we are very concerned about media pluralism and we therefore support the broad principles of the report. it is a pity that we have not been able to reach agreement on all the points. we fully support the need for the public service broadcaster to be given the space to play its role in a digital, interactive media environment, in balance, of course, with the commercial broadcasters. open discussion on weblogs should be encouraged. weblogs and other so-called 'user-generated content' are contributing in a fresh way to a diverse media landscape and are playing an increasingly important role in news reporting. however, the quality of a blog depends on its author and not all bloggers have the same honorable intentions. so that weblogs do not become anonymous sources of slander, we need to think about how we deal with, for example, privacy violations or right of reply. but we as a parliament must not be too patronizing.
mr president, from time to time we hear that the fight against terrorism would lead to the undermining of our freedoms. but this is a false contradiction. freedom of expression, freedom of the press and the right to privacy are indeed fundamental features of our western society. but it is precisely open societies - and this was mentioned by the previous speaker - that are today threatened by an islamic extremism which calls for terror against these values. the measures in this report are a step, but only a step in the right direction. the member states have not only the duty to protect their citizens from terrorism, but also the right to take all measures to safeguard public order. i would like to make a marginal remark, however, because italy is often mentioned in the margins of this debate today. however, the italian government has every right to fight illegal immigration and crime in any way it deems necessary, if this is justified by objective and legitimate factors. and the scandalous hearing in rome last week, in which some far-left members of this parliament accused the italian carabinieri of torturing roma children, is an insult to the italian people and unworthy of this parliament. i hope that the president of this parliament will therefore apologize to the italian government on behalf of all of us.
mr president, is it any wonder that some member states are more than tired of giving aid, as this report literally points out? i don't think it was. more and more member states and other donors are tired of continuing to pump money into all kinds of corrupt regimes that really do not care about good governance or the well-being of their own citizens. about a year ago, we were told by an unsuspecting source, the aid organization oxfam, that the wars in africa have already cost about as much as the hundreds of billions in development aid that the continent has received in recent years. it is time for africa to take some real steps first on democracy, good governance and the fight against corruption. only then can we talk about highly targeted development aid. the fact that we are talking about a budget for the development of the community, and that we are talking about a budget for the development of the community, is a very serious matter.
mr president, it is a cliché, the young are our future. and so i will be the first to admit that the quality of our teachers and of teacher training is extremely important. the question, of course, is: is it the european parliament's job to teach the member states lessons on this? is it the role of parliament to decide on the composition of teaching staff at all levels of education in the member states? the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. should all this be made compulsory in teacher training because europe demands it? i think parliament can think what it wants, but it has no power over this. education is a matter for the member states and i believe it should remain so. that is subsidiarity and it must be respected.
i am naturally in favour of an effective fight against terrorism and i believe that this area of the fight against terrorism is one of the areas where there is a real need for very intensive crossborder cooperation in europe. for once, therefore, i disagree, or even disagree with the most eurocritical voices which, in my view, draw the map of national sovereignty too strictly in this area. that said, we should have the courage, also in this report for example, to speak a little clearer language. terrorism in europe is extreme left-wing and/or islamic. the incitement to terrorism is also that, and it is not least in some of those mosques which are not controlled by anyone or anything and which are mushrooming in europe today. that is the core of the problem in europe for the 21st century. islam is incompatible with our western values and freedoms and i fear that our open door and open border policies will break us down even more acutely in the future.
i voted in favour of the lefrançois report, although it is not a perfect report. but at least attention is being paid to the problem of islamists calling for violence and jihad. we all know that many mosques are hotbeds of fundamentalism, where young people are recruited into terrorist organizations and where calls are made daily for a holy war against our european values. it is high time that the end of the game was announced and that the accomplices of terrorist acts were also dealt with harshly.
mr president, this parliament, after having already done so in committee, decided by a large majority that racial or ethnic origin and a number of other parameters should not be taken into account in the processing of personal data under any circumstances. the original article 7 of the council proposal was, in my view, cautious and balanced, but the political correctness of this parliament is of course legendary and it has therefore amended this article. we are therefore on the wrong track as a parliament. not only crime prevention, but even any form of good public administration needs correct basic information. and the ethnic or national origin of people can be particularly significant in this regard and has nothing to do with racism or discrimination. i am surprised, time and again, that the same members who call in a stalinist manner for a ban on speech or even imprisonment or loss of parliamentary immunity for right-wing dissidents, show such a cold-blooded fear when it comes to the very ordinary processing of data, especially in the context of the fight against terrorism.
the green group has already used the votes on the hammerstein report to vote on the location of the european parliament in what is known as a 'sneak vote', a little bit in secret, although this had nothing to do with the report as such. i should like to make it clear that i voted in favour of the green amendment because i also believe that the travelling theatre 'european parliament' is already collecting enough taxpayers' money without having to add to it the monthly movement of people from brussels to strasbourg. i therefore also call for - and i voted for - one seat and one place of work in europe. i would just add, for the sake of clarity, that i think the debate should be open and that for me that one seat should not necessarily be brussels. the presence of the european institutions in this city and in this region also entails social, political and human costs which must be discussed and which must not be underestimated.
in writing. - as a former teacher at a so-called multicultural problem school in antwerp, i can only applaud the rapporteur's concern about the quality of education in the eu. however, it is up to the member states individually, and not the eu, to see what can be done about the quality of education. if there is one area in which the principle of subsidiarity and respect for the diversity of different cultures must apply, it is certainly education. education should not be multicultural as this report claims. education just has to be good and have quality. in flanders, for example, i have too often seen that it is the multicultural concentration schools that are reducing the quality. ideological blindfolds will not help. i therefore voted against this report.
in writing. - the financial world is shaking at its foundations. while ordinary american taxpayers pay the price of a bailout ($700 billion), the perpetrators go free. the european parliament has had an instrument in its hands with the rasmussen report to do something about better control of certain aspects of the financial sector: hedge funds and private equity. with the urgency of the crisis, we had an opportunity to call on the european commission to impose strict regulations. the greens therefore tabled several amendments to the report. since our amendments were not accepted by parliament, we voted against. there will be no european authority to oversee the financial sector, no european regulation for the registration and supervision of hedge funds, no limit on excessive investment by private investment firms. the european parliament has missed its chance, precisely in a week when the capitalist system is about to implode. we greens are sending a signal that we will continue to push for a drastic restriction of a free market which seems to have as its sole purpose speculation in order to make a profit as quickly as possible for a small group of people. this is socioeconomically irresponsible.
in writing. the lefrançois report proposes a number of good measures to make the fight against terrorism in the eu more efficient and more coordinated. that's why i voted for it. the eta attacks a few days ago and the murderous attack in islamabad prove that we can never be too vigilant and efficient in this fight. crossborder cooperation in the fight against terrorism - and that is today mainly islamic terrorism - is absolutely necessary if we are to achieve results. but we must not turn a blind eye to the mistakes of the past. the so-called schengen area has for years provided potential terrorists and criminals with an ideal framework for carrying out their criminal plans, often with impunity. europe must urgently reflect on its open borders policy with its disastrous consequences in the field of immigration, crime and islamic extremism. otherwise the framework proposed here will also prove useless.
in writing. - i voted against this report with all my heart. the roure report shows for the umpteenth time how europe is turning a blind eye to political correctness. it is clear that in the fight against crime and terrorism the government has the right to collect as much data as possible from potential suspects, including so-called ethnic data. the rapporteur himself admits that. but why should civil authorities not be able to do the same in other areas of data processing - and with respect for people's privacy - if this ensures good governance? why, for example, should the italian government not be allowed to take fingerprints of illegal immigrants if that is the only method of identifying them? the original council proposal on this subject was sufficiently balanced. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it's so cool to be free.

- i'm the reporter. the question we have to ask ourselves is whether, nine years after the erica accident and almost six years after the prestige accident, we are ready if something like this happens again. that is the question we are faced with. are we all right? do we have enough experts who are free enough to make decisions? can they work fast enough? are all the preparations made so that they can call on other specialists or on helpers to solve the case? is the crew being treated properly in such a case? will there be any compensation? do we know enough about ships passing by? these are the questions we have asked after the erica and also after the prestige. the commission then made proposals very quickly on this subject and we also put some things down on paper which we now have to refine. we have had a first reading, to which the council has responded in a common position, which was very disappointing. but i am very pleased to say that since then we have had informal talks with the council, first with the slovenian presidency and now especially with the french, which have produced very good results. as far as the competent authority is concerned, we are almost there. we have a good text on paper which i would like to defend with my colleagues. there is a body involved in planning, which is permanently present, which has the necessary expertise and which can make independent decisions so that ships are better received. we have also made progress in the field of tracking ships, not only by conventional radar but also by satellite. we have more detailed information about the cargo and we know who's responsible for giving that information. the monitoring also applies to fishing vessels, so that the safety of small vessels is also increased. we'll take precautions in case of ice. so we've made some progress, but we're not quite done yet. what's open? the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud, including the introduction of a new system of penalties for fraud against shipowners, the compensation of shipowners for economic losses in ports and the obligation to notify bunker fuel. because sometimes small amounts of this highly polluting fuel can cause a lot of damage to the environment, as in the case of the accident with the tricolor off the belgian-french coast. there, 180 tons of fuel, very little, did quite a bit of damage. i would like to express my appreciation, mr minister, for the work that we, parliament and the council, have been able to do together on my report and i would encourage parliament and the council to continue along this path. that brings me to the two texts on which we do not have a common position. as rapporteur for one of the texts, i urge that we also obtain a decision from the council of ministers on the two texts that are missing. i know, mr. secretary, that you are working hard on this. but the financial responsibility of shipowners and the role of flag states, these two points we in parliament regard as essential to strengthening safety at sea. the hns convention, for example, must be ratified by all the member states if a proper system of reception of ships is to be established for the various member states and for the union. we, as a parliament, urge you to do everything possible to ensure that the council also takes a decision on these two texts. if not, we have a very serious problem. it is possible that all that we have achieved, as much as i appreciate it, may be considered insufficient by this parliament. i would therefore ask you, mr minister, to continue as you are doing. parliament, and in any case this rapporteur, is behind you. let us continue to work towards the best possible result for maritime safety.
