in writing. - my vote against the so-called telecommunications package (harbour report) was prompted by the loopholes left in the directive which could constitute a possible infringement of our freedoms. for example, member states may allow providers to monitor the activities of individuals on the internet. i hope that when member states implement these new rules, they will not be tempted to filter the content of the internet. this task should be left to the police alone. i understand that infringements of property rights must be addressed via the internet, but that should not mean that the freedom of an individual internet user is compromised. we cannot create a situation similar to that of a postman opening letters to see if the contents are legal. the amendments tabled by the greens to improve this text were voted down, so that i could no longer support this proposal. i would like to vote in favour of the many consumer benefits, but i think it is unacceptable to make internet service providers responsible for the content of the internet. that's not what this legislation was intended to do.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, the disease of sedentary life, the disease of young people too, obesity has been at the heart of our parliamentary work more and more, as we have said, since the beginning of this parliamentary term. each year, more than 400,000 young people are added to this long list of people who will one day experience the yo-yo effect of dieting. europe has therefore done well to take up this problem head-on. it has also developed a clear strategy in this area with the launch, in march 2005, of this european platform which brings together all the actors involved in the agri-food chain, the large retail sector, the medical professions and also consumer associations. it must be acknowledged - and we have heard this before - that it is not easy to convince that europe has more than its say in the fight against obesity. to do this we must begin by clarifying the levels of power, which does not prevent us, and we do, from reminding the member states of their competence and responsibility. i am thinking here of two strong proposals we make in the report: to avoid discrimination and stigmatisation of obese people by officially recognising obesity as a chronic disease - as is the case, for example, in the who and in portugal - to also ensure that all children have access to physical activity and sport at school - two hours a week seems to me to be the minimum - to better finance school canteens to ensure that children are provided with fresh produce, and i welcome the commission's proposal for free distribution of fruit at school, which the commissioner has mentioned. i would say that it is with this kind of simple, practical and common sense action that the citizens will also learn to love europe again. i come now to the report by mr foglietta, whom i also thank for all the efforts he has made. i would like to highlight two proposals. firstly, the choice of an incentive policy - lower prices, tax cuts - rather than a system based on an increased taxation of caloric products, the famous fat tax, which would ultimately penalise european households with modest incomes. the liberal and democratic group, which i represent here, is therefore opposed to amendment no 6 by the greens and prefers to stick to the consequent reduction in vat on fruit and vegetables, as stated in paragraph 28. my group also supports the ban on trans-artificial fatty acids which, as we know, are linked to a significant increase in cardiovascular problems. we therefore also oppose mr blokland's amendments, which seek to relax paragraphs 32, 34 and 35 and to clear, if not to bleach, these hydrogenated fats. our message to manufacturers is clear: show goodwill, innovate, for the sake of consumers' health and also for your own benefit. i would like to conclude by referring, since time is running out, to the first european parliamentary meetings on food and health which i launched in this house a year ago. it's a huge crossroads for all stakeholders, and it's been centered around four priorities: getting active at an early age, eating a varied and balanced diet, treating obesity as a chronic disease, and legislating if necessary. i would say that this is much more than a slogan, it is a real moral duty for the european union, which must take this whole part in the fight against obesity and sedentary lifestyle.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. i am therefore very grateful to you, commissioner, for your initiative, which undoubtedly has many positive aspects, particularly in the areas of quality and safety, patient information, increased european cooperation, ehealth, reference centres and so on. i also agree with you, madam minister, when you say that it is not right to leave patient mobility to the court of justice, but that legislation is really in place here. i also share the view of mr bowis and mr lambrecht that we should perhaps think again about a better balance in the area of prior authorization for patient mobility, because that prior authorization is an important instrument of control and planning by the member states. there are also a number of questions, commissioner, about the tariffs that can be applied, about mechanisms that should prevent patient mobility from leading to waiting lists in certain countries. but i am sure, ladies and gentlemen, that these are concerns and questions which we will all be able to resolve in the course of the discussion of this directive. once again, commissioner, thank you for your initiative. madam secretary, we look forward to working with you.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. it responds to a need, to a reality on the ground and we can do something for the people. some colleagues here say that the rich will be the only ones who will be able to seek care abroad. we must avoid that only the wealthiest people have access to care abroad, because they can afford costly procedures through the courts. patients have a right to clarity and legal certainty, and we can work on that now, that is our job. the proposal will bring added value to eu citizens. of course it is not perfect, we will have to make some improvements. i am thinking here of the definitions of 'hospital', 'outpatient care', etc. and the fixing of the specific cases in which prior authorization may still be justified. these are points which we shall certainly include in the debate. i hope, however, that we shall have a serene and pragmatic debate and not an ideological debate. more matters will have to be dealt with by national regulations, but i cannot agree with those who say that this proposal would affect the competence of the member states to organise their health care. i think it is very important that this proposal should be discussed now.
in writing. we are still happy that the european parliament kept health services out of the general services directive. health services are a specific sector which requires a specific approach. it is essential that the proposal should assume that, in accordance with settled case law, the organisation and financing of health care is a matter for the member states. this means that on the one hand the mobility of the patient cannot be made an absolute right and on the other hand it cannot be an excuse for not investing in the health system. this principle should also have the effect of enabling member states to charge the foreign patient for the actual cost of treatment. solidarity must be there, but it cannot exclude that there is a difference in treatment between a patient who has already paid social security contributions and taxes in his own country and a foreign patient who has not. the fact that the directive exists is a positive thing, but anyone who follows the sector will certainly feel that there is still a lot of improvement to be made. the quality, accessibility and affordability of health care on a solidarity basis remain for me the evaluation criteria.
mr president, the resolution we have just voted on was unacceptable to me and certainly to my group for a number of reasons. the main reason, of course, is that i believe that europe needs nothing less than a new wave of immigration, even if it is illegal. - i'm not sure. it is very easy for companies and for the government to keep importing foreigners from non-european countries. this is causing a brain drain from developing countries to europe, which in the long run does not benefit either those developing countries or europe, but rather the opposite. let us finally begin - and i am looking first at the public authorities and the business community - with the assimilation, retraining and integration into the normal work process of the already massive, particularly massive numbers of foreigners who are here and who have not and will never be adapted.
i am glad to hear this parliament say, mr president, that all member states must ensure the pluriform nature of their media and that public service broadcasters certainly have a major role to play in this. - that's right. - what? in a normal society, this is called democracy and freedom of information, first and foremost freedom of information for opposition forces. by these criteria, belgium and even flanders are not a democracy. for example, my political party, a major political party in this country, is systematically and openly discriminated against and boycotted by the flemish public broadcaster, and this on the basis of official guidelines. - why? - why? because our views and proposals are not politically correct or deviate from the prevailing discourse. the former head of the public broadcaster recently publicly admitted that he had been made a baron by the belgian king because of his struggle against discrimination by the opposition party. a paragraph on the treatment of opposition parties who go against the grain would not be wrong in this otherwise not so bad report.
i have abstained on this report. this report rightly exposes some of the pain points of pluralism and media concentration that we have in various member states. as a fleming, i can talk about it. because no state in the european union needs a neutral media ombudsman, for example, to guarantee freedom of expression and pluralism, more than belgium. here in brussels, where the heart of the institutions beats, it is not only the private media but also the public authorities who shamelessly boycott the main opposition party, as my colleague said just now, and who deny the citizens the right to balanced and free information. the freedom of the press charter which the rapporteur is advocating may well make this kind of abuse impossible, otherwise everything will remain a mere window dressing. on the other hand, i wonder why the rapporteur wants to regulate the free medium par excellence, the internet, and in particular bloggers, more strictly, and i am not even talking about the rightful concern for respect for copyright. because it's just those states where there's no real media pluralism that are the most vocal advocates for more control over the internet. this report gives them additional arguments, and that is regrettable.

for the greens/efa group madam president, commissioner, i have just said on behalf of my group that we support the conclusions of the ecofin council. i see that the stock markets are still plunging today, all european markets are falling, and falling sharply. these provisions are therefore insufficient. on the question of responsibility, i can agree with mrs kauppi that there has indeed been a lot of resistance in the council, but it is clear that in the commission, and you in particular, you have never been a fan - to say the least - of more regulation at european level. the whole of your mandate has been dominated by this idea of self-regulation linked to the sector. and that is why we are far behind in european legislation on the subjects we are discussing today. for the report which is to be voted on tomorrow, my group will support all the amendments which have been proposed, in particular by the rapporteur, mrs van den burg, which also seek, in our view, to have more stringent provisions on securitisation and mechanisms which hamper speculative flows, and which make the lamfalussy level 3 committees a bit of the embryo of that european supervision, that european supervisory body which we want. that is the direction we must take and i think that the commission, which has a monopoly on the legislative initiative, has failed in its duty during this parliament. from this point of view, the good words are fine, but you have very, very little time to make up for lost time.

in writing. i voted for the ecris report because behind this obscure acronym is the priority issue of networking national criminal records. make no mistake about it: we are not going to create a super-centralized data bank. each member state will continue to centrise information on its citizens, each national central administration will be the only one with access to the interconnection with the other criminal records in the union. national judicial authorities will therefore not be able to access the european criminal records directory directly: they will have to address their requests for information to the central criminal records directory in their country, which will act as an intermediary. until now, however, there has been no effective functioning of the european criminal record. we had the pilot project, launched in 2006, involving belgium, the czech republic, france, germany, luxembourg and spain, which was joined by others, given its success. we also had the decision on the organisation and content of the exchange of information on criminal records between member states. but the text was missing, which technically organized this electronic interconnection. this is now being done thanks to 'ecris', which shows that new technologies are making the daily work of justice within the union easier.
in writing. - the romagnoli report deserves our support because it represents real progress in the field of inter-judicial cooperation between member states. in the past, there have been too many cases where the criminal records of serious criminals who were in a member state other than their country of origin were not handed over to the public prosecutor's offices. the fournier case in belgium is the saddest example of this. it is good that there should be a harmonized european system of criminal records, provided, of course, that the fundamental rights of everyone are guaranteed. in particular, freedom of expression, which is severely restricted in some member states by so-called anti-racism laws, which deprive freedom, could pose a problem in this area. the criminalisation of the crime of opinion in belgium, for example, goes much further than in countries such as italy and the united kingdom, where freedom of expression is respected.
in writing. - i voted against this report. the european union is not a state and should not be allowed to play with the symbols of a state or nation, such as a national anthem, a flag, etc. it was also the intention of the treaty of lisbon, rejected by the irish, french and dutch, and its copy, the so-called european constitution, to do so. a little respect for the democratic will of the people would be appropriate here if europe is to acquire democratic legitimacy. flags and anthems belong to nations, not to economic alliances between states.
i shall vote against this report tomorrow with full conviction, mr president, because the erasmus mundus programme in its present form is totally unacceptable to me. it is unacceptable because it favours students from outside the eu over european students on an excessively large scale. the figures are what they are. since the programme started in 2004, some 4 150 students from outside the union have participated. the amount of the grant is eur 21 000 for one year of training and eur 42 000 for two years. this appropriation is intended to cover the cost of the training of staff and the purchase of equipment and supplies for the training of teachers and other trainers. the approximately 200 eu students participating in the programme received on average only a grant of eur 3 100 to study outside europe. the total cost of their participation was only eur 620 000. i am not in favour of such discrimination and i am against extending this programme.

in writing. − it has long been clear that the least developed countries (ldcs) are the most affected by global warming, while they have contributed the least to it. their vulnerability will only make them more vulnerable to poverty. i think it is good that the rapporteur, mr wijkman, has emphasized this very strongly. the idea is to create an alliance to combat climate change, but the commission is not providing enough money for this. the cost of climate change could be as high as 80 billion euros. the budget allocated by the commission is eur 60 million: far too little for the mols to prepare for climate change. it's now up to the alliance to find or release more funds. this means that the individual member states of the union must assume their responsibility. they have to make available a much larger amount than they are currently making. the european parliament also proposes to use at least 25% of the eu's revenues from the emissions trading system for the alliance. it seems that the union is beginning to think differently about development cooperation in the light of climate change, and that is a good thing. that is why i support the report.
in writing. - erasmus mundus ii is broadly similar to its predecessor, although it has been significantly modified. important are a more balanced and guaranteed geographical representation in erasmus mundus programmes that can be offered by consortia of universities from at least three european countries, and additional attention to vulnerable groups. the admission criteria for training should be quality-oriented, while respecting equality between men and women and improving access for disadvantaged groups. the principle of equal opportunities and non-discrimination must also be respected by the institutions offering the training when awarding scholarships to european students and students from third countries. erasmus mundus ii should also contribute to the sustainable development of higher education in europe and in third countries, with the commission making every effort to prevent brain drain. we greens will ensure that these changes are put into practice. the evaluation of erasmus mundus should also show that access to erasmus mundus training has improved for vulnerable groups. in these circumstances, the greens/efa group in the european parliament adopted this report.

in writing. the internet is an integral part of our children's daily lives. as people age, the frequency of internet use increases. from the age of 11, young people surf daily and by the age of 15 they are connecting and exchanging on the net several times a day. however, the use of this formidable tool for access to knowledge and as a stepping stone to the cognitive society is not without risks. many children are unwittingly exposed to harmful images or content, such as commercial scams, harassment, pornography, racism, and suicide. one of the reasons for this is the lack of adequate international regulation and cooperation. the european safer internet programme, with a budget of €55 million, aims to raise awareness not only of the dangers of the internet for children but also for parents and teachers. it also aims to encourage the development of filtering systems and to encourage the labelling of internet sites deemed safe for children. i therefore naturally voted in favour of the report by the italian mep roberta angelilli, who advocates the use of the internet in a secure environment, ensuring that the physical and moral integrity of our children is protected as much as possible.
i believe that we must be very careful and think again whether it is wise to offer bosnia and herzegovina the prospect of membership of the european union. as has been said, there are many conditions to be met. it is the evidence itself, but i would also like to mention the problem of the growth of islamic fundamentalism in bosnia. it is becoming increasingly clear that the salafist networks are being built up with financial and logistical support from saudi arabia. these networks are a danger to the peaceful society in the country itself, but also to the security of, in fact, the whole of europe. i would therefore like to join mr belder in his questions. can the council and the commission comment on the measures being taken to prevent the problem of possible terrorist networks from becoming a problem of the european union?

mr president, i did not approve the 2009 draft budget, and in particular the commission budget. - why? - why? i want to make a political statement by not agreeing in any way with the way the commission is working. in general, i regard the commission as an institution which by definition, in terms of its principles, operates undemocratically, since it is in fact a college of purely politically appointed high officials, who, however, behave like a kind of european mandarins who can hardly tolerate control and who cannot be sanctioned either. on the political front, as regards the 2009 budget, i am opposed, first of all, to the commission's continuing efforts to continue mordicus on the doomed path of the accession of muslim and non-european turkey to the european union. i have no intention of endorsing this policy.
i voted against the final vote on the draft general budget for 2009 for the various european institutions, including, of course, parliament. firstly, because i am not convinced that all these european institutions, without exception, are sparingly and responsibly managing the large amounts of taxpayers' money that are spent on them. on the contrary, to be honest. the european institutions, we must be well aware of this, have an image in the eyes of our electorate of fat people, where overpaid and underpaid apparatchiks and parliamentarians constitute a sort of nomenclature on the soviet model, where decisions are often taken over the heads of the citizens and certainly against the will and interests of the citizens. that is the image we have, which is certainly not right for everyone, but which i fear is a basis of truth in a good number of european institutions. i think we have a long way to go before we can paint a more positive picture of our europe.
i have not abstained on the resolution on air safety and body scanners because i object to the reservation made in it on the right of privacy of passengers, on the contrary. i also believe that body scanners cannot be put into service without a clear scientific and medical assessment of the possible health effects of the use of the technology in question on the users. i can only regret that the proposal to postpone the vote for a moment and to invite commissioner tajani to propose a study on the subject which might enable us to make a more informed assessment of the use of body scanners was voted down. this is a very serious matter . the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to meet the challenges of the future. i therefore regret that we are treating this matter in this parliament in a careless way.

in writing. − the fight against obesity should start at an early age. the supply of fresh fruit at school can be a great incentive to eat healthily. so this programme, which provides at least one piece of fruit a day for every child between the ages of three and ten, is a very good thing. an estimated 22 million children in the european union are overweight, of whom 5.1 million are obese. this is not only causing a lot of health problems, but also high health care costs for the member states. if the commission follows parliament's proposal to increase the budget from eur 90 million to eur 500 million, every child can learn good eating habits from an early age. this gives them more chances to maintain these habits later on and thus avoid becoming overweight. the report also deals with the composition of the fruit supply. local and seasonal fruit should be given priority by the member states. the report also calls for the children to receive health and nutritional advice, along with information on the characteristics of organic farming. i am satisfied with the content of the report and have therefore voted in favour.
on behalf of the pse group. mr president, mr president-in-office, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by setting the stage in the middle of these delicate debates, in a very comfortable atmosphere, as i have been there three times in recent months. and there, as we speak, are violence, rape and death. this is, for the moment, the daily life of the many citizens who live in this horrible part of the world. i believe that the time is no longer for rhetoric; the time is now for action. this dirty war, in fact, is not at all, in my opinion, an ethnic war, as some are beginning to say; we can always create ethnic wars, that's fine with us europeans, because when we talk about ethnic wars, it means that africans fight each other and that's not our problem. no, the war that is taking place there has to be founded on the mines - the diamond mines, the coltan mines, the gold mines - which, despite the massacres, continue to be gently and efficiently exploited, whose minerals are exported, to the nose and beard of the un forces, from the airport in goma or the road to kigali, and from which some continue to enrich themselves very much. this is war. if we want to stop this war, we must first look at this issue, at the closure of these mines, at the measures to be taken to prevent this scandalous trade. we can go back to processes, like the kimberley process, which was issued for the diamond, and i suppose some colleagues will speak about that here. basically, in the last ten years, this is a figure that some people dispute, but i assure you that it is easily verifiable, more than 5 million congolese died in this region in a violent way, whether victims of the war, immediately, or victims of collateral damage, such as diseases, etc., that it brought about around it. the question now is who is making this war work and who has an interest in its continuation. i think that is an important first point. i believe that there are three important things about the european union, and my group also believes this. first of all, we must support the peace efforts that have been under way for some time, not just in recent weeks. we have proposed father malu malu for the sakharov prize, because this gentleman has been committed for a long time to trying to find peaceful solutions in this region. he brought together in goma not only the cndp - because the cndp came, went, returned, returned - but all the rebel movements and the government of the drc. i think it was important. there is the nairobi process and the recent nairobi summit, to which the commissioner was referring. i think that we must of course support these efforts and support our commissioner, louis michel, who, i say, as a socialist, is a liberal, but who is making very significant efforts in this area. secondly, we must defend the population at all costs. we are told that monuc is not there to make war, that it is there to ensure peace. yes, perhaps, but monuc has a "chapter 7" mandate. it is not like in 1994 in kigali, where we could do nothing, where we had to watch, helpless, the massacres. here, there is a chapter 7, and the mission of monuc is to defend the civilian population. well, that civilian population over there is being slaughtered and monuc is not moving. there was a massacre not long ago in a village whose name i have not yet been able to remember, in kiwanja, at the foot of a monuc camp. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. in any case, we will table an amendment to that effect. finally, i believe, and i will say this very quickly in conclusion, that we must of course take the necessary decisions to stop the exploitation and trade of minerals, which are essentially responsible for all the massacres that are taking place there.

madam president, i am pleased that the council and the commission are making a statement on the economic and social impact of current demographic trends. many politicians have the unhealthy tendency to think short-term and neglect longer-term policy. our demographic challenge is a vital long-term problem which also requires long-term solutions. the average birth rate among women in the european union is 1.5. that's too low to replace the current generations. herein lies the crux of the problem. the short-term solution to the problem of immigration is to advocate an even greater wave of immigration from outside europe. on paper this may seem a solution, but the daily reality in our big cities proves the total failure of the lax immigration policy of the last thirty years. we have 20 million unemployed in europe and yet the commission wants to bring in more immigrants. i would also point out that the unemployment rate among non-european immigrants is significantly higher than among the native population in the member states. i shall not, for lack of time, go into the social problems such as the social disruption caused by mass immigration. what is needed is a policy in the member states which encourages young european families to have children. fiscal measures are needed in the member states to make having children more attractive. more and better childcare facilities must be provided. we must also dare to think about the introduction of a parental allowance for one of the parents who chooses to devote himself first and foremost to the education of the children.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, the commission welcomes the court’s annual report. i would like to underline the very constructive cooperation we have enjoyed with the court. there is one piece of really good news in the report: the court has given the accounts a completely clean bill of health, what the auditors call an ‘unqualified opinion’. this is a remarkable achievement in only the third year of the new accounting system. there is a second piece of very good news: the court acknowledges that we are strengthening our supervisory systems. in 2007, for the first time, there is not a single chapter with a red card on control systems from the external auditor. many efforts are ongoing in this field. i would like to highlight the annual summaries of existing audits in structural funds submitted by member states for the first time last spring. the court recognises these efforts, even if results are not yet translated into significantly reduced error rates on the ground. the commission is encouraged by this. it is a fact that on individual transactions the picture remains mixed. for the european agricultural guarantee fund, the largest volume of agricultural expenditure, the court recognises that this year again the error rate is below materiality level. the same is not true for the rest of the chapter on natural resources, where rural development is prone to a high level of errors. for cohesion funds too, the court still finds far too many errors. the commission is giving the highest priority to reducing these error rates, and it is not shying away from taking a tough stance if needed. in 2008 we have already imposed financial corrections on the erdf and esf – the cohesion funds – for eur 843 million and an estimated eur 1.5 billion more is in the pipeline. allow me to recall that, as far as errors in the underlying payments are concerned, the bar is set very high. at least 98% must be error-free. nevertheless, we are getting closer: the auditors now say that for all budget areas but one as much as 95% or more of the payments are free from serious financial error. for external aid and internal policies, such as transport and energy, as well as education and citizenship, the court does note improvements. administration and economic and financial affairs fare even better. these areas are in direct management by the commission, which may partly explain why efforts undertaken have a more immediate impact. so, to summarise: in the commission’s view the court of auditors’ annual report for 2007 shows that there is steady, gradual progress. we have come a long way over the last five years. looking at the progress made, i can assert that the commission has absolutely no regrets about having set itself the objective of achieving a positive declaration of assurance concerning the underlying transactions. we hope that the european parliament will recognise the positive developments and continue to support efforts for simplification, better management, and more accountability from the member states.
i would like to thank the court of auditors, and especially its staff. they've done another great job. commissioner, i would remind you that you did indeed promise at the beginning of your mandate as an anti-fraud commissioner and you promised that at the end of your mandate you would ensure that there was a statement that the reliability of the accounts and the regularity and legality of all the accounts were in order. are we there yet? obviously not. for the 14th time in a row, that statement is not there. are we on the right track? without a doubt. that is what the court of auditors says and what you defend with all your might. should we be worried? i think i did. but you have exactly one year to fulfil your promise, your commitment, and there is - as my colleagues have said - work to be done. what does the court of auditors find? there are shortcomings in accounting systems, partly due to the complex legal and financial framework. the court of auditors still says that there is a risk to the quality and financial information. what does the court of auditors say about the regularity and legality of the disbursements? okay, what about the administrative expenses? major problems in large parts of the budget. agriculture, the cohesion fund, the structural funds, the regional fund, social policy, rural development, research and development, energy, transport, external aid, development and enlargement, education and citizenship. the cohesion fund itself and the structural funds are a representative example of all that is involved in cohesion. we note that the court of auditors says in its report that in 11% of cases payments should never have been made. i think that is a very strong statement and we will have to pay very close attention to it in the report. 2007 was the first year - several colleagues have pointed this out - in which member states were required to draw up an annual overview of the available checks and declarations. the court of auditors says this isn't working. the statistics are not comparable and do not contain all the information we need. however, mr mulder rightly said that there was a commitment, a political agreement. we have worked hard for that in this parliament. and what do we see? a whole series of member states are not really willing to cooperate. it is the eurosceptic countries that are doing the best: the united kingdom, denmark and the netherlands also have a large share. this is not possible. we must indeed hold the council to account. finally, on behalf of the greens, i would also like to stress the political requirement for the member states, namely that they should indeed take political responsibility for the expenditure they are co-managing. that is a goddamn duty! we also want more transparency about the final destination, about the final beneficiaries. there is a fine website, but i see that some member states, including my own country, belgium, put information on it that is totally inadequate and totally non-transparent. so there is still a way to go and we will have to fight for it during the discharge.

madam president, it is very important that the european union has an efficient and well-established anti-fraud agency. this is all the more so as budgets increase and foreign aid increases, without it being possible to always check that the resources used are being properly used. the public, the taxpayer, often wonders about that, and rightly so, i think. this report contains a number of good proposals and i will therefore approve it, although i think that further consideration should be given to the independence of olaf. olaf is a directorate-general of the european commission. the political responsibility lies with the vice-president of the commission. the agency is independent in terms of its operations and research, but this hybrid status is potentially problematic, to say the least. i am convinced that an independent statute can only increase the agency's effectiveness.
madam president, i voted against the klamt report for the simple reason that the whole concept of economic immigration and the so-called blue card is short-term thinking. we can better implement a policy of training, retraining and activation in the member states for the approximately 20 million unemployed who are now in the european union. we'd better learn from the mistakes of the past. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the people are being persuaded that this is only a matter of highly educated and temporary immigrants, but who am i to doubt the words of louis michel, who says that other immigrants should also be welcome. in other words: the floodgates will remain open. you just create another one. this is about a coalition against society. big business wants cheap labor and works with the multicultural left. the costs are passed on to society.
in writing. the blue card seemed to be a good start for a streamlined migration policy in the european union. i think that a european migration policy is necessary, especially since the european workforce will be reduced by 20 million people by 2050. the commission's proposal, which was already weak, has been greatly weakened by the european parliament. the commission proposal left some room for migration of people without a higher education degree but with strong skills. but parliament has put a stop to this by making the conditions for migration much stricter. the income threshold is set by the european parliament at 1.7 times the average wage in the member state. that's way too high. if we want to compete with the united states and canada, the countries that are now attracting the most highly educated people, we have to make it easier for people to come and work here. the european parliament's demand that migrants have to have five years of work experience, including two years as a 'senior employee', is also unacceptable. it is incomprehensible that this proposal has not been extended to include a migration procedure for all those who can find work here. legal immigration will be possible with the blue card, but as this is certainly not for everyone, i abstained from the vote.
author. - mr president, there are two aspects to the painful problem of the expulsion of the al-kurd family: one political, the other humanitarian. politics is the status of east jerusalem, which is claimed as an integral part of israel by the israelis. it should be remembered that neither the europeans nor the international community have ever heard of it as such. in its resolution 252, the un security council was very clear and stated that all the legislative and administrative measures and measures taken by israel, including the expropriation of land and real estate, which tend to change the legal status of jerusalem, are invalid and cannot change this status. the security council reminded israel of this when it adopted measures in 1980 to make jerusalem its unified capital, and resolution 476 called for an immediate end to the implementation of policies and measures affecting the character and status of the holy city. resolution 478 affirms that all measures taken to change the status of the city are null and void. the un and europe have never reconsidered this point. that is why, despite all the respect that everyone in this house has for the independence of the israeli judiciary, we know that it can only be based on the laws of its own country, which are in contradiction here with international law, and that, moreover, international law does not give it jurisdiction over east jerusalem. the expulsion of the al-kurd family must therefore be seen in this political perspective and cannot be reduced to a dispute over property. the al-kurd family is expelled in favor of a jewish family that has recently immigrated to israel. it is being stripped of its right to property after fighting for it for 40 years now, and some of our parliamentarians who have met it can, better than i can, describe the human tragedy that this expulsion represents. all gone, ashamed, i insist, and i rejoice, we ask that justice be done to him and his property returned to him. - i'm the reporter. - the president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, tax fraud, vat fraud, these are crimes. it's crime, white collar crime, but it's crime. crime that sometimes has ties to organized crime. what sums are we talking about, because that's always interesting. commissioner kovács estimated total tax fraud in 2007 at between 2 and 2.5 times the european budget. that is between 200 and 250 billion euros. the share of vat fraud in this is estimated at 40 billion. and all of this is probably a gross underestimation, because the court of auditors estimated the loss of vat revenue in germany in 2005 at eur 17 billion and in the united kingdom at eur 18.2 billion, together slightly more than eur 35 billion in vat revenue missed. it is therefore good that the commission has funded a study, that it is being carried out, that the results will be published, so that we can get a good idea of the true extent of the problem. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. firstly, we must improve cooperation between national tax authorities and secondly, we must really reduce the time taken for the collection and exchange of information by the administrative authorities of the member states, so that the ball can be played really faster. my report analyses in particular the court of auditors' investigation into vat fraud. the court of auditors examined the situation in seven member states: in the united kingdom, france, italy, luxembourg, the netherlands, poland and slovenia, the number of employees is increasing. those countries cooperated well. but germany refused to cooperate. in the meantime, the commission opened infringement proceedings against germany in september. i have to say, commissioner, i support requests for information are not answered within a period of three months. that's actually vindictive, because actually, this kind of crime-fighting should happen in real time. but the internal organization of countries is also being criticised. the netherlands and germany in particular are lacking in this respect. on top of that, there is a total lack of sufficiently strong control mechanisms. it is a mystery to me, ladies and gentlemen, why member states, although they know they are missing billions in revenue, do not take strong action against this. this is really in writing. i am pleased that mrs grabowska's report is being put to the vote today, firstly because we have been waiting for the revised version of the regulation in question for a long time, and secondly because this vote should allow the text to be adopted while france is still in office, a presidency which has spared no effort to bring it to fruition. as you know, at present in the union, when there is a divorce and there are children, if a spouse has left for another country, it is often difficult and time-consuming to actually get alimony paid. the proposed text, which i support, should make life much easier for european citizens in matters of maintenance obligations and help creditors to recover what they are owed: by abolishing the exequatur, it makes any decision of a court of a member state on an obligation to provide maintenance for an absent spouse immediately applicable in all member states. it will also enable the citizens concerned, from their place of usual residence, to complete the formalities for obtaining salary and bank account attachments, to trigger cooperation mechanisms, to have access to information enabling the debtor to be located and to assess his assets.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in the multiannual programme on consumer protection, in the resolution and in the debate on the withdrawal of unsafe toys, mainly chinese, we have made a very strong call for the protection of the smallest and most vulnerable consumers, namely children, to be placed high on the agenda. as rapporteur for the new toy safety act, i am therefore very pleased to announce today that we may be able to bring a new and strict directive on toy safety into force in the first place, at least if everything goes as planned here. i thank the commission for having responded to our call to propose a new directive. i also thank the opinion rapporteurs, the shadow rapporteurs, the president and the colleagues of imco for the excellent cooperation which made it possible to adopt my report unanimously on 6 november. and i thank the council, the commission and all our staff for their efficient work and their constructive attitude, which enabled us to complete this important piece of consumer legislation within ten months. colleagues, we have several reasons to be proud of our work. the safety standards for toys are being improved and tightened, and that is what people expect from us. the use of carcinogens, mutagens and reproductive toxicants in authorized parts of toys is prohibited in principle; the commission has also proposed that the rules on unavoidable traces of heavy metals be tightened, not only by introducing maximum levels for more types of substances, but also by formulating much stricter limits for unavoidable traces of lead, cadmium, mercury, chromium-6 and organic tin. the regulation on allergenic fragrances, which we do not want to expose children to, is also completely new. we are also making this approach stricter than the commission proposal; the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council adopt a common position on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. for educational games on smell and taste, we are a little more flexible, provided labelling and consistency with other relevant legislation. it is also important, ladies and gentlemen, to improve the rules against the risk of choking, to clarify the essential safety requirements and, as a completely new rule, to introduce rules on toys in sweets. the system of warnings is also being extended and strengthened and must not only be clearly visible in a language that consumers can understand, but also be visible at the point of sale. finally, we give the precautionary principle its proper place in the law. but of course, all those rules are only valid if they are enforced. we are doing this by integrating the new approach into the new package of goods, by tightening the requirements for dossiers and by introducing traceability requirements. vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us in the last few years. i would like to address some of the problems that have been raised in this debate. first, the issue of chemicals. the rules we have here cannot be stricter than they have been. a total ban on chemicals is not possible because they occur in nature as trace elements. mrs. breyer, i can't stop you from ignoring the laws of nature. but what you did was irresponsible panicking. i must make this very clear. they give the impression that the european legislator is releasing toxic toys on children. the opposite is true! i strongly object to the implication in your article. we've done something here that's never been done before. the rule, even in this parliament, is that a substance is banned only when it has been shown to be harmful. it's the opposite here. the substances are prohibited and will only be authorized if their safety is proven beyond doubt. i wonder what more can be asked here. i can't do more than what's being done here. if anyone gives the impression that something is being done here which does not sufficiently protect children - i am sorry - he is deliberately misleading the european public. i really wonder why you're doing this. what has been said about noise - that toys should not cause hearing damage - is indeed true. that is why the directive contains rules on this. the limit values, i.e. the decibel values, are set as is customary in european legislation. the toy directive is nothing special. technical rules are established in the course of standardization and standardisation. it's the same here. the decibel values are thus established in the course of standardization. the directive provides the legal basis for this to happen. on the subject of books: this has been a topic in the last few days, and it has surprised me very much. the word "books" does not appear in the text. it changes nothing at all from the existing state of affairs! it seems that some german manufacturer has started a press campaign here and has also been lobbying the european parliament vigorously. not a word of it is true! it does not change the status quo. but the commission is willing, because it is right, as parliament has requested, to ensure that the standards which apply here are improved and modernised. the commission will award contracts. i would like to say this to the tüv group in this house on third party certification: the idea that a toy or any product in europe is safer because it is certified by a third party is unfortunately not justified. the commission has indeed had the results for many years in terms of product safety. there is nothing, nothing, to suggest that third-party certification makes any product safer. we then prescribe it - and this is also a principle of european legislation which this house has been observing for many years - when it is a highly complex product. dear mrs. gebhardt, i'll have to say this: there's a difference in complexity between a high-tech product like a modern car and a teddy bear. i think it's a little bit weird to compare these things. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would urge you not to think that all you have to do is ask a third party to certify it and you have a safe toy. the dangers that actually occur cannot be tackled by certification at all. look at the cases we've been talking about. the difficulties were not in the prototype of the product presented to the third party, but in the supply chain. there were unreliabilities in the manufacturers. no one but the manufacturer of the product is able to guarantee the complete reliability and safety of its supply chain! i urge you not to depart from this principle and not to relieve the manufacturers of products of their full responsibility for the safety of their products! it is not that manufacturers simply have to say 'my product is safe' or 'my toy is safe', they have to be able to document this fully and without gaps at any time when market surveillance so requires. that will and must be checked. the same applies to the importer. these are rules which i believe cannot be made any stricter, because they already ensure maximum effectiveness. but i agree with all those who say that it is very important that the member states take this control seriously and increase the possibilities of market surveillance. i think that the commission's proposal is a good one. i really wonder what the point is in banning fragrances in toys which are allowed in cosmetics intended for children where the product is applied directly to the child's skin. the perfume is allowed, and the toy, so that it does not smell so terrible, it should be forbidden. it doesn't make sense! nevertheless, in this directive we have gone even further than the cosmetics directive. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's environmental policy is not distorted. so i don't see what else we can do here. let me conclude with this: the commission has already announced amendments, and of course the european parliament is entirely free to adopt them. i must point out, however, that we are dealing here with an overall compromise with the council, parliament and the commission, and that the amendment on third party certification announced by mrs gebhardt, for example, will lead to the compromise not being achieved. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. you can't have everything. it is - i repeat - an overall compromise. this overall compromise is balanced, it provides the toy safety that is possible and that we need. i urge you to accept this overall compromise. declaration of the european commission on monitoring of safety aspects (article 47) following the entry into force of the revised toys safety directive, the commission will closely monitor all developments relating to its implementation in order to assess whether it provides for an adequate level of toy safety, notably as regards the application of the conformity assessment procedures as set out in chapter iv. the revised toys safety directive provides for a reporting obligation of member states on the situation concerning the safety of toys, the effectiveness of the directive and market surveillance carried out by member states. the commission's evaluation will be based inter alia on the member states' reports to be submitted three years after the date of application of the directive with a particular focus on market surveillance in the european union and its external borders. the commission will report back to the european parliament at the latest one year after submission of member states' reports. declaration of the european commission on the requirements concerning toys which are designed to emit a sound (annex ii.i.10) based on the new essential safety requirement for toys which are designed to emit a sound under the toys safety directive, the commission will mandate cen to establish a revised standard which limits the peak values for both impulse noise and prolonged noise emitted by toys in order to adequately protect children from the risk of hearing impairment. declaration of the european commission on the classification of books (annex i. 17) taking into account the difficulties related to the relevant tests required in the harmonised toy standards en 71:1 for books made of cardboard and paper, the commission will mandate cen to establish a revised standard which covers adequate testing for children's books.

president, the target of 20% reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2020 has been met. but let's face it, that goal was actually understated. science tells us we have to go to -25, -40%. i regret that the auction system has been eroded in the non-electricity sector. in 2020, we'll still be giving out 30% free allowances, and the 100% auction won't happen until 2027. this is a really bad signal to those who have to negotiate the global climate agreement in copenhagen, and that is when the best answer to carbon leakage is actually a global agreement. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the failure of the member states to implement the directive. less revenue from auctioning rights means less investment in renewable energy, less investment in research and development. i would ask the rapporteur: madam rapporteur, mrs doyle, do you have any idea what the expected lower yield from the auction will be, now that this system has been totally eroded?
in writing. i was at a clean mobility conference at a university in limburg this morning. the conference was focused on electric cars. it is clear that the time has come to switch to this environmentally friendly technology. the age of fossil fuels is coming to an end. the current crisis in the automotive sector also offers opportunities to fully embark on this new path. in exchange for the government support and guarantees, manufacturers will have to invest much more heavily in this technology of the future, in affordable batteries with a wider range of action, in other words. and the government must now support that revolution much more resolutely, including fiscal support. since 2005, the european commission has been proposing to reform the car tax in a coordinated way in a european context. that proposal must be put back on the table of the ministers. the car tax should be reoriented on the basis of emission criteria. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the member states are not able to meet the requirements of the directive. it's time for a radical change. the engineers have finished their work. the government must now push the car manufacturers further and quickly in the right direction.
i have long held the view, mr president, that europe should not be a mere piece of cake, and i think that we in this parliament must first learn to accept that not everything has to be regulated at european level and that there are many local and national rules and practices which we should respect, including those relating to the protection of workers and to the rules on health and safety at work and working time. what is important to me is that it should be the member states who decide on it, and that in my view all aspects of employment law should remain the exclusive competence of the member states; the commission and the european court of justice are best able to avoid this. that is what subsidiarity is all about and we all say we are in favour of it. in this respect, i am absolutely opposed to the abolition of the so-called opt-out possibilities, and i believe that it is up to the member states themselves and even the länder themselves to decide whether or not to work on sundays.
in writing. − i agreed to the report on the climate package because it includes different directives, all of which are in line with the eu's target of reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 20% by 2020 and even by 30% if an international agreement is reached. it is the result of long negotiations and a joint compromise between parliament and the council, representing the 27 member states. one of the directives concerns renewable energy. it includes the objective of increasing the share of renewable energy sources to 20% and increasing energy efficiency by 20%. the aim is to achieve 10% of fuel consumption from renewable energy sources. sustainability criteria have been defined to facilitate their application. i welcome this regulation because it not only reduces europe's energy dependence and creates new jobs but also encourages innovation in technological development. the directive, which relates to the european emissions trading scheme (ets), modernises the existing emissions trading scheme and requires that emissions allowances previously free of charge are now auctioned by industry. exceptions in the form of transitional periods are made for eastern european countries, which initially have to purchase allowances for only 30% of their emissions. the commission has also proposed incentives for energy efficiency, which are not tied to a specific purpose but require member states to invest at least half of their revenues in developing countries and new technologies. i am in favour of a successful balance between taking account of the industries facing difficult problems and an ambitious environmental policy. another directive regulates the fair burden sharing of emissions not covered by the ets. this includes heating and air conditioning and many economic sectors (transport, small industrial plants, services and agriculture) which, although not covered by the ets, are nonetheless significant contributors to greenhouse gas emissions. the commission will also be able to use the existing instruments to support the implementation of the new rules. the carbon capture and storage (ccs) directive makes it possible to separate co2 from the exhaust gas and then store it underground. the aim is to finance 12 ccs power stations by 2015. i do recognise that co2 capture and storage is an essential transitional technology, but that particular attention should be paid to the safety of storage. in addition, regulations have been introduced within another directive which provides for limit values for co2 emissions from new cars. from 2015, the average co2 emissions of each new car will be reduced to 120 grams per kilometre and from 2020 to 95 grams per kilometre. the penalties for non-compliance with the limit values have been reduced compared to the commission proposal due to the economic crisis and amount to between eur 5 and eur 95, depending on the quantity exceeded. however, from 2019 onwards, the fines should be eur 95 for the first gram of co2 exceeded. i am in favour of the compromise reached between the european institutions, because criticism is often an easy thing to do, but finding compromises is a challenge. the arrangements agreed are the successful outcome of negotiations between countries which, although economically very different, nevertheless share a common objective. the fact that the new member states in particular are unable to achieve all the objectives in the short term without destroying entire sectors of the economy and facing a social catastrophe cannot be ignored when looking at the european objective as a whole. i see the climate package not only as an important step, but as a leap forward that will counter the progress of climate change and strengthen europe's leadership on the path to an efficient energy policy. europe has succeeded in speaking with one voice, and this will make it possible to make stronger demands at international level as well. the major challenge is to avoid environmental dumping at international level. for this reason, countries that do not comply with the kyoto protocol and are therefore not bound by the co2 standards should be subject to an import tax or similar measures to counter environmental dumping. this is an aspect that should be taken into account in the successor agreement to the kyoto protocol within the un climate change conference, which will be negotiated in december 2009 by the us, china and india, among others. the climate package has provided a solid basis for the conclusion of a new international agreement.
in writing. - i voted with great conviction in favour of the compromise reached on renewable energy sources. my green colleague and rapporteur, claude turmes, has done a splendid job. his work and that of the whole parliament is ensuring that a strong legislative framework is in place to ensure that the total share of renewable energy is at least 20% by 2020. this is not a hype. this is a real energy revolution and it's going to create a massive number of jobs. some reports speak of over 2 million jobs. jobs for highly skilled engineers, designers and technicians, but also a majority of jobs for technicians, people who make wheels, install solar panels, build wind farms. the negotiations also led to the adaptation of the original proposals on agro- or biofuels. we greens are not very happy with this kind of technology and we are putting strict conditions on the use of this kind of fuel. the turmes report clearly strengthens the sustainability criteria and also refers to social criteria within the framework of the international labour organisation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
in writing. - i voted against the compromise reached. this does not diminish my appreciation of the work done by mr satu hassi in this respect. but the agreement which can ensure that almost 80% of all efforts are made in third countries is intolerable to me. a quick calculation shows that this will enable belgium to achieve between 50 and 60 per cent of the necessary effort outside the european union. it covers important sectors such as buildings and transport. it is economically absurd to invest many millions of euros abroad through the clean development mechanism when you as a country have to make significant efforts to properly insulate your own buildings or design a transport policy aimed at more low-carbon mobility. moreover, there is no guarantee that the projects in which foreign investment is made are of high quality. worse still, there is no certainty that the investments made through the cdm are really complementary or additive. it is also unethical to buy the easiest efforts in third countries. this is a form of neo-colonialism and it complicates the position of those third countries which will have to make additional and more costly efforts in a subsequent phase.
in writing. - i voted against the compromise in the sacconi report on co2 emissions from cars. this is a disgraceful dossier in which the car manufacturers are once again evading the obligation to build cars which consume less energy and pollute less. this case has been dragging on for over 10 years. first, time was bought by concluding a voluntary agreement. it was not respected. today's agreement will buy time once again. the fines are ridiculously low. a standard of 95 grams of co2 emissions/km is envisaged by 1 january 2020. in 1996, industry agreed to a maximum emission of 140 grams in 2008. the reality is: we're now at an average emission of 162 grams. the truth is, short-term thinking always works. we would rather continue to pay large sums of money to corrupt or undemocratic regimes than invest in green innovation technology. the argument that the economic crisis is making us do this is a fallacy. the automobile industry has no future unless it chooses energy-efficient and non-polluting vehicles. if she is in trouble now, it is largely because of her own mistakes and shortsighted choices in the past.
in writing. - the working time system is at the heart of social europe. it balances the protection of workers against a flexible organization of work. the council of ministers is focusing on flexibility. it is unacceptable that in member states which opt out, employers negotiate longer working hours, even up to 65 hours a week. this derogation should expire three years after its entry into force. what is the use of collective agreements on health and safety at work if member states are to enforce them? an average working week of 48 hours, calculated over a year, provides more than enough room to cover peak times and still respect the necessary rest periods. by raising this average, you give employers a free pass to stop paying overtime. it is also absurd to exclude sleeping on duty from working time. whoever is on guard is on standby and that must be respected. just as rest periods must be respected. tired workers can endanger themselves and others. working should not be at the expense of a quality life! we have sent a strong signal to the council today. the conciliation procedure which is now under way must lead to a more social europe.
in writing. - i supported the report on the crossborder enforcement of road safety rules because these rules make it more effective to prosecute drivers who commit road traffic offences in a third country. the commission has found that road traffic offences are often not prosecuted when they are committed with a vehicle registered in a member state other than the one where the offence is committed. this is accompanied by the tendency of many motorists to drive more recklessly in third countries than in their country of origin, because they are less afraid of prosecution for the offences. the new directive is designed to counteract this trend. the electronic data exchange and data protection provisions of the directive ensure efficient cooperation between countries, which makes it possible to prosecute road traffic offences as if they had been committed in one's own country. the directive is limited to the prosecution of the four traffic offences which account for 75% of all serious and fatal road accidents. the commission proposes that the speed limit be increased to cover speeding, driving under the influence of alcohol, not wearing a seatbelt and running a red stop light. however, two years after the entry into force of the directive, a review by the commission is to take place, which may then lead to other offences being taken into account, if appropriate. i support the directive and consider it an important step not only to promote road safety but also to give europe the opportunity to grow even more as a large area of mobility.

considerably. from a totally embryonic stage in 1999 - i recall that the ambition of the tampere programme was first of all to lay the foundations for cooperation between the member states and to promote the mutual recognition of judicial decisions - the jls sector has undergone a new development on the occasion of the hague programme which has strengthened the community base. it is on this double basis and under the pressure of dramatic external events - i will not speak of the attacks which have caused us so much concern and caused so many deaths and misery - that many policies have made spectacular progress: the fight against terrorism and serious crime, the fight against illegal immigration, the fight against racism and xenophobia, the fight against drugs and drug addiction. and it is not a bad thing to say of all of us - council, commission, parliament - that over the last ten years our approach has been driven primarily - i do not say exclusively - by a defensive reflex dictated both by the dramatic events i have just mentioned and by the legitimate expectations of our people for security. in recent times, however, another approach has gradually emerged, one that is less defensive and driven by a desire for a more positive, more voluntary approach to the space we want to create together. the lisbon treaty, as you know, provided for the charter of fundamental rights to be made binding. it was therefore decided to transform the vienna observatory into the fundamental rights agency. the pact on immigration and asylum, while not neglecting defence and security requirements, has thus paved the way for a more open policy, focusing on active management of migration flows and progress in partnership. i would now like to come to the challenge we are going to have to face together and the new guidelines which should inspire the stockholm programme, which i fervently hope will be able to be implemented on the basis of the lisbon treaty which will finally be ratified. since i won't have time, i'll draw only one conclusion. it is not our fears or the fears of our fellow citizens that should be our guides. what must guide us are first of all our values, which must be backed up by objective analyses of the risks we have to face. i hope that we will draft the stockholm programme in this spirit and that we will all work together to achieve it.
