president. - mr president-in-office, i have listened to your reply with great attention. you say, "yes, we will look at these proposals with the greatest vigilance, avec la plus grande attention". i am afraid that answer is not sufficient for us. we did our homework. we have adopted mrs gräßle's report at first reading. we did that because there are loopholes in the current legislation. we have tried to remedy those shortcomings and mr bösch is quite right to say that there are a number of challenges. we have very recently approved the report on vat fraud. there is a tax fraud in the european union of between 200 and 250 billion euros. so we need good legislation. both you, mr president-in-office, and the council as a whole must do your job. i would ask you to be a little more specific about the timetables which the council wishes to adhere to in this matter. that is absolutely necessary.
i had the honour, mr president, of drawing up an opinion on frontex on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, but that opinion was rejected by a slim majority because the left did not want to accept that the problems with libya and turkey were explicitly mentioned in the text. i was therefore surprised and pleased to see that the libe committee had finally incorporated my concerns into the text. libya is a major transit country for illegal immigration to europe; turkey is also a supplier of immigrants. it is therefore essential that these countries and their border control authorities cooperate fully with frontex. this is also true of readmission agreements. turkey has refused to cooperate for years. turkey refuses to sign an agreement and, moreover, does not make any effort to ensure that its borders with europe are effectively guarded. we should expect something different from a country which wants to join the european union. it is appalling that the commission and the council have failed to point out turkey's responsibilities. frontex and eurosur are essential instruments in the fight against illegal immigration. they could also be used to combat crossborder crime, drug trafficking, human trafficking and arms trafficking. without an efficient management of the common external borders, schengen cannot work, and we should not even think of a common immigration policy. it is not just a question of resources, but also and above all of political will. and when i hear about fort europa, i am sad to say that there is no fort europa. that there is more of a seven europe at the moment.
president, as i am by nature very critical of the functioning and institutions of the european union, i have naturally not approved this report on the 2009 budget. first of all, i am not at all convinced that the european institutions are sparing and responsible in the use of the large amounts of taxpayers' money that they spend. secondly, i think that we are interfering in far too many policy areas and that the subsidies we give to the member states are always regarded as a kind of 'far from home' european money and are therefore poorly and inefficiently managed by the member states themselves. i also note that my parliamentary questions on the operating costs of the many subsidiary organisations and agencies of the european union are not or hardly answered. all this makes me suspicious and only confirms my vote against this budget.
i voted in favour of the resolution on olaf, mr president, because i fully agree with parliament's call for greater independence for olaf. it is indeed urgent that something be done about it. olaf is at present, after all, little more than a directorate-general of the commission, and the political responsibility for it lies with the vice-president of the commission. that's not a healthy thing to do. the commission has also been asked to provide a report on the implementation of the new regulation on the control of the use of the financial system by the member states. so, we're in agreement. more generally, i think that the european institutions very often manage the large sums of taxpayers' money in a sloppy way. at the very least, olaf must have the resources, the manpower and the responsibility to put an end to the manifestly criminal aspects of this situation. i am afraid we will have to put an end to the more legalistic use of taxpayers' money.
i voted in favour of this surprisingly good report on frontex because i can only applaud the strengthening of this institution that it calls for. i believe that the fight against illegal immigration must be a top priority for the union, and in this context the agreements concluded by frontex with the authorities of third countries are very important. it is good that this report calls a cat a cat and that it criticises in particular the unacceptable attitude of the applicant country, turkey. i think it should be made very clear that the active refusal of the authorities of a third country, turkey - a fortiori a candidate country - to cooperate in frontex must have direct consequences for the political and economic relations between the union and that country, namely the suspension of the accession negotiations with the non-european country turkey.
i voted for the moreno sánchez report with some reservations. i have to say that i did not have very high expectations of the report, given the political correctness which normally prevails in the libe committee. but i must say that this is a balanced report, which also addresses a few pain points, such as the lack of cooperation - or rather, the opposition - of third countries such as libya and turkey. it is a shame that a candidate country should fail to meet its obligations in such a flagrant manner, especially in the case of turkey. frontex - and here the report is actually lacking - must be developed into an effective instrument in the fight against illegal immigration, but also in the fight against international crime, drug trafficking and arms trafficking.
i voted in favour of the susta report, mr president, because of course counterfeiting is a serious problem and the text presented is a matter of common sense. i also agree with paragraph 30 of the report, which states that 'turkey will only be a credible candidate for accession if it is able to take over the acquis communautaire and ensure full respect for intellectual property rights'. the only conclusion to be drawn from this is that turkey is not a full-fledged candidate for eu membership, and i take note of that.
in writing. toys should be safer than other products, because children are very vulnerable consumers. however, dangerous toys still exist in the union. it is therefore welcome that parliament and the council have reached a compromise on a text which sets out a whole series of safety criteria for the industry to meet before a toy can be placed on the european market. as with many compromises, this text has had its share of successes and disappointments. as regards the advances, i would mention in particular the requirement for manufacturers to guarantee that their toys do not have any harmful effect on health or safety, the strengthening of the limit values for toxic metals, better prevention of suffocation and strangulation by small detachable parts, and the clarification of the instructions on the packaging or on the toys themselves. these advances justify my vote in favour of the final text. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens, mutagens and toxins. i voted for this provision, but it was not adopted, and i regret that.
in writing. − with this new safe toys law we are taking a step in the right direction, but we are leaving some opportunities open. that is why i did not support the report. the use of certain allergenic fragrances and certain chemicals, which are carcinogenic, mutagenic or harmful to, among other things, reproduction, is restricted. the use of these substances is not, however, completely prohibited, but only gradually phased out. there are also no binding standards for noise-producing toys. the positive aspect is that importers of toys are legally treated in the same way as producers. the directive provides for a half-hearted control of safety standards for toys. because manufacturers have to guarantee safety themselves. the directive does require member states to carry out sampling, but there are fears that this is too unconstrained. the safety monitoring is arbitrary and to date there is no real european quality label that allows parents to make well-documented decisions and thus avoid toys that may be harmful to the health of their children. mandatory certification by independent bodies could solve this problem. both the us and china take product safety very seriously and recently passed legislation mandating these checks. why is europe being left behind in this?
